## Skills
A skill is a set of local instructions to follow that is stored in a `SKILL.md` file. Below is the list of skills that can be used. Each entry includes a name, description, and a short path that can be expanded into an absolute path using the skill roots table.
### Skill roots
- `r0` = `/home/codex-b/.codex/skills`
- `r1` = `/home/codex-b/.codex/skills/.system`
- `r2` = `/etc/codex/skills`
- `r3` = `/home/codex-b/.codex/plugins/cache/openai-bundled/sites/0.1.57/skills`
- `r4` = `/home/codex-b/.codex/plugins/cache/openai-bundled`
- `r5` = `/home/codex-b/.codex/plugins/cache/openai-curated-remote`
### Available skills
- imagegen: Generate or edit raster images when the task benefits from AI-created bitmap visuals such as photos, illustrations, textures, sprites, mockups, or transparent-background cutouts. Use when Codex should create a brand-new image, transform an existing image, or derive visual variants from references, and the output should be a bitmap asset rather than repo-native code or vector. Do not use when the task is better handled by editing existing SVG/vector/code-native assets, extending an established icon or logo system, or building the visual directly in HTML/CSS/canvas. (file: r1/imagegen/SKILL.md)
- openai-docs: Use for Codex models/pricing, scheduled tasks, skills, settings, setup, troubleshooting, customization, automations, and self-knowledge—including 'you,' 'your,' 'this app,' or 'this coding agent' when they refer to Codex—and for OpenAI APIs/products and ChatGPT Work. Also use for model choice/migration, prompting, SDKs, Responses, Realtime, agents, evals, and Chat/Work/Codex comparisons. Do not use for generic app/software tasks that merely mention Codex. (file: r1/openai-docs/SKILL.md)
- plugin-creator: Create and scaffold plugin directories for Codex with a required `.codex-plugin/plugin.json`, optional plugin folders/files, valid manifest defaults, and personal-marketplace entries by default. Use when Codex needs to create a new personal plugin, add optional plugin structure, generate or update marketplace entries for plugin ordering and availability metadata, or update an existing local plugin during development with the CLI-driven cachebuster and reinstall flow. (file: r1/plugin-creator/SKILL.md)
- skill-creator: Create or update a Codex skill with appropriately scoped instructions and any needed supporting resources. (file: r1/skill-creator/SKILL.md)
- skill-installer: Install Codex skills into $CODEX_HOME/skills from a curated list or a GitHub repo path. Use when a user asks to list installable skills, install a curated skill, or install a skill from another repo (including private repos). (file: r1/skill-installer/SKILL.md)
- handoff-codex-project-tasks: Coordinate supported Codex app task handoffs among saved Git projects, direct checkouts, managed worktrees, and local or remote hosts. Use when a user asks to move, hand off, migrate, regroup, batch-transfer, diagnose, recover, or verify existing Codex tasks, threads, or chats across machines or project sidebar locations, including Recents-only and partial-failure cases. Do not use for Git-repository migration by itself, ordinary file transfer, remote-host setup alone, new-task creation, or ChatGPT cloud conversations. (file: r2/handoff-codex-project-tasks/SKILL.md)
- investigate-conjectures: Develop, refine, and adversarially test open mathematical, computational, or mechanistic conjectures through bounded theory–experiment and multi-route proof-search programs. Use when Codex must formulate a non-vacuous research conjecture, recover or update a long-running research program, orchestrate an explicitly authorized search across independent proof approaches, separate proof obligations and claim levels, audit loopholes or competing explanations, design a mechanism-preserving discriminating experiment, interpret new evidence, or synthesize a supersession-aware research state. Do not use for teaching a technical source, solving an isolated mathematical problem, reviewing an external paper, or summarizing material that needs no research-state reconciliation. (file: r2/investigate-conjectures/SKILL.md)
- solve-math-rigorously: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering. (file: r2/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md)
- teach-technical-math: Teach the mathematical content of technical documents—papers, reports, notes, appendices, and linked revisions—through rigorous, self-contained, one-concept-at-a-time lessons. Use when the user supplies or references one or more sources and wants to understand their definitions, equations, derivations, proofs, models, limiting constructions, evidence, assumptions, contradictions across versions, or open gaps over multiple turns; also use when they say “next block” or ask to rebuild a confusing part of such a lesson. Reconcile source versions before teaching, preserve stable typed notation, derive rather than paraphrase, and distinguish exact results from formal, heuristic, empirical, conjectural, and open claims. (file: r2/teach-technical-math/SKILL.md)
- deep-research-work:deep-research: Conduct thorough research using authoritative sources and produce evidence-backed findings with citations. Use this skill only when the user asks specifically for Deep research, uses $deep-research, or selects Deep research in Work mode. Do not use it for ordinary research requests. (file: r5/deep-research-work/0.1.14/skills/deep-research/SKILL.md)
- plugin-management:plugin-management: Discover and suggest relevant plugins, inspect app permissions and dependencies, and manage plugin connections or removal. Use when the user asks about plugins or when a task would materially benefit from an external app, account, service, or data source that available tools cannot access. (file: r5/plugin-management/0.1.0/skills/plugin-management/SKILL.md)
- review-ai-paper: Conduct autonomous, evidence-backed peer reviews of AI and machine-learning research papers and their supplementary code. Use when the user asks to review, referee, audit, red-team, or score a paper or submission; verify its claims, proofs, citations, baselines, experiments, or code; reproduce results; or fill a conference review form. Resolve the review format from a user-supplied rubric or current official venue instructions, otherwise ask one concise clarification or use a venue-neutral unscored format. Inventory and read the full submission, enforce confidentiality and AI-use policies, retrieve critical sources, audit code safely, build a claim ledger, calibrate flaw severity, and produce evidence_log.md, code_audit.md when code exists, claim_ledger.md, and review.md. Do not use for an incremental teaching lesson without review intent. (file: r0/review-ai-paper/SKILL.md)
- sites:sites-building: Use Sites to build websites, including landing pages, portfolios, dashboards, portals, trackers, hubs, and internal tools. Always use Sites when the project contains `.openai/hosting.json`. (file: r3/sites-building/SKILL.md)
- sites:sites-hosting: Host websites with Sites. Always use after `sites-building`, and use for website publishing, deployment, hosting management, or projects containing `.openai/hosting.json`. (file: r3/sites-hosting/SKILL.md)
- visualize:visualize: Create visualizations and interactive tools directly in conversation. Proactively use to show how something works; explore 'what happens when', 'what changes', or 'help me understand'; compare or inspect; create simulations, maps, charts, graphs, and mockups. Use standard tools for static scientific figures. (file: r4/visualize/1.0.29/skills/visualize/SKILL.md)

`approvals_reviewer` is `auto_review`: Sandbox escalations with require_escalated will be reviewed for compliance with the policy.
If a rejection happens, you can continue with a safer alternative, or carry out checks to prove that the action is authorized or low risk before trying again. Complete unaffected work without asking for confirmation. Report anything that remains blocked, clarify why it was blocked by auto-review, inform the user of the risk and ask for approval.

For empirical laws, matching coordinates gives the bound\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\!\\\\left(\\\\frac1n\\\\sum_i\\\\delta_{v_i},\\\\frac1n\\\\sum_i\\\\delta_{\\\\widetilde v_i}\\\\right)^2\\n\\\\le\\\\frac1n\\\\sum_i\\\\|v_i-\\\\widetilde v_i\\\\|_2^2.\\n\\\\tag{10}\\n\\\\]\\nConvergence in this distance means weak convergence together with convergence of second moments. Thus it includes all dot products of a fixed joint tuple.\\n\\nFirst, the Gaussian matrix has a uniform operator bound with exponentially high probability. A \\\\(1/4\\\\)-net of the Euclidean unit sphere has at most \\\\(9^n\\\\) elements. Using two such nets,\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathbb P(\\\\|W_0^{(2)}\\\\|_{\\\\rm op}>M)\\n\\\\le 2\\\\,9^{2n}\\\\exp(-nM^2/8).\\n\\\\tag{11}\\n\\\\]\\nIndeed the operator norm is at most twice the largest bilinear form on these nets, and each bilinear form is \\\\(N(0,1/n)\\\\). Choose a fixed sufficiently large \\\\(M\\\\).\\n\\nTo avoid inverting almost-dependent query directions, temporarily add \\\\(\\\\varepsilon\\\\xi\\\\) to the input of each matrix call, where each \\\\(\\\\xi\\\\) is a new independent standard Gaussian vector. Fix \\\\(\\\\varepsilon>0\\\\). This is only a device for the proof; we will remove it.\\n\\nSuppose previous calls have revealed\\n\\\\[\\nW_0^{(2)}V=Y,\\\\qquad (W_0^{(2)})^\\\\top U=R.\\n\\\\]\\nThe columns of \\\\(V\\\\) are old first-layer input vectors, the columns of \\\\(U\\\\) are old second-layer input vectors, and \\\\(Y,R\\\\) contain the respective answers. Their column counts are fixed. All coordinates derived from these answers give no further information once the answers and roots are fixed. Gaussian conditioning therefore leaves\\n\\\\[\\nW_0^{(2)}\\\\overset d=\\nY(V^\\\\top V)^{-1}V^\\\\top\\n+U(U^\\\\top U)^{-1}R^\\\\top P_{V^\\\\perp}\\n+P_{U^\\\\perp}\\\\widetilde W_0^{(2)}P_{V^\\\\perp}.\\n\\\\tag{12}\\n\\\\]\\nHere \\\\(P_{V^\\\\perp}=I-V(V^\\\\top V)^{-1}V^\\\\top\\\\), and similarly for \\\\(U\\\\); terms for an empty column list are zero. The fresh matrix has independent \\\\(N(0,1/n)\\\\) entries. The first two terms obey both recorded equations; the remaining Gaussian matrix lies in exactly the directions that leave those equations unchanged. Orthogonal components of a centered isotropic Gaussian are independent, which proves (12), including for adaptive queries: each input is chosen from the earlier answers before its new answer is observed.\\n\\nFor a new right-side input \\\\(v\\\\), multiplication gives\\n\\\\[\\nW_0^{(2)}v\\\\overset d=Y\\\\alpha_n+U\\\\beta_n+\\n\\\\frac{\\\\|P_{V^\\\\perp}v\\\\|_2}{\\\\sqrt n}P_{U^\\\\perp}\\\\gamma,\\n\\\\tag{13}\\n\\\\]\\nwhere\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\alpha_n=(V^\\\\top V/n)^{-1}(V^\\\\top v/n),\\\\qquad\\n\\\\beta_n=(U^\\\\top U/n)^{-1}(R^\\\\top P_{V^\\\\perp}v/n).\\n\\\\]\\nThe transpose case exchanges the two layer populations.\\n\\nThe induction statement is joint \\\\(\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\) convergence of every finite tuple of previously constructed vectors on each layer. Equivalently, the average of any continuous measurement \\\\(\\\\psi\\\\) with\\n\\\\[\\n|\\\\psi(s)|\\\\le C(1+\\\\|s\\\\|_2^2)\\n\\\\]\\nconverges to its population expectation. The choices \\\\(\\\\psi(s)=s^2\\\\) and \\\\(\\\\psi(s,t)=st\\\\) give the required squared norms and contractions. Revealing each fresh input noise first extends the old tuple by independent Gaussian coordinates; conditional averaging proves this extension exactly as for the fresh output noise below. Thus all the normalized products in (13) converge. The perturbation guarantees invertibility of their limiting input Gram matrices: when a new column \\\\(a+\\\\varepsilon\\\\xi\\\\) is added to any old input list, conditional Gaussian second-moment calculations give\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\frac{\\\\|P_{V^\\\\perp}(a+\\\\varepsilon\\\\xi)\\\\|_2^2}{n}\\n=\\\\frac{\\\\|P_{V^\\\\perp}a\\\\|_2^2…2409 tokens truncated… this loss, with the same scaling as (1).\\n\\nThe chain rules used here hold along these mean-square differentiable paths: bounded scalar derivatives give convergence of difference quotients by domination, first for bounded directions and then by mean-square approximation. We are not asserting that every coordinatewise nonlinear map is differentiable in operator norm on all of \\\\(L^2\\\\).\\n\\nSince \\\\(K\\\\ge0\\\\), \\\\(|r(t)|\\\\le|r(0)|\\\\). Therefore, on \\\\([0,T]\\\\),\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\|W^{(3)}(t)\\\\|_\\\\infty\\\\le\\\\|W^{(3)}(0)\\\\|_\\\\infty+\\\\pi|r(0)|T,\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\|\\\\dot W^{(2)}\\\\|_{\\\\rm op}\\\\le\\\\pi|r(0)|\\\\|W^{(3)}\\\\|_{L^2},\\\\qquad\\n\\\\|\\\\dot X^{(1)}\\\\|_{L^2}\\\\le2|r(0)|\\\\|W^{(2)}\\\\|_{\\\\rm op}\\\\|W^{(3)}\\\\|_{L^2}.\\n\\\\tag{25}\\n\\\\]\\nThese bounds prevent finite-time blow-up. At finite width, (11), the initial law of large numbers for \\\\(F(z_0^{(1)})\\\\), and \\\\(\\\\|W_0^{(3)}\\\\|_\\\\infty\\\\to0\\\\) in probability put the same bounds on an event of probability tending to one.\\n\\nLet the vector-field bound and Lipschitz constant on a slightly larger bounded set be \\\\(M_T,L_T\\\\). One exact flow step of length \\\\(\\\\Delta\\\\) differs from its Euler step by\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\left\\\\|\\\\int_t^{t+\\\\Delta}[\\\\dot\\\\Theta(\\\\Theta(s))-\\\\dot\\\\Theta(\\\\Theta(t))]\\\\,ds\\\\right\\\\|\\n\\\\le \\\\frac12L_TM_T\\\\Delta^2.\\n\\\\]\\nConsequently the accumulated error satisfies\\n\\\\[\\ne_{k+1}\\\\le(1+L_T\\\\Delta)e_k+C_T\\\\Delta^2,\\n\\\\qquad \\\\max_{k\\\\Delta\\\\le T}e_k\\\\le C_T\'\\\\Delta.\\n\\\\tag{26}\\n\\\\]\\nTo justify the bounded set for Euler, stop at a first exit. The bound proves that the \\\\(X^{(1)}\\\\) and operator components remain inside their margins. Although (21) does not control the output supremum, its update gives\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\|W_k^{(3)}\\\\|_\\\\infty\\\\le\\\\|W_0^{(3)}\\\\|_\\\\infty+\\n\\\\pi\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{s<k}|r_s|.\\n\\\\]\\nUp to the stop, (22) and (26) make \\\\(|r_s|\\\\le|r(0)|+C_T\\\\Delta\\\\); choose the supremum margin larger than \\\\(\\\\pi T(|r(0)|+1)\\\\). For small \\\\(\\\\Delta\\\\) no first exit is possible. Thus (26), also between grid times, holds for both population and finite-width Euler.\\n\\nWe can now define and compare the oracle precisely. Attach the word “oracle” as a subscript to its finite arrays; it denotes the auxiliary system, not another normalization. For fixed population Euler mesh \\\\(\\\\Delta\\\\), all the numbers\\n\\\\[\\nr_k=\\\\mathbb E[W_k^{(3)}H_k^{(2)}]-1,\\\\quad\\n\\\\mathbb E[H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}],\\\\quad\\n\\\\mathbb E[\\\\delta_s^{(2)}\\\\delta_k^{(2)}]\\n\\\\tag{27}\\n\\\\]\\nare deterministic. Start the oracle from the same \\\\(z_0^{(1)},W_0^{(2)}\\\\) as the finite network and from \\\\(W_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},0}^{(3)}=0\\\\). In order at each step set\\n\\\\[\\nh_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}=\\\\phi(F^{-1}(x_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)})),\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nz_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}h_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}\\n-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{s<k}r_s\\\\delta_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(2)}\\\\mathbb E[H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}],\\n\\\\tag{28}\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nh_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}=\\\\phi(z_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}),\\\\qquad\\n\\\\delta_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}=W_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(3)}\\\\odot\\\\phi\'(z_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}),\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\begin{aligned}\\nx_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k+1}^{(1)}=x_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}-2\\\\Delta r_k\\n\\\\bigg((W_0^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}\\n-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{s<k}r_sh_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(1)}\\\\mathbb E[\\\\delta_s^{(2)}\\\\delta_k^{(2)}]\\\\bigg),\\\\\\\\\\nW_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k+1}^{(3)}=W_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(3)}-2\\\\Delta r_kh_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}.\\n\\\\end{aligned} \\\\tag{29}\\n\\\\]\\nThis is a fixed finite Gaussian calculation. Its output weights obey the deterministic coordinate bound \\\\(\\\\pi\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{s<k}|r_s|\\\\). Hence the product defining \\\\(\\\\delta^{(2)}\\\\) can be replaced, without changing any oracle value, by a globally Lipschitz map that clips its first argument above this bound. All other maps meet the finite Gaussian lemma. Its limiting variables are exactly the population Euler variables: this follows instruction by instruction from (5)–(6) at population level and the already constructed action (18)–(19).\\n\\nRecord its finite matrix memory as\\n\\\\[\\nW_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}-\\\\frac{2\\\\Delta}{n}\\\\sum_{s<k}\\nr_s\\\\delta_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(2)}(h_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(1)})^\\\\top.\\n\\\\]\\nIts actual matrix action is not exactly the oracle preactivation (28). Their difference is\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\begin{aligned}\\nW_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}h_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}-z_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}\\n=-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{s<k}r_s\\\\delta_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(2)}\\n\\\\left(\\\\frac{(h_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(1)})^\\\\top h_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}}n-\\n\\\\mathbb E[H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}]\\\\right).\\n\\\\end{aligned} \\\\tag{30}\\n\\\\]\\nThe analogous backward discrepancy is the same sum with \\\\(h_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(1)}\\\\) multiplying the empirical-minus-population \\\\(\\\\delta^{(2)}\\\\) contraction. These are exact identities.\\n\\nLet \\\\(\\\\zeta_n\\\\) be the largest absolute error among the finitely many contractions in (30), their backward versions, the output averages, and the squared normalized norms of the oracle vectors. The finite Gaussian result proves \\\\(\\\\zeta_n\\\\to0\\\\) in probability. In particular the oracle vector norms remain bounded. Put\\n\\\\[\\ne_{n,k}=\\\\frac{\\\\|x_k^{(1)}-x_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}\\\\|_2}{\\\\sqrt n}\\n+\\\\|W_k^{(2)}-W_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}\\\\|_{\\\\rm op}\\n+\\\\frac{\\\\|W_k^{(3)}-W_{\\\\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(3)}\\\\|_2}{\\\\sqrt n}.\\n\\\\]\\nEquations (22) and (30) bound every forward, backward, and residual difference by \\\\(C_{T,\\\\Delta}(e_{n,k}+\\\\zeta_n)\\\\). Subtracting the updates gives\\n\\\\[\\ne_{n,k+1}\\\\le(1+C_{T,\\\\Delta}\\\\Delta)e_{n,k}+C_{T,\\\\Delta}\\\\Delta\\\\zeta_n.\\n\\\\tag{31}\\n\\\\]\\nInitially \\\\(e_{n,0}=\\\\|W_0^{(3)}\\\\|_2/\\\\sqrt n=O_{\\\\mathbb P}(n^{-1})\\\\). Since \\\\(N=\\\\lceil T/\\\\Delta\\\\rceil\\\\) is fixed, summing this recurrence proves \\\\(\\\\max_{k\\\\le N}e_{n,k}\\\\to0\\\\). We have therefore proved the actual finite Euler width limit without feeding random scalar coefficients into the Gaussian lemma.\\n\\nThe same argument controls any fixed finite set of extra forward and backward measurements: expand each learned-matrix use by (5) or (6), use the population contraction to construct its oracle version, and add its empirical contraction errors to \\\\(\\\\zeta_n\\\\). There are only finitely many such errors. For continuity of these measurements, allow arbitrary finite compositions of bounded Lipschitz coordinate maps, \\\\(F^{-1}\\\\), linear combinations, and the current \\\\(W^{(2)},(W^{(2)})^*\\\\). All coordinate instructions must be globally Lipschitz. In a product, both varying factors must be bounded, either intrinsically or by clipping. Products containing the unbounded backward field are added only later through the explicit tail argument. Induction on these operations, using (22), gives an error at most \\\\(C_{\\\\mathrm{measurement}}d_n\\\\) for two states on the same finite space, and \\\\(C_{\\\\mathrm{measurement}}d\\\\) on the population spaces.\\n\\nChoose a countable dense collection of those measurements. For each finite list of same-layer measurements, compare its joint empirical law in \\\\(\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\), including dot products. The action-law distance is the sum of these bounded distances with weights \\\\(2^{-j}\\\\), over a fixed enumeration of finite lists. By (26) and (31), its finite-flow versus population-flow error, for any fixed list, has the form\\n\\\\[\\nC_T\\\\Delta+o_{\\\\mathbb P,n\\\\to\\\\infty;\\\\,\\\\Delta}(1)+C_T\\\\Delta.\\n\\\\tag{32}\\n\\\\]\\nFor any requested accuracy, choose a small fixed \\\\(\\\\Delta\\\\), then take \\\\(n\\\\to\\\\infty\\\\). Uniform time continuity of each fixed measurement, supplied by (22) and the bounded derivatives, gives uniformity on \\\\([0,T]\\\\). The summable weights extend this to the action-law distance. This statement also holds for any finitely many time points jointly, by appending all those measurements to the same finite oracle calculation before taking the limit.\\n\\nThe population evolution is autonomous on the state just constructed. To see this also at the level of its measured law, start the collection of measurements at a current state instead of at initialization, and take all their mean-square limits on each layer. These spaces are preserved by the current action and adjoint and by the coordinate operations. If two current measured laws agree, sending each measurement to its counterpart preserves every squared norm and inner product. It extends to a surjective linear isometry between the two generated spaces, commutes with the coordinate functions, intertwines the current action and adjoint, and sends \\\\(\\\\delta^{(2)}\\\\otimes H^{(1)}\\\\) to its counterpart. The integral iteration proving existence stays in these spaces. Uniqueness therefore identifies the future laws of the two states. Restarting at time \\\\(s\\\\) and evolving for time \\\\(t\\\\) gives the same law as evolving for \\\\(s+t\\\\); no extra historical information is required.\\n\\nIt remains to pass from flow to the exact GD in (1), and to justify the unbounded observables. For a single GD step let \\\\(b=-2r_n(W^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta^{(2)}\\\\), the transformed first-layer velocity. The letter \\\\(b\\\\) is used only in the following cubic calculation. Coordinatewise,\\n\\\\[\\nF(z^{(1)}+\\\\eta_n\\\\phi\'(z^{(1)})\\\\odot b)\\n=F(z^{(1)})+\\\\eta_nb\\n+\\\\eta_n^2z^{(1)}\\\\odot\\\\phi\'(z^{(1)})^2\\\\odot b^2\\n+\\\\frac{\\\\eta_n^3}{3}\\\\phi\'(z^{(1)})^3\\\\odot b^3.\\n\\\\tag{33}\\n\\\\]\\nOn the bounded state set, \\\\(\\\\|b\\\\|_2/\\\\sqrt n\\\\le C_T\\\\), hence \\\\(\\\\|b\\\\|_\\\\infty\\\\le C_T\\\\sqrt n\\\\). Adding the \\\\(O(\\\\eta_n^2)\\\\) local flow error and summing the recurrence as in (26) gives\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\sup_{t\\\\le T}d_n(\\\\Theta_n^{\\\\mathrm{GD}}(t),\\\\Theta_n^{\\\\mathrm{flow}}(t))\\n\\\\le C_T(\\\\eta_n+\\\\eta_n\\\\sqrt n+\\\\eta_n^2n)\\\\longrightarrow0\\n\\\\tag{34}\\n\\\\]\\nwith probability tending to one. Stop first on a larger bounded set, as before. The output supremum is controlled separately by its sum of bounded activation updates and the residual bound transferred from the flow. This closes the stop. At intermediate times (33) with \\\\(0\\\\le\\\\eta\\\\le\\\\eta_n\\\\) controls the transformed coordinate of the linearly interpolated raw parameter, so (34) holds for the specified interpolation.\\n\\nHere is the required control for quantities containing a possibly large backward coordinate. For this paragraph only, write \\\\(p=(W^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta^{(2)}\\\\) at finite width and \\\\(P=(W^{(2)})^*\\\\delta^{(2)}\\\\) in the population. This abbreviation names the same repeatedly used backward measurement, not a new network state. Equations (22), (32), and (34) show uniformly in time that the empirical joint law of \\\\((z^{(1)},p)\\\\) converges in \\\\(\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\) to that of \\\\((Z^{(1)},P)\\\\). The latter laws form a compact \\\\(\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\) family because these population variables are continuous in mean square.\\n\\nConsequently the squared tails vanish uniformly:\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\lim_{A\\\\to\\\\infty}\\\\limsup_{n\\\\to\\\\infty}\\n\\\\mathbb P\\\\left(\\\\sup_{t\\\\le T}\\\\frac1n\\\\sum_i p_i(t)^2\\\\mathbf1_{|p_i(t)|>A}>\\\\varepsilon\\\\right)=0.\\n\\\\tag{35}\\n\\\\]\\nFor completeness, if \\\\(p,P\\\\) are coupled, splitting according to \\\\(|P|\\\\le A/2\\\\) gives\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathbb E[p^2\\\\mathbf1_{|p|>A}]\\n\\\\le4\\\\mathbb E[(p-P)^2]+2\\\\mathbb E[P^2\\\\mathbf1_{|P|>A/2}].\\n\\\\]\\nCompactness gives uniformly small population tails by a finite cover; the uniform \\\\(\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\) convergence then proves (35).\\n\\nClip \\\\(p\\\\) to \\\\([-A,A]\\\\). For fixed \\\\(A\\\\), both \\\\(\\\\phi\'(z^{(1)})\\\\operatorname{clip}_A(p)\\\\) and \\\\(\\\\phi\'(z^{(1)})^2\\\\operatorname{clip}_A(p)\\\\) are Lipschitz measurements. Their squared errors from the unclipped fields are bounded by the tail in (35), since \\\\(|\\\\phi\'|\\\\le1\\\\). Applying \\\\(W^{(2)}\\\\) to their difference multiplies its root-mean-square size by at most its operator bound. Thus, first taking \\\\(n\\\\to\\\\infty\\\\), then \\\\(A\\\\to\\\\infty\\\\), proves uniform convergence of the three kernel blocks in (24), with finite expectations replaced by normalized coordinate sums. It also proves convergence of the squared speeds computed from\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\dot Z^{(1)}=-2r\\\\phi\'(Z^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\\\\delta^{(2)},\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\dot Z^{(2)}=-2r\\\\left\\\\{\\\\mathbb E[(H^{(1)})^2]\\\\delta^{(2)}\\n+W^{(2)}[\\\\phi\'(Z^{(1)})^2(W^{(2)})^*\\\\delta^{(2)}]\\\\right\\\\}.\\n\\\\tag{36}\\n\\\\]\\nThe prediction and loss converge uniformly as well, directly from bounded activations and the output-weight bound.\\n\\nFor GD, the first-layer interpolated slope is its gradient at the left endpoint. The derivative of \\\\(z^{(2)}=W^{(2)}\\\\phi(z^{(1)})\\\\) uses those constant parameter slopes and the current interpolated activation. To compare it with (36) at the endpoint, on \\\\(|p_i|\\\\le A\\\\) the change in \\\\(\\\\phi\'(z_i^{(1)})\\\\) times its first-layer slope is bounded by \\\\(C_T\\\\eta_nA^2\\\\); on the complement both terms are bounded by \\\\(C_T|p_i|\\\\). At fixed partition, joint grid-law convergence follows from (32) and (34), including second moments. Polygonal interpolation is a continuous linear map of the finite grid vector. ## Ordinal 1245 agent_message /root/two_input_activity 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/two_input_activity
Payload:
For normalized inputs \(x_1,x_2\in\mathbb R^d\), put

\[
G_{ab}=\frac{x_a^\top x_b}{d},
\qquad
G=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&\rho\\
\rho&1
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad |\rho|<1.
\]

Take \(z_a^{(1)}=W^{(1)}x_a/\sqrt d\), with iid \(N(0,1)\) first-layer weights. Then the initial first-layer coordinate pair is \(N(0,G)\).

For the sum loss \(L_n=\sum_{a=1}^2r_{n,a}^2\), the exact discrete updates are

\[
z_{a,k+1}^{(1)}
=
z_{a,k}^{(1)}
-
2\eta_n\sum_{b=1}^2
G_{ab}r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(1)},
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\eta_n}{n}
\sum_{b=1}^2
r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}
(h_{b,k}^{(1)})^\top,
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
-
2\eta_n\sum_{b=1}^2r_{n,b,k}h_{b,k}^{(2)}.
\]

Here

\[
\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\phi'(z_a^{(2)}),
\qquad
\delta_a^{(1)}
=
\phi'(z_a^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^\top\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]

The corresponding candidate population flow is therefore

\[
\dot Z_a^{(1)}
=
-2\sum_bG_{ab}r_b\phi'(Z_b^{(1)})
(W^{(2)})^*\delta_b^{(2)},
\]

\[
\dot W^{(2)}
=
-2\sum_br_b\delta_b^{(2)}\otimes H_b^{(1)},
\qquad
\dot W^{(3)}
=
-2\sum_br_bH_b^{(2)}.
\]

This is a material change from the one-input proof. In particular, the coordinate change \(X_a^{(1)}=F(Z_a^{(1)})\) gives

\[
\dot X_a^{(1)}
=
-2\sum_b
G_{ab}r_b
\frac{\phi'(Z_b^{(1)})}{\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})}
(W^{(2)})^*\delta_b^{(2)}.
\]

Only the diagonal terms cancel.

More strongly, a smooth coordinate change that removed all activation derivatives for arbitrary backward fields would require Jacobian

\[
J(z)=\operatorname{diag}(1+z_a^2)\,G^{-1}.
\]

For \(a\ne b\), its entries violate mixed-partial compatibility:

\[
\partial_{z_a}J_{ab}=2z_a(G^{-1})_{ab},
\qquad
\partial_{z_b}J_{aa}=0.
\]

Thus such a transformation does not exist when \(\rho\ne0\). The previous \(L^2\)-Lipschitz existence/stability proof cannot simply be reused. The remaining results below are rigorous consequences once the new population evolution and its finite-width approximation have been established.

The three kernel matrices are

\[
K_{ab}^{(1)}
=
G_{ab}\mathbb E[\delta_a^{(1)}\delta_b^{(1)}],
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(2)}
=
\mathbb E[H_a^{(1)}H_b^{(1)}]\,
\mathbb E[\delta_a^{(2)}\delta_b^{(2)}],
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(3)}
=
\mathbb E[H_a^{(2)}H_b^{(2)}].
\]

Each is positive semidefinite. With \(K=\sum_\ell K^{(\ell)}\),

\[
\dot f=-2Kr,
\qquad
\dot L=-4r^\top Kr.
\]

All the desired initial feature-learning conclusions survive for every \(|\rho|<1\) and either label pattern. The precise calculation follows.

Write

\[
Q_{ab}
=
\mathbb E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}],
\qquad
Y_a=Z_{0,a}^{(2)}.
\]

Then \(Q\) is positive definite and \((Y_1,Y_2)\sim N(0,Q)\). Positivity follows because a nonzero linear combination of \(\arctan(Z_{0,1}^{(1)})\) and \(\arctan(Z_{0,2}^{(1)})\) cannot vanish on a full-support Gaussian pair.

For labels \(y_1,y_2\in\{-1,1\}\), define

\[
S=\sum_a y_a\phi(Y_a),
\qquad
U_a=S\phi'(Y_a),
\]

\[
V_{ab}=\mathbb E[U_aU_b],
\qquad
P_a=(W_0^{(2)})^*U_a,
\qquad
B_a=\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})P_a,
\qquad
D_{ab}=\mathbb E[B_aB_b].
\]

The temporary symbols here each recur in several coefficients.

The matrix \(V\) is positive definite. Indeed \(S=0\) only on one line \(Y_2=\pm Y_1\), a probability-zero event. If \(c_1U_1+c_2U_2=0\) almost surely, then

\[
c_1\phi'(Y_1)+c_2\phi'(Y_2)=0
\]

almost surely and hence everywhere by full support and continuity. Varying \(Y_1\) and \(Y_2\) independently gives \(c_1=c_2=0\).

Conditioning on both initial forward calls gives the simultaneous transpose law

\[
P_a
=
\sum_{c=1}^2
H_{0,c}^{(1)}
\left[
Q^{-1}\mathbb E
\begin{pmatrix}
Y_1U_a\\Y_2U_a
\end{pmatrix}
\right]_c
+
\Gamma_a,
\]

where \((\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\sim N(0,V)\) is independent of the first-layer root pair. Consequently \(D\) is positive definite: conditional on that pair, \((B_1,B_2)\) has positive-definite covariance

\[
\operatorname{diag}(\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}))
\,V\,
\operatorname{diag}(\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})).
\]

Define the displacement coefficients

\[
T_a=\sum_bG_{ab}y_bB_b,
\]

\[
R_a
=
\sum_by_bQ_{ab}U_b
+
W_0^{(2)}
\left[\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a\right].
\]

Direct substitution into the integral equations yields

\[
W^{(3)}(t)=2tS+o_{L^2}(t),
\qquad
\delta_a^{(2)}(t)=2tU_a+o_{L^2}(t),
\]

\[
Z_a^{(1)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(1)}
=
2t^2T_a+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
W^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}
=
2t^2\sum_by_bU_b\otimes H_{0,b}^{(1)}
+
o_{\mathrm{op}}(t^2),
\]

\[
Z_a^{(2)}(t)-Y_a
=
2t^2R_a+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\]

For each \(a\), \(\mathbb E[T_a^2]>0\), since conditional on the first-layer roots its Gaussian variance is positive.

Put

\[
A_1=y^\top(G\circ D)y>0,
\qquad
A_2=y^\top(Q\circ V)y>0,
\]

where \(\circ\) means entrywise product. Their positivity also follows directly from

\[
A_1
=
\mathbb E[(y_1B_1,y_2B_2)G(y_1B_1,y_2B_2)^\top],
\]

and its counterpart for \(A_2,Q,U\). Adjunction gives

\[
\sum_a y_a\mathbb E[U_aR_a]=A_1+A_2>0.
\]

Thus the second hidden layer moves. In fact each input’s second-layer preactivation moves. Swapping the two inputs multiplies \(U_a\) by \(y_1y_2\) and interchanges the \(R_a\). Hence

\[
y_1\mathbb E[U_1R_1]
=
y_2\mathbb E[U_2R_2]
=
\frac{A_1+A_2}{2}>0.
\]

The kernel expansions are

\[
K^{(1)}(t)=4t^2(G\circ D)+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)=4t^2(Q\circ V)+o(t^2),
\]

and

\[
y^\top K^{(3)}(t)y
=
y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y
+
4(A_1+A_2)t^2+o(t^2).
\]

Therefore

\[
y^\top K(t)y
=
y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y
+
8(A_1+A_2)t^2+o(t^2).
\]

The limiting kernel changes for both label patterns and every admissible angle. Each of \(G\circ D\) and \(Q\circ V\) is positive definite; for example,

\[
c^\top(G\circ D)c
\ge
\lambda_{\min}(G)\sum_ac_a^2D_{aa}>0
\]

for nonzero \(c\). Also \(K^{(3)}(0)\) is positive definite by the same full-support argument used for \(Q\). Thus all three blocks are positive definite for sufficiently small \(t>0\).

Let \(C=K^{(3)}(0)\). Since \(L(0)=2\),

\[
f(t)=2tCy+o(t),
\qquad
L(t)=2-4t\,y^\top Cy+o(t),
\]

with \(y^\top Cy>0\). The squared hidden displacements and speeds for each input have respectively the leading coefficients

\[
4t^4\mathbb E[T_a^2],
\qquad
16t^2\mathbb E[T_a^2]
\]

on the first layer, and the same expressions with \(R_a\) on the second layer. Every coefficient is positive.

Initial variances and errors of the best affine fits to \(\arctan\) remain positive for all four preactivation variables on a common short interval by their nondegenerate initial Gaussian laws and mean-square continuity.

The central unresolved extension is the continuous-time existence/uniqueness and stable width/GD approximation for \(\rho\ne0\); none of these small-time positivity calculations resolves that missing analytic step.

## Ordinal 1253 agent_message /root/two_input_limit_route 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/two_input_limit_route
Payload:
The fixed-mesh two-input width limit extends, but the continuous-time limit cannot be obtained by the existing scalar proof without a new stability argument.

For normalized inputs, write their Gram matrix as

\[
G=\begin{pmatrix}1&\rho\\ \rho&1\end{pmatrix},
\qquad |\rho|<1.
\]

Ordinary first-layer GD induces the population candidate

\[
\dot Z_a^{(1)}
=
-2\sum_{b=1}^2
G_{ab}r_b\phi'(Z_b^{(1)})
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta_b^{(2)},
\qquad a=1,2,
\]

with corresponding finite equations. The shared matrix and output updates are finite sums over \(b\). Their fixed-step rank-one expansions and fixed-program Gaussian conditioning arguments extend directly.

The problem arises when making the mesh tend to zero. In the one-input proof, \(X=F(Z)\), \(F'(Z)=1/\phi'(Z)\), removes the first-layer multiplier. With two inputs, componentwise \(X_a=F(Z_a)\) instead gives

\[
\dot X_a^{(1)}
=
-2\sum_{b=1}^2
G_{ab}r_b
\frac{1+(Z_a^{(1)})^2}{1+(Z_b^{(1)})^2}
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta_b^{(2)}.
\]

The off-diagonal coefficients are unbounded. The uniform \(L^2\)-Lipschitz estimates used for population existence, uniqueness, Euler convergence, and GD-to-flow comparison therefore do not follow.

Working directly in \(Z\) also leaves the problematic difference

\[
\bigl(\phi'(Z_b^{(1)})-\phi'(\widetilde Z_b^{(1)})\bigr)
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta_b^{(2)}.
\]

A bounded \(L^2\) norm of the backward field does not bound this expression by a constant times
\(\|Z_b^{(1)}-\widetilde Z_b^{(1)}\|_{L^2}\). Multiplication by an unbounded random variable is not a bounded operator on \(L^2\). Bounded activation and bounded readout do not make the Gaussian backward coordinate bounded.

Nor can one simply choose a better coordinate change cancelling both input directions. Such a change would need, up to a fixed nonsingular left factor,

\[
DX(z)
=
\operatorname{diag}(1+z_1^2,1+z_2^2)G^{-1}.
\]

For \(\rho\ne0\), its mixed partial derivatives disagree. The two scalar cancellations cannot simultaneously be integrated into a coordinate map.

Two rigorous extensions are immediately available:

1. **Orthogonal inputs, \(\rho=0\).** Componentwise \(X_a=F(Z_a)\) works. All arguments extend with two first-layer fields, two derived second-layer fields, and the same shared \(W^{(2)},W^{(3)}\). Both label patterns are allowed.
2. **Any \(|\rho|<1\), with inverse-Gram preconditioning of the first-layer updates.** This makes the preactivation updates diagonal in the input index and restores the same proof. This explicitly changes the optimizer and must not be presented as ordinary GD.

For ordinary GD with nonzero \(\rho\), a sufficient new route would establish mesh-uniform, sufficiently light tails of the backward fields and an Osgood stability estimate. For example, moment growth \(\|P\|_{L^q}\le Cq\) leads, after localization, to a modulus comparable to \(e\log(1/e)\), strong enough for uniqueness. The finite-program theorem supplies moment convergence at each fixed mesh, but does **not** supply this bound uniformly as the number of Euler steps grows. Proving it is a substantive new obligation.

Thus I cannot certify the full ordinary-GD two-input extension from the existing proof. The limitation is a genuine missing argument, not evidence that the limit itself is false.

## Ordinal 1259 agent_message /root/two_input_coordinates 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/two_input_coordinates
Payload:
For two inputs with empirical input Gram matrix
\[
G=\begin{pmatrix}1&\rho\\ \rho&1\end{pmatrix},
\qquad -1<\rho<1,
\]
ordinary first-layer GD induces, under the sum-loss convention,
\[
\dot Z_a^{(1)}
=
-2\sum_{b=1}^2
G_{ab}r_b\phi'(Z_b^{(1)})
(W^{(2)})^*\delta_b^{(2)}.
\]
This differs substantively from the single-input first-layer equation.

The cubic transformation gives
\[
\dot X_a^{(1)}
=
-2\sum_{b=1}^2
G_{ab}r_b
\frac{1+(Z_a^{(1)})^2}{1+(Z_b^{(1)})^2}
(W^{(2)})^*\delta_b^{(2)}.
\]
For \(\rho\ne0\), the cross ratios are unbounded. The initial bivariate Gaussian law is nondegenerate, so this is already an issue on its full support.

There is no replacement \(C^2\) invertible pointwise coordinate change that cancels both backward fields. Indeed, cancellation would require
\[
DT(z)\,G\,\operatorname{diag}(\phi'(z_1),\phi'(z_2))=C
\]
for a fixed invertible matrix \(C\), or
\[
DT(z)=C\operatorname{diag}(1+z_1^2,1+z_2^2)G^{-1}.
\]
For row \(a\),
\[
\partial_1 T_a
=
\frac{C_{a1}(1+z_1^2)-\rho C_{a2}(1+z_2^2)}
{1-\rho^2},
\]
\[
\partial_2 T_a
=
\frac{-\rho C_{a1}(1+z_1^2)+C_{a2}(1+z_2^2)}
{1-\rho^2}.
\]
Equality of mixed partials forces
\[
C_{a2}z_2=C_{a1}z_1
\]
for every \((z_1,z_2)\). If \(\rho\ne0\), each row of \(C\) is zero, contradicting invertibility.

Consequently, the previous global \(L^2\)-Lipschitz vector-field argument does not extend by adding sample indices. The problematic term in a difference estimate is
\[
[\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})-\phi'(\widetilde Z_a^{(1)})]
\,\widetilde P_a,
\qquad
\widetilde P_a=(\widetilde W^{(2)})^*\widetilde\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]
An \(L^2\) bound on \(\widetilde P_a\) cannot bound this term by a constant times \(\|Z_a^{(1)}-\widetilde Z_a^{(1)}\|_{L^2}\). Perturbing \(Z\) only on \(\{|\widetilde P_a|>R\}\), where \(\phi''(Z)\) stays away from zero, makes the ratio arbitrarily large.

This failure is not merely formal: bounded-operator states exhibiting it exist. On a first-layer Gaussian space, take a centered Gaussian \(P\) and the rank-one action
\[
W^{(2)}V=\mathbb E[PV]\,1.
\]
At \(Z^{(1)}\equiv1\), \(W^{(3)}\equiv1\), the second-layer preactivation is zero, and the backward field is \(P\). Perturbations supported on the Gaussian tail give precisely the unbounded local Lipschitz ratios above. This example concerns the bounded-state class used in the old existence argument; it does not establish nonuniqueness along the actual initialized trajectory.

A sufficient new regularity lemma would be uniform exponential tails for the backward fields:
\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\mathbb E\exp(c|P_a(t)|)\le C_T.
\]
Splitting into \(|P_a|\le R\) and its complement yields, for small \(e=\|Z-\widetilde Z\|_{L^2}\),
\[
\|[\phi'(Z)-\phi'(\widetilde Z)]P_a\|_{L^2}
\le C_T e\log(C_T/e).
\]
The resulting error modulus gives uniqueness because
\[
\int_0
\frac{de}{e\log(C_T/e)}=\infty.
\]
Sub-Gaussian tails give the stronger modulus \(e\sqrt{\log(C_T/e)}\). However, neither estimate is supplied by the existing proof. Fixed finite-program moment bounds do not give bounds uniform in the Euler mesh as the number of calls tends to infinity.

For \(\rho=0\), the extension is valid using the old method:
\[
X_a^{(1)}=F(Z_a^{(1)}),
\qquad
\dot X_a^{(1)}=-2r_a(W^{(2)})^*\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]
There are two first-layer coordinate fields, finitely many extra sample contractions, and sums of two rank-one updates; the previous operator/\(L^2\) stability and learning-rate estimates then extend directly. Applying a \(G^{-1}\) preconditioner to first-layer training would recover these decoupled equations for \(\rho\ne0\), but it changes the finite training algorithm and cannot be silently substituted.

Thus the rigorous status is: orthogonal inputs are covered by the old architecture; correlated inputs require an additional continuous-time regularity or stability argument. The scalar-coordinate proof does not currently establish that extension.

## Ordinal 1263 message assistant commentary
There is a real new issue for nonorthogonal inputs: updating the shared first-layer weights couples the two preactivations, so the cubic change of coordinates no longer cancels the activation derivatives. I’m checking whether a different stability argument closes that gap; the two-input equations alone would not prove the limit exists.

In particular,\\n\\\\[\\nx_{k+1}^{(1)}=x_k^{(1)}-2\\\\Delta r_{n,k}(W_k^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta_k^{(2)}.\\n\\\\tag{3}\\n\\\\]\\nThis is Euler for the transformed flow; transforming the GD iterates in (1) produces an additional defect, estimated below.\\n\\nIterating the Euler matrix update gives\\n\\\\[\\nW_k^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}-\\\\frac{2\\\\Delta}{n}\\n\\\\sum_{s<k}r_{n,s}\\\\delta_s^{(2)}(h_s^{(1)})^\\\\top.\\n\\\\tag{4}\\n\\\\]\\nConsequently, for any appropriate vectors \\\\(v,u\\\\),\\n\\\\[\\nW_k^{(2)}v=W_0^{(2)}v-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{s<k}r_{n,s}\\\\delta_s^{(2)}\\n\\\\frac{(h_s^{(1)})^\\\\top v}{n},\\n\\\\tag{5}\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\n(W_k^{(2)})^\\\\top u=(W_0^{(2)})^\\\\top u-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{s<k}r_{n,s}h_s^{(1)}\\n\\\\frac{(\\\\delta_s^{(2)})^\\\\top u}{n}.\\n\\\\tag{6}\\n\\\\]\\nThus training adds a finite sum of rank-one matrices. At fixed \\\\(\\\\Delta\\\\) and \\\\(T\\\\), only finitely many initial-matrix calls and scalar contractions need to be understood.\\n\\nThere is a logical detail here: the empirical coefficients in (5)–(6) are random. We first analyze an auxiliary finite system, the oracle, using deterministic population residuals and contractions in their place. Those numbers will be constructed below from the initial population action; they are not assumptions about the empirical network. We then prove that its empirical contractions approach the supplied numbers, and compare it with the empirical-feedback Euler system.\\n\\nThe matrix calculation needed for this construction is elementary Gaussian conditioning. For example, the first forward call is\\n\\\\[\\nz_0^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}.\\n\\\\]\\nConditional on \\\\(h_0^{(1)}\\\\), its coordinates are independent centered Gaussians of variance \\\\(\\\\|h_0^{(1)}\\\\|_2^2/n\\\\). Hence the initial population coordinates satisfy\\n\\\\[\\nZ_0^{(1)}\\\\sim N(0,1),\\\\quad H_0^{(1)}=\\\\phi(Z_0^{(1)}),\\\\quad\\nZ_0^{(2)}\\\\sim N(0,\\\\mathbb E[(H_0^{(1)})^2]).\\n\\\\tag{7}\\n\\\\]\\nIn the oracle, \\\\(W_0^{(3)}=0\\\\) and \\\\(r_0=-1\\\\). Until the oracle comparison below, the vectors in these first-call examples are oracle vectors. Its first update leaves \\\\(h_0^{(1)}\\\\) and \\\\(W_0^{(2)}\\\\) unchanged and gives\\n\\\\[\\nW_1^{(3)}=2\\\\Delta\\\\phi(z_0^{(2)}),\\\\qquad\\n\\\\delta_1^{(2)}=2\\\\Delta\\\\phi(z_0^{(2)})\\\\odot\\\\phi\'(z_0^{(2)}).\\n\\\\]\\nThe next transpose call depends on the matrix already used in the forward pass. Define, for nonzero \\\\(b\\\\),\\n\\\\[\\nP_{b^\\\\perp}=I-\\\\frac{bb^\\\\top}{b^\\\\top b}.\\n\\\\]\\nConditioning each Gaussian row on its observed dot product with \\\\(h_0^{(1)}\\\\), and then summing the rows with coefficients \\\\(\\\\delta_{1,j}^{(2)}\\\\), gives\\n\\\\[\\n(W_0^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta_1^{(2)}\\n\\\\overset d=\\n\\\\frac{(z_0^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta_1^{(2)}}{\\\\|h_0^{(1)}\\\\|_2^2}h_0^{(1)}\\n+\\\\frac{\\\\|\\\\delta_1^{(2)}\\\\|_2}{\\\\sqrt n}P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\\\\perp}\\\\gamma^{(1)}.\\n\\\\tag{8}\\n\\\\]\\nHere equality is conditional in distribution, and \\\\(\\\\gamma^{(1)}\\\\sim N(0,I_n)\\\\) is independent of the preceding history. The first term supplies precisely the overlap required by\\n\\\\[\\n(h_0^{(1)})^\\\\top(W_0^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta_1^{(2)}\\n=(z_0^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta_1^{(2)};\\n\\\\]\\nthe second is perpendicular to \\\\(h_0^{(1)}\\\\).\\n\\nThe next update is\\n\\\\[\\nx_2^{(1)}=x_0^{(1)}-2\\\\Delta r_1(W_0^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta_1^{(2)},\\\\qquad\\nh_2^{(1)}=\\\\phi(F^{-1}(x_2^{(1)})),\\n\\\\]\\nwhere \\\\(r_1=2\\\\Delta\\\\mathbb E[\\\\phi(Z_0^{(2)})^2]-1\\\\).\\nThus the next initial-matrix call is \\\\(W_0^{(2)}h_2^{(1)}\\\\). Condition on both previous observations. Multiplying the resulting conditional matrix by \\\\(h_2^{(1)}\\\\) gives\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\begin{aligned}\\nW_0^{(2)}h_2^{(1)}\\\\overset d={}&\\n\\\\frac{(h_0^{(1)})^\\\\top h_2^{(1)}}{\\\\|h_0^{(1)}\\\\|_2^2}z_0^{(2)}\\\\\\\\\\n&+\\\\frac{[P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\\\\perp}(W_0^{(2)})^\\\\top\\\\delta_1^{(2)}]^\\\\top h_2^{(1)}}{\\\\|\\\\delta_1^{(2)}\\\\|_2^2}\\\\delta_1^{(2)}\\\\\\\\\\n&+\\\\frac{\\\\|P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\\\\perp}h_2^{(1)}\\\\|_2}{\\\\sqrt n}\\nP_{(\\\\delta_1^{(2)})^\\\\perp}\\\\gamma^{(2)}.\\n\\\\end{aligned} \\\\tag{9}\\n\\\\]\\nHere \\\\(\\\\gamma^{(2)}\\\\sim N(0,I_n)\\\\) is independent of the previous history. The denominators in (8)–(9) are positive almost surely for \\\\(\\\\Delta>0\\\\), because the initial Gaussians are nondegenerate and \\\\(\\\\phi\'(s)>0\\\\). The first response retains the old forward answer. The second supplies the remaining overlap required by the old transpose answer. The last term disturbs neither observation. Formula (5), with population coefficients for the oracle, then adds the learned rank-one correction to obtain its actual preactivation node.\\n\\nWe now prove the finite Gaussian fact needed to repeat this argument, including the case of dependent query directions. The proof below requires only empirical convergence against continuous measurements with at most quadratic growth. It does not claim convergence against arbitrary discontinuous functions.\\n\\nConsider a fixed finite calculation starting from iid coordinate tuples with finite second moments, independent of \\\\(W_0^{(2)}\\\\). Its instructions are fixed globally Lipschitz coordinate functions, linear combinations with deterministic coefficients, and calls to \\\\(W_0^{(2)}\\\\) or its transpose. Root tuples may include both \\\\(z_0^{(1)}\\\\) and \\\\(F(z_0^{(1)})\\\\). Then all same-layer joint empirical laws have deterministic limits, including their second moments.\\n\\nFor precision, the distance between two laws used here is the smallest root-mean-square distance between random vectors having those laws, usually denoted \\\\(\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\). For empirical laws, matching coordinates gives the bound\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\!\\\\left(\\\\frac1n\\\\sum_i\\\\delta_{v_i},\\\\frac1n\\\\sum_i\\\\delta_{\\\\widetilde v_i}\\\\right)^2\\n\\\\le\\\\frac1n\\\\sum_i\\\\|v_i-\\\\widetilde v_i\\\\|_2^2.\\n\\\\tag{10}\\n\\\\]\\nConvergence in this distance means weak convergence together with convergence of second moments. Thus it includes all dot products of a fixed joint tuple.\\n\\nFirst, the Gaussian matrix has a uniform operator bound with exponentially high probability. A \\\\(1/4\\\\)-net of the Euclidean unit sphere has at most \\\\(9^n\\\\) elements. Using two such nets,\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathbb P(\\\\|W_0^{(2)}\\\\|_{\\\\rm op}>M)\\n\\\\le 2\\\\,9^{2n}\\\\exp(-nM^2/8).\\n\\\\tag{11}\\n\\\\]\\nIndeed the operator norm is at most twice the largest bilinear form on these nets, and each bilinear form is \\\\(N(0,1/n)\\\\). Choose a fixed sufficiently large \\\\(M\\\\).\\n\\nTo avoid inverting almost-dependent query directions, temporarily add \\\\(\\\\varepsilon\\\\xi\\\\) to the input of each matrix call, where each \\\\(\\\\xi\\\\) is a new independent standard Gaussian vector. Fix \\\\(\\\\varepsilon>0\\\\). This is only a device for the proof; we will remove it.\\n\\nSuppose previous calls have revealed\\n\\\\[\\nW_0^{(2)}V=Y,\\\\qquad (W_0^{(2)})^\\\\top U=R.\\n\\\\]\\nThe columns of \\\\(V\\\\) are old first-layer input vectors, the columns of \\\\(U\\\\) are old second-layer input vectors, and \\\\(Y,R\\\\) contain the respective answers. Their column counts are fixed. All coordinates derived from these answers give no further information once the answers and roots are fixed. Gaussian conditioning therefore leaves\\n\\\\[\\nW_0^{(2)}\\\\overset d=\\nY(V^\\\\top V)^{-1}V^\\\\top\\n+U(U^\\\\top U)^{-1}R^\\\\top P_{V^\\\\perp}\\n+P_{U^\\\\perp}\\\\widetilde W_0^{(2)}P_{V^\\\\perp}.\\n\\\\tag{12}\\n\\\\]\\nHere \\\\(P_{V^\\\\perp}=I-V(V^\\\\top V)^{-1}V^\\\\top\\\\), and similarly for \\\\(U\\\\); terms for an empty column list are zero. The fresh matrix has independent \\\\(N(0,1/n)\\\\) entries. The first two terms obey both recorded equations; the remaining Gaussian matrix lies in exactly the directions that leave those equations unchanged. Orthogonal components of a centered isotropic Gaussian are independent, which proves (12), including for adaptive queries: each input is chosen from the earlier answers before its new answer is observed.\\n\\nFor a new right-side input \\\\(v\\\\), multiplication gives\\n\\\\[\\nW_0^{(2)}v\\\\overset d=Y\\\\alpha_n+U\\\\beta_n+\\n\\\\frac{\\\\|P_{V^\\\\perp}v\\\\|_2}{\\\\sqrt n}P_{U^\\\\perp}\\\\gamma,\\n\\\\tag{13}\\n\\\\]\\nwhere\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\alpha_n=(V^\\\\top V/n)^{-1}(V^\\\\top v/n),\\\\qquad\\n\\\\beta_n=(U^\\\\top U/n)^{-1}(R^\\\\top P_{V^\\\\perp}v/n).\\n\\\\]\\nThe transpose case exchanges the two layer populations.\\n\\nThe induction statement is joint \\\\(\\\\mathcal W_2\\\\) convergence of every finite tuple of previously constructed vectors on each layer. Equivalently, the average of any continuous measurement \\\\(\\\\psi\\\\) with\\n\\\\[\\n|\\\\psi(s)|\\\\le C(1+\\\\|s\\\\|_2^2)\\n"}'}]

## Ordinal 1329 agent_message /root/two_input_coordinates 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/two_input_coordinates

The bounded logarithmic derivative yields a useful estimate, but mean energy alone does not close it.

Write \(q=\phi'\) temporarily and prescribe the two backward controls through
\[
\dot z=G\operatorname{diag}(q(z_1),q(z_2))c(t).
\]
The linearized equation is
\[
\dot J
=
G\operatorname{diag}(q'(z_1)c_1,q'(z_2)c_2)J.
\]
Since
\[
c_bq'(z_b)
=
\frac{q'(z_b)}{q(z_b)}
(G^{-1}\dot z)_b,
\qquad
\left|\frac{q'}q\right|\le1,
\]
one obtains
\[
\|J(t)\|
\le
\exp\!\left(C_\rho\int_0^t|\dot z(s)|\,ds\right).
\]
The integrated velocity is controlled in \(L^2\) by mean energy, but its exponential need not even be integrable. Therefore this does not yield an \(L^2\) comparison estimate.

Moreover, the dependence on path variation cannot generally telescope to an endpoint factor as it does in the scalar case. Put
\[
a(s)=\frac{q'(s)}{q(s)}=-\frac{2s}{1+s^2}.
\]
Along the reference trajectory,
\[
d\log\det J
=
\frac{
a(z_1)\,dz_1+a(z_2)\,dz_2
-\rho a(z_1)\,dz_2-\rho a(z_2)\,dz_1
}{1-\rho^2}.
\]
The curl of this one-form is
\[
\frac{\rho}{1-\rho^2}\bigl(a'(z_2)-a'(z_1)\bigr).
\]
It is nonzero near \((z_1,z_2)=(0,1)\), because \(a'(0)=-2\) and \(a'(1)=0\).

Choose a small closed loop around that point, oriented so its integral is positive. It can be realized exactly by the controls
\[
c(t)=\operatorname{diag}(q(z(t)))^{-1}G^{-1}\dot z(t).
\]
After repeating the loop \(N\) times, the state remains inside one fixed compact set, while
\[
\det J=\exp(N\lambda)
\]
for some \(\lambda>0\). Thus no endpoint-only change of metric can remove the amplification for arbitrary backward controls. Total path variation detects it; mean energy supplies no exponential moment for that variation.

The constant \(G^{-1}\) metric also has no sign cancellation. For two paths driven by identical controls,
\[
\frac d{dt}
\bigl[(z-\widetilde z)^\top G^{-1}(z-\widetilde z)\bigr]
=
2\sum_b
(z_b-\widetilde z_b)
\bigl[q(z_b)-q(\widetilde z_b)\bigr]c_b.
\]
At \(z_b\approx1\), \(q'(z_b)<0\), so negative \(c_b\) creates positive expansion of arbitrary magnitude. The bounded activations and output weight do not rule this out: their adjoint image is only controlled in \(L^2\).

These examples concern general controls and bounded-operator states, not a counterexample to the initialized network itself. They show that the proposed repairs need an additional property of the actual trajectory—for example, exponential integrability of its coordinatewise total variation, or suitable uniform tails of the backward fields. Neither follows from the existing mean-energy estimate.

## Ordinal 1331 agent_message /root/two_input_activity 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/two_input_activity
Payload:
The cavity decomposition gives a useful first step but does not currently close the proof.

Remove the influence of first-layer neuron \(i\) from the bulk dynamics, so that the resulting second-layer backward vector \(\delta_{a,\mathrm{cav}}^{(2)}\) is independent of column \(i\) of \(W_0^{(2)}\). Then

\[
(W_{0,:i}^{(2)})^\top\delta_{a,\mathrm{cav}}^{(2)}
\]

is conditionally Gaussian, with variance

\[
\frac{\|\delta_{a,\mathrm{cav}}^{(2)}\|_2^2}{n}
\le
\|\delta_{a,\mathrm{cav}}^{(2)}\|_\infty^2.
\]

The ordinary loss and output-weight bounds control this variance uniformly on finite time intervals.

The unproved step is the correction

\[
(W_{0,:i}^{(2)})^\top
\bigl(
\delta_a^{(2)}-\delta_{a,\mathrm{cav}}^{(2)}
\bigr).
\]

The column deletion creates an initial forward perturbation of root-mean-square order \(n^{-1/2}\). To retain that order dynamically, the bulk stability estimate must control terms of the form

\[
p_{a,j}\phi''(z_{a,j}^{(1)})
\bigl(
z_{a,j}^{(1)}-z_{a,j,\mathrm{cav}}^{(1)}
\bigr),
\qquad
p_a=(W_0^{(2)})^\top\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]

The corresponding variational equation contains the diagonal multiplier

\[
\operatorname{diag}
\bigl(p_{a,j}\phi''(z_{a,j}^{(1)})\bigr).
\]

Its propagator is exactly where uniform tail control is needed. The available operator and root-mean-square bounds yield only \(\max_j|p_{a,j}|=O(\sqrt n)\). Even assuming a bootstrap \(O(\sqrt{\log n})\) maximum gives an amplification \(\exp(C T\sqrt{\log n})\), which does not control the cavity correction uniformly in \(n\). A refined averaged or weighted propagator estimate would therefore be essential.

Bounded second-layer coordinates alone cannot supply the missing estimate. For example, the bounded adaptive choice

\[
\delta_j=\operatorname{sign}(W_{0,ji}^{(2)})
\]

gives

\[
\bigl[(W_0^{(2)})^\top\delta\bigr]_i
=
\sum_j|W_{0,ji}^{(2)}|
\asymp \sqrt n.
\]

Choosing \(i\) uniformly at random preserves coordinate exchangeability while producing fixed-coordinate moments of order \(n^{q/2-1}\) for \(q>2\). This example is not asserted to be realizable by the neural flow; it shows why boundedness and Gaussian initialization do not by themselves imply the desired tails after adaptation.

A rigorous cavity proof would need an additional network-specific estimate controlling bulk sensitivity to one column—potentially through simultaneous cavity systems or response kernels. I have not established that estimate, even on an \(n\)-independent short interval.

For example, RMT has been used to calculate the Jacobian3singular value distribution\nL43@P1: of a wide neural network, which is an important indicator of its architectural soundness: If this\nL44@P1: distribution becomes very diffuse as the network gets deeper (i.e. more layers), then an error signal\nL45@P1: (i.e. the gradient) will be badly distorted as it is backpropagated. Next, we will soon see intuitively that A>Av is\nL125@P2: asymptotically the sum of v and a random Gaussian vector with iid coordinates, independent from v\nL126@P2: (illustrating point (1)). Thus A>Av is coordinatewise correlated with v, illustrating point (2).\nL127@P2: To get this intuition, write each coordinate\nL128@P2: (A\nL129@P2: >Av)α =\nL130@P2: X\nL131@P2: β,γ\nL132@P2: AβαAβγvγ =\nL133@P2: X\nL134@P2: β\nL135@P2: A\nL136@P2: 2\nL137@P2: βαvα +\nL138@P2: X\nL139@P2: β;γ6=α\nL140@P2: AβαAβγvγ, β, γ ∈ [n]. (1)\nL141@P2: Because each Aβα has variance 1/n, the first sum will converge via law of large numbers to vα.\nL142@P2: In the second sum, one can note that each summand is uncorrelated with others (and higher order\nL143@P2: correlations drop off rapidly with n), so one may expect the second sum to converge to a Gaussian\nL144@P2: through some central limit behavior. We can calculate that the second sum P\nL145@P2: β;γ6=α AβαAβγvγ for\nL146@P2: different αs will be uncorrelated, so their limits for different α should be independent Gaussians.\nL147@P2: Likewise, it should be asymptotically independent from v for the same reason.\nL148@P2: This finishes our example and illustrates the overarching philosophy of our proposed\nL149@P2: method. But it is still tedious if we have to manually derive the correlation between\nL150@P2: vα,(Av)α,(A>Av)α, . . . ,((A>A)\nL151@P2: kv)α. In addition, it’s not entirely clear at this point that this\nL152@P2: method can handle the nonlinear ensemble example A = D2BD1B given above. This is where the\nL153@P2: Tensor Programs framework is crucial, which we overview now.\nL154@P2: 1.3 The Tensor Programs Framework\nL155@P2: The Tensor Programs framework can be thought of as a clean, scalable, and rigorous packaging of\nL156@P2: the intuition explained in the last section. We first demonstrate how one can apply this framework\nL157@P2: to compute the correlations between vα,(Av)α,(A>Av)α, . . . ,((A>A)\nL158@P2: kv)α. Then we describe in\nL159@P2: more general terms what the framework is about.\nL160@P2: Calculating Ev v\nL161@P2: >(A>A)kv with Tensor Programs The framework associates a random variable\nL162@P2: Z\nL163@P2: u ∈ R to each u ∈ {v, Av, A>Av, . . . ,(A>A)kv}, representing the distribution of coordinates of\nL164@P2: u in the large n limit. It also comes with a set of symbolic rules to derive Z\nL165@P2: Av given Zv\nL166@P2: , Z\nL167@P2: A\nL168@P2: >Av\nL169@P2: given Z\nL170@P2: Av and Zv\nL171@P2: , and so on. For example, consistent with the intuition laid out in Section 1.2, Z\nL172@P2: v\nL173@P2: and Z\nL174@P2: Av are defined as independent standard Gaussians (since v and Av are asymptotically standard\nL175@P2: Gaussian vectors, independent from each other), and Z\nL176@P2: A\nL177@P2: >Av = Z\nL178@P2: v + S for a standard Gaussian\nL179@P2: S ∈ R independent from Z\nL180@P2: v\nL181@P2: (and Z\nL182@P2: Av). Finally, we can calculate limn→∞ Ev v>(A>A)kv =\nL183@P2: EZ\nL184@P2: vZ(A\nL185@P2: >A)k\nL186@P2: v\nL187@P2: .\nL188@P2: 5\nL189@P2: but this identity holds for any v with iid, zero-mean, unit-variance entries\nL190@P2-3: 3This set of Z-rules handle nonlinearities. For example, we have Z\nL191@P3: φ(v) def = φ(Zv\nL192@P3: ) for any φ : R → R\nL193@P3: (where φ on the LHS is applied coordinatewise to the vector v, but is applied in the usual sense to\nL194@P3: the scalar random variable Z\nL195@P3: v on the RHS.) This reflects the intuition that Zφ(v)\nL196@P3: is the distribution\nL197@P3: of φ(v)’s coordinates. As such, it is not hard to see our proposed method in Section 1.2 can deal\nL198@P3: with nonlinear random matrix ensembles like the example A = D2BD1B given there. The full\nL199@P3: description of these rules can be found in Box 1, but we will not dive into more details here.\nL200@P3: Tensor Programs in general While a major focus of this paper is on RMT, the original motivation\nL201@P3: of Tensor Programs is in understanding wide neural networks. There were 3 lines of research that\nL202@P3: molded the framework, which we briefly overview so we can later state some other contributions of\nL203@P3: this paper: (Research Line 1) In 1994, Radford Neal [31] discovered that shallow neural networks\nL204@P3: with random weights converge to Gaussian processes as their widths tend to infinity. This Neural\nL205@P3: Network-Gaussian Process correspondence has become a fascinating topic ever since, with a flurry of\nL206@P3: activities in recent years extending it [9, 17, 22, 23, 28, 32, 47]. (Research Line 2) Relatedly, a line\nL207@P3: of work, closely connected to the study of Jacobian singular values mentioned above, researched how\nL208@P3: signal propagates inside a neural network with random weights [7, 14–16, 36, 38–40, 52–54], and\nL209@P3: use such insights to improve the initialization scheme or architecture of neural networks. (Research\nL210@P3: Line 3) Recently, Jacot et al. and others [2–5, 11, 19, 24, 55] found that, when trained with gradient\nL211@P3: descent, wide, fully connected neural networks evolve like linear models. This resolved many open\nL212@P3: questions regarding the training and generalization of neural networks, and was followed by intense\nL213@P3: activity generalizing these results to more modern neural architectures [1, 5, 12, 18, 26, 49].\nL214@P3: In all three lines of research, each new architecture required a new paper. For example, going from\nL215@P3: fully connected networks to convolutional neural networks required careful thinking about how the\nL216@P3: newly added dimension of pixel position in the latter changes the argument for the former. It was the\nL217@P3: expectation that every architecture is special in some way, and extending the theory to catch up with\nL218@P3: modern practice would require a paper covering every major architectural advancement starting from\nL219@P3: convolutional neural networks (e.g. normalization layers, attention, etc). Considering the breakneck\nL220@P3: pace of empirical deep learning, this “catch-up” might never happen.\nL221@P3: It was against this backdrop that the Tensor Programs framework [49–51] surprisingly showed all\nL222@P3: three lines of research can be unified into one and simultaneously be generalized to all practically\nL223@P3: relevant neural architectures, now or in the future. There are two major insights that make this\nL224@P3: possible:\nL225@P3: Insight 1 Every computation done in deep learning can be written as a program (i.e. a composition)\nL226@P3: of matrix multiplication and coordinatewise nonlinearities6(for example, a simple neural\nL227@P3: network can be written this way as h = W x, y = φ(h) for some activation function φ, like\nL228@P3: tanh, applied coordinatewise; the example of A = D2BD1B given above can be written\nL229@P3: likewise).\nL230@P3: Insight 2 In every such computation, if the matrices are randomized, then every vector computed\nL231@P3: over the course of such a program has roughly iid coordinates when the matrices’ sizes\nL232@P3: are large. However, for each α, the αth coordinates of different vectors will in general\nL233@P3: be correlated, in the same way across α, and in a way that can be inductively calculated\nL234@P3: from the program structure. The machinery of Tensor Programs allows one to mechanically\nL235@P3: perform this calculation.\nL236@P3: Note that insight 2 is a generalization of (1) and (2) in Section 1.2, and is reflected in the\nL237@P3: Ev v\nL238@P3: >(A>A)kv example above. Given these two insights, it then becomes rather straightforward to\nL239@P3: generalize the three lines of research to any relevant architecture.\nL240@P3: 1.4 Tensor Programs Master Theorems\nL241@P3: The Tensor Programs framework was first laid out in (the unpublished manuscript) Yang [49] but\nL242@P3: was written densely. Subsequently, [50] and [51] rewrote, in an accessible, pedagogical way, the\nL243@P3: parts of Yang [49] that deal with research lines 1 and 3 above (resp. generalizing Neal and Jacot\nL244@P3: et al.) (and research line 2 is essentially covered by the combination of [50] and [51]). The main\nL245@P3: 6A coordinatewise nonlinearity is, in the simplest case, a function that is applied to each coordinate of an\nL246@P3: input vector independently. More generally, see Nonlin.\nL247@P3-4: 4technical result of each of [50] and [51] is a Master Theorem that tells one how to mechanically track\nL248@P4: the correlation between vectors computed in a program. The difference between their versions of the\nL249@P4: Master Theorems is in the generality of programs considered: In [50], the Master Theorem applies to\nL250@P4: programs that allow one to re-use matrices, but one cannot use both a matrix and its transpose at the\nL251@P4: same time. For example, it applies to the computation AAv but not to A>Av (both computations\nL252@P4: reuse the matrix A but the latter do so through its transpose A>). The Master Theorem of [51]\nL253@P4: allows A>, but only under some restrictive condition. For example, it 1) allows DA>u where D is a\nL254@P4: diagonal matrix with diagonal given by Av, and u and v are independent standard Gaussian vectors,\nL255@P4: but 2) disallows A>Av.\nL256@P4: Nevertheless, the restrictions of [50] and [51]’s Master Theorems are natural for their respective\nL257@P4: settings. Thus, [50] and [51] are able to generalize research lines 1 and 3 to their most practically\nL258@P4: relevant scenarios (which is more than enough for the purpose of deep learning), but not all practically\nL259@P4: conceivable scenarios (for example, when a weight matrix and its transpose are both used in the\nL260@P4: forward pass of a neural network). Furthermore, their Master Theorems are not enough for proving\nL261@P4: the random matrix results of this paper, which requires machinery that handles programs like A>Av.\nL262@P4: A major contribution of this paper is to prove a Master Theorem for any Tensor Program, without the\nL263@P4: restrictions above7. From this would naturally follow the aforementioned generalizations of [50, 51]\nL264@P4: as well as all of the RMT results in this paper.\nL265@P4: The Tensor Programs Series This paper will complete the rewriting of Yang [49]. Between [50],\nL266@P4: [51], and this paper, most results of Yang [49] are now re-presented in an accessible way. This paper\nL267@P4: also finishes the foundation for the current version of the Tensor Programs machinery, which we will\nL268@P4: rely on crucially for several future papers. We intend this paper to be an authoritative reference for\nL269@P4: the technical details and the formulation of this foundation, going forward.\nL270@P4: Summary of Our Contributions 1. We prove the unrestricted Master Theorem, as explained above\nL271@P4: (Section 2). 2. Give new proofs of the semicircle and Marchenko-Pastur laws, benchmarking our\nL272@P4: theoretical framework against these fundamental mathematical results (Section 3 and Appendix H).\nL273@P4: 3. Prove the Free Independence Principle (FIP) (Section 4). 4. Apply FIP to rigorously compute the\nL274@P4: Jacobian singular value distribution of a randomly initialized NN (Section 5).\nL275@P4: For readers familiar with [50, 51], we also: 5. Generalize the GP (research line 1) and NTK (research\nL276@P4: line 3) results of [50, 51] by allowing the transpose of any weight matrix in the NN forward\nL277@P4: computation (Appendices B and C). 6. Use FIP to give a new proof of how Simple GIA Check allows\nL278@P4: one to assume GIA when computing NTK rigorously (Appendix D).\nL279@P4: Taken together with [50, 51], this paper shows the versatility and power of the Tensor Programs\nL280@P4: technique: To calculate (or show the existence of) some limit, one can just express the quantity of\nL281@P4: concern in a Tensor Program and apply the Master Theorem mechanically.\nL282@P4: 2 NETSOR>: Language for Neural Computation\nL283@P4: There are many versions of languages for Tensor Programs, but we shall focus on one version called\nL284@P4: NETSOR> here. This entire section is summarized in Fig. 1, which we encourage the reader to\nL285@P4: regularly consult over the course of this section. ## Ordinal 1367 message assistant commentary
The orthogonal case does extend with the existing proof. For other angles, a promising route is to control the tails of the backward fields and use those bounds in the stability argument. Payload:
The short-time route is now concrete in [/tmp/two-input-shorttime-route.md](/tmp/two-input-shorttime-route.md).

It derives the exact response recursions

\[
W_0^{(2)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}
=
\xi_{a,k}
+
\sum_{b,s<k}
\mathbb E\!\left[
\frac{\partial H_{a,k}^{(1)}}{\partial\eta_{b,s}}
\right]\delta_{b,s}^{(2)},
\]

\[
(W_0^{(2)})^*\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}
=
\eta_{a,k}
+
\sum_{b,s\le k}
\mathbb E\!\left[
\frac{\partial\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}}{\partial\xi_{b,s}}
\right]H_{b,s}^{(1)},
\]

including the learned rank-one memory corrections. The two Gaussian collections have source-Gram covariances and are independent across orientations. The derivative convention holds all deterministic population coefficients fixed.

Let \(\mathcal A_k\) and \(\mathcal C_k\) be the absolute row sums of the two response coefficient arrays. The first-layer tangent recurrence and Jensen’s inequality give

\[
\mathcal C_k
\le
CT\exp\!\left(
CT(1+\mathcal A_{k-1})+CT^4
\right).
\]

The second-layer tangent recurrence gives

\[
V_k
\le
\max\!\left\{
V_{k-1},
1+C_1T(\mathcal C_k+C_0T)V_{k-1}
\right\},
\qquad
\mathcal A_k\le C_1T V_k.
\]

A deterministic induction closes

\[
\mathcal A_k\le CT,\qquad
\mathcal C_k\le CT,\qquad
V_k\le2
\]

uniformly in the Euler mesh on a fixed \([0,T_0]\).

Every backward field is consequently a Gaussian of variance \(O(T_0^2)\), plus a bounded remainder of size \(O(T_0)\). This proves the missing mesh-uniform Gaussian tail bound.

The proof uses bounded derivatives on the second-layer side; unbounded first-layer tangent coefficients are integrated using the Gaussian exponential moment. No independence across time is required.

This supports the local continuous-time/joint-width theorem through your reference-oracle localization argument. It also establishes uniqueness against arbitrary bounded \(L^2\) solutions, since only the reference solution needs the tail bound.

The remaining limitation is global time: the argument presently proves a fixed positive \(T_0\), not every finite \(T\). Restarting locally is possible with accumulated response bounds, but no estimate yet excludes restart times accumulating at a finite horizon.

- Singular covariance causes no obstruction: alternative coefficient vectors differ by a covariance-null vector, whose corresponding source combination vanishes in \(L^2\).
- The bootstrap is causal: \(\mathcal C_k\) uses only \(\mathcal A_{k-1}\); the second-layer memory uses only earlier \(\delta\)’s, giving (18) with \(V_{k-1}\).
