## Ordinal 1435 custom_tool_call  
exec const main = String.raw`# Two inputs: a rigorous local-time joint width and gradient-flow limit

## Result and scope

Fix two inputs \(x_1,x_2\in\mathbb R^d\), with
\[
\frac{\|x_a\|_2^2}{d}=1,\qquad
\rho=\frac{x_1^\top x_2}{d}\in(-1,1),\qquad
G=\begin{pmatrix}1&\rho\\\rho&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Let \(y_1,y_2\in\{-1,1\}\). There is \(T_0>0\) such that the ordinary, layer-scaled full-batch GD specified below has a unique autonomous population limit on \([0,T_0]\), for every sequence \(\eta_n\to0\). In particular this includes the earlier regime \(\eta_n\sqrt n\to0\) and the original choice \(n^{-2}\). The proof works for all the stated angles and both label patterns.

Predictions, residuals, loss, and all entries of all three kernel blocks converge in probability uniformly on this interval. The joint two-input hidden path laws converge with their second moments in the uniform path norm. Integrated squared hidden velocities converge. The joint empirical averages of any fixed finite collection of continuous, globally Lipschitz forward/adjoint probes, with bounded products when a product instruction is used, converge to their corresponding population expectations, uniformly in time. Unbounded first-layer backward fields and their quadratic contractions are included by the tail argument below.

On a possibly smaller interval \((0,T_*]\), each input's preactivations move in both hidden layers, all three \(2\times2\) kernel blocks are positive definite, the total kernel is nonconstant, loss strictly decreases, and \(\arctan\) has positive best-affine-fit error on each hidden preactivation distribution.

**Scope:** for nonorthogonal inputs this proof establishes a fixed positive time interval, not convergence on every compact time interval. The latter remains unproved here. When \(\rho=0\), the earlier cubic-coordinate argument does extend to every finite time interval. No optimizer preconditioning is being introduced.

## The finite network and its exact updates

The dimensions are \(W^{(1)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times d}\), \(W^{(2)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}\), and \(W^{(3)}\in\mathbb R^n\). For input \(a=1,2\), use
\[
z_a^{(1)}=\frac{W^{(1)}x_a}{\sqrt d},\quad
h_a^{(1)}=\phi(z_a^{(1)}),\quad
z_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)},\quad
h_a^{(2)}=\phi(z_a^{(2)}),\qquad \phi=\arctan,
\]
\[
f_{n,a}=\frac{(W^{(3)})^\top h_a^{(2)}}n,\qquad
r_{n,a}=f_{n,a}-y_a,\qquad L_n=r_{n,1}^2+r_{n,2}^2.
\]
The summed loss retains the earlier factors of two; using the average loss halves the speed.

All initialization entries are independent:
\[
W_{0,ij}^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),\quad
W_{0,ji}^{(2)}\sim N(0,1/n),\quad
W_{0,j}^{(3)}\sim N(0,1/n^2).
\]
Consequently each first-layer root pair is \(N(0,G)\), independently over neuron index \(i\). The two preactivations for one neuron are not independent unless \(\rho=0\).

Set
\[
\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\odot\phi'(z_a^{(2)}),\qquad
\delta_a^{(1)}=\phi'(z_a^{(1)})\odot(W^{(2)})^\top\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]
Thus \(\delta_a^{(\ell)}=n\,\partial f_{n,a}/\partial z_a^{(\ell)}\).
In the displayed rescaled parameterization the layer learning-rate multipliers are \(n,1,n\). The exact first-weight update is
\[
W_{k+1}^{(1)}
=W_k^{(1)}-\frac{2\eta_n}{\sqrt d}
\sum_{b=1}^2r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(1)}x_b^\top.
\]
Multiplication by \(x_a/\sqrt d\) gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
z_{a,k+1}^{(1)}
&=z_{a,k}^{(1)}
-2\eta_n\sum_bG_{ab}r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(1)},\\
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
&=W_k^{(2)}-\frac{2\eta_n}{n}
\sum_b r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}(h_{b,k}^{(1)})^\top,\\
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
&=W_k^{(3)}-2\eta_n\sum_b r_{n,b,k}h_{b,k}^{(2)} .
\end{aligned}\tag{M1}
\]
Use physical time \(t=k\eta_n\), linearly interpolate these parameters, and recompute the forward pass at intermediate times.

The whole effect of the input geometry on first-layer training is the matrix \(G\). For example, the update of \(z_1^{(1)}\) contains its own gradient and \(\rho\) times input 2's gradient.

The finite continuous flow has the kernel blocks
\[
K^{(1)}_{n,ab}
=G_{ab}\frac{(\delta_a^{(1)})^\top\delta_b^{(1)}}n,\quad
K^{(2)}_{n,ab}
=\frac{(h_a^{(1)})^\top h_b^{(1)}}n
 \frac{(\delta_a^{(2)})^\top\delta_b^{(2)}}n,\quad
K^{(3)}_{n,ab}
=\frac{(h_a^{(2)})^\top h_b^{(2)}}n .
\tag{M2}
\]
Differentiation gives \(\dot f_n=-2K_n r_n\) and \(\dot L_n=-4r_n^\top K_n r_n\), where \(K_n\) is the sum of the blocks. Each block is positive semidefinite: it is the Gram matrix of the corresponding scaled parameter gradients. Individual residual magnitudes need not decrease; their squared sum does.

## Why a new stability estimate is needed

With \(F(s)=s+s^3/3\), the componentwise transformation \(X_a^{(1)}=F(Z_a^{(1)})\) gives
\[
\dot X_a^{(1)}
=-2\sum_bG_{ab}r_b
\frac{1+(Z_a^{(1)})^2}{1+(Z_b^{(1)})^2}
(W^{(2)})^*\delta_b^{(2)}.
\tag{M3}
\]
For \(\rho=0\) the ratios cancel. For \(\rho\ne0\) they do not, and are unbounded. We therefore work with the original preactivations and prove a uniform tail estimate for the backward fields.

The exact limiting equations will be
\[
H_a^{(1)}=\phi(Z_a^{(1)}),\quad
Z_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}H_a^{(1)},\quad
H_a^{(2)}=\phi(Z_a^{(2)}),\quad
f_a=\mathbb E[W^{(3)}H_a^{(2)}],\quad r_a=f_a-y_a,
\]
\[
\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\phi'(Z_a^{(2)}),\qquad
\delta_a^{(1)}=\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\delta_a^{(2)},
\]
\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot Z_a^{(1)}&=-2\sum_bG_{ab}r_b\delta_b^{(1)},\\
\dot W^{(2)}&=-2\sum_b r_b\delta_b^{(2)}\otimes H_b^{(1)},\\
\dot W^{(3)}&=-2\sum_b r_bH_b^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}\tag{M4}
\]
Here \((u\otimes v)V=u\,\mathbb E[vV]\). This definition displays the normalization that replaces the finite factor \(1/n\). First-layer and second-layer coordinates belong to separate populations. \(W^{(2)}\) acts from the former to the latter, and its adjoint acts in reverse. Every expectation pairs coordinates of the same population.

The initial root pair is \(N(0,G)\), the population readout is zero, and the initial action \(W_0^{(2)}\) is the joint forward-and-transpose limit of the initial Gaussian matrix, constructed next. The state consists of two first-layer fields and the shared matrix action and readout field. It has finitely many kinds of state objects, not finitely many scalar degrees of freedom.

## The fixed-mesh construction and the new tail lemma

We use the following finite Gaussian fact. For a fixed finite calculation from independent Gaussian roots and a Gaussian matrix of variance \(1/n\), with smooth polynomially bounded coordinate functions and finitely many calls to that matrix and its transpose, every same-layer joint empirical average of a continuous polynomially bounded measurement has a deterministic limit. The limiting action is Gaussian randomness plus the responses forced by earlier opposite-direction calls. The precise response coefficients and their derivative convention are given and used in the appendix below. This is the smooth specialization of Theorem 2.10 and Box 1 of [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685). Its hypotheses hold here: after fixing population scalar coefficients, each instruction and each derivative needed below is a polynomially bounded function of finitely many Gaussian variables. No claim of iid finite coordinates after matrix reuse is used.

For completeness, the underlying conditioning mechanism is as follows. If previous calls have revealed \(W_0^{(2)}V=Y\) and \((W_0^{(2)})^\top U=R\), then the remaining Gaussian matrix is an independent Gaussian projected onto the directions orthogonal to the columns of \(U\) and \(V\). Its conditional mean obeys both recorded equations. A new product is the response fixed by these equations plus that projected Gaussian applied to the unexplored part of the new input. Gaussian integration by parts identifies the response coefficients. The finite Gaussian theorem makes this induction valid also with dependent query directions. We explicitly rely on this stated finite-program result, not on a claim that the infinite-time or growing-program-length theorem already follows from it.

These finite limits can be realized on two common population coordinate spaces. Here are the details relevant to the subsequent continuous-time argument. Take all the programs needed for rational meshes, their finite unions, and a countable closure under rational linear combinations, the coordinate operations in the proof, and bounded Lipschitz approximations. Their finite joint laws are consistent, so they can be realized jointly. Take the mean-square closure of their linear spans on each layer. Exact finite linear identities pass to these laws.

A fixed sufficiently large \(M\) satisfies
\[
\mathbb P(\|W_0^{(2)}\|_{\rm op}>M)
\le2\,9^{2n}e^{-nM^2/8}\longrightarrow0.
\tag{M5}
\]
Indeed two \(1/4\)-nets of the unit sphere each have at most \(9^n\) points, and every fixed bilinear form of the matrix is \(N(0,1/n)\). This finite operator inequality and joint second-moment convergence imply
\[
\mathbb E[(W_0^{(2)}V)^2]\le M^2\mathbb E[V^2]
\]
for every generated \(V\). Thus the action is well-defined on equal-in-mean-square variables and extends to the closure. The same reasoning applies to the transpose. The exact finite identity
\[
\frac{u^\top W_0^{(2)}v}{n}
=\frac{((W_0^{(2)})^\top u)^\top v}{n}
\]
passes to the limit and identifies the two actions as adjoints. Bounded coordinate multipliers and their required products belong to these spaces by clipping and mean-square approximation. This constructs the common bounded-operator realization used below; it is not an additional assumption.

Define population Euler for (M4) in this common realization, starting from the stated initialization. Its rank expansion is
\[
W_k^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}
-2\Delta\sum_{s<k}\sum_b
r_{b,s}\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}\otimes H_{b,s}^{(1)}.
\tag{M6}
\]
Thus each fixed mesh is a finite Gaussian calculation with deterministic population residuals and contractions. Iterative construction is causal: the current fields determine their expectations and then the next fields. Its realization agrees with the finite Gaussian law, because applying the same rank expansion reduces each action to an initial-matrix call and those deterministic contractions.

The appendix proves the essential new estimate:
\[
\sup_{\Delta:\,\Delta\le T_0}\ 
\max_{a,k:\,k\Delta\le T_0}
\mathbb E\exp\!\left(
c\left|(W_k^{(2)})^*\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\right|^2
\right)\le C .
\tag{M7}
\]
Here \(T_0,c,C>0\) do not depend on the mesh. In particular the squared tail beyond \(R\) is at most \(C e^{-c'R^2}\), after reducing \(c'\).

The appendix is a derivation, not an assumed light-tail condition. It controls the accumulated deterministic response coefficients on both layers. The second-layer derivatives stay bounded; the first-layer derivatives have a Gaussian exponential bound. A short-time induction closes these bounds uniformly in the number of Euler steps.

## Population Euler converges to a unique flow

For comparing two population states use
\[
d=\sum_{a=1}^2
\bigl(\mathbb E|Z_a^{(1)}-\widetilde Z_a^{(1)}|^2\bigr)^{1/2}
+\|W^{(2)}-\widetilde W^{(2)}\|_{\rm op}
+\bigl(\mathbb E|W^{(3)}-\widetilde W^{(3)}|^2\bigr)^{1/2}.
\tag{M8}
\]
At finite width replace each mean-square norm by the Euclidean norm divided by \(\sqrt n\).

All Euler trajectories, and all the actual discrete trajectories with \(\eta_n\le1\), have bounds independent of width on a fixed bounded time interval, whenever the initial readout supremum and matrix operator norm are bounded. To see this, boundedness of \(\phi\) gives
\[
|r_a|\le 1+\frac{\pi}{2}\|W^{(3)}\|_\infty,\qquad
\|W^{(3)}_{k+1}\|_\infty
\le\|W^{(3)}_k\|_\infty+
C\eta_n(1+\|W^{(3)}_k\|_\infty).
\]
The geometric sum bounds the readout supremum. The rank-one update then bounds the matrix operator norm, and the first-layer update bounds the root-mean-square velocity and displacement. The same calculation applies to population Euler with \(\Delta\). All initial bounds hold with probability tending to one for the finite initialization.

On these bounded sets, the forward quantities, residuals, second-layer backward fields, and the maps
\[
(Z_1^{(1)},Z_2^{(1)},W^{(2)},W^{(3)})
\longmapsto (W^{(2)})^*\delta_a^{(2)}
\]
are Lipschitz in (M8). This follows by adding and subtracting one factor at a time, using boundedness and Lipschitz continuity of \(\phi,\phi'\), the readout supremum bound, and the operator bound.

Only the first-layer multiplication needs a separate estimate. Write
\[
P_a=(W^{(2)})^*\delta_a^{(2)},\qquad
\widetilde P_a=(\widetilde W^{(2)})^*\widetilde\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]
Then
\[
\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})P_a-\phi'(\widetilde Z_a^{(1)})\widetilde P_a
=\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})(P_a-\widetilde P_a)
+[\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})-\phi'(\widetilde Z_a^{(1)})]\widetilde P_a.
\]
Split the last term at \(|\widetilde P_a|=R\). Since \(\phi'\) is bounded and Lipschitz,
\[
\|[\phi'(Z)-\phi'(\widetilde Z)]\widetilde P\|_{L^2}
\le C R\|Z-\widetilde Z\|_{L^2}
+C\|\widetilde P\,1_{\{|\widetilde P|>R\}}\|_{L^2}.
\tag{M9}
\]
Here \(\|V\|_{L^2}=(\mathbb E V^2)^{1/2}\). Only the reference field needs a tail bound. For a reference Euler grid point, (M7) bounds the final term by \(C e^{-cR^2}\).

An Euler interpolant moves by at most \(C\Delta\) in (M8) between grid points. Its backward field therefore differs from the grid field by at most \(C\Delta\) in mean square, using the Lipschitz map above. Thus, at any intermediate time, the vector field differs from the interpolant's assigned grid velocity by at most
\[
C(1+R)\Delta+C e^{-cR^2}.
\tag{M10}
\]
The same localization gives the comparison inequality for two meshes. Integration and the elementary Gronwall inequality yield, for \(0<T\le T_0\),
\[
\sup_{t\le T}d(\theta^\Delta(t),\theta^{\Delta'}(t))
\le
C e^{C(1+R)T}
\left((1+R)(\Delta+\Delta')+e^{-cR^2}\right).
\tag{M11}
\]
First send both meshes to zero with \(R\) fixed. Then send \(R\) to infinity. The term \(e^{C(1+R)T-cR^2}\) vanishes, so the Euler paths are Cauchy uniformly in time in (M8).

The state spaces are complete, and the limit keeps the readout supremum and operator bounds. The coordinate product \((Z,P)\mapsto\phi'(Z)P\) is continuous from \(L^2\times L^2\) to \(L^2\): split off the change of \(P\), then use boundedness of \(\phi'\) and dominated convergence against the fixed limiting \(P^2\). This allows passage to the integral form of (M4). The limit is therefore a solution, not just a limit of observables. It inherits (M7) by convergence in probability and Fatou's inequality; at intermediate grid times use the \(O(\Delta)\) backward-field difference.

The same estimate compares this solution with any other solution of (M4) in the bounded state class with the same initial state. Only the constructed reference solution requires (M7). Their error is bounded by \(C e^{C(1+R)T-cR^2}\) for every \(R\), hence is zero. This proves uniqueness.

The equations use only the current fields and the current action and adjoint. In particular, their future evolution does not require the stored Euler history. More formally, the closed spaces generated by current fields, bounded coordinate operations, and current forward/adjoint actions contain the future: clipped vector fields and their Euler approximations remain there, and (M9) removes the clipping. Equal current joint action laws identify these generated spaces by a mean-square isometry, which intertwines their actions. Uniqueness therefore identifies their future action laws. This proves autonomy and restartability along the constructed interval.

## From the population flow to the actual fine-step GD

Fix a coarse mesh \(\Delta\), independently of the actual step \(\eta_n\). Build a finite oracle by expanding (M6), supplying it with the population residuals and every population contraction in these expansions. It starts from the same finite first-layer initialization and the same \(W_0^{(2)}\) as GD, but has \(W_0^{(3)}=0\). Every oracle instruction now has deterministic scalar coefficients. The fixed finite Gaussian theorem applies jointly to all its nodes and proves convergence of all the needed empirical contractions and second moments.

There is a useful distinction between oracle nodes and an actual finite network state. Construct proxy parameter arrays using its first-layer nodes and readout, and the matrix
\[
W_{k,\mathrm{proxy}}^{(2)}
=W_0^{(2)}-\frac{2\Delta}{n}
\sum_{s<k,b}r_{b,s}\delta_{b,s,\mathrm{oracle}}^{(2)}
(h_{b,s,\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)})^\top.
\tag{M12}
\]
Applying this proxy matrix to a current oracle activation differs from its prescribed oracle preactivation only by sums of empirical-minus-population contractions. For example each forward discrepancy has the form
\[
-2\Delta r_{b,s}\delta_{b,s,\mathrm{oracle}}^{(2)}
\left(
\frac{(h_{b,s,\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)})^\top
h_{a,k,\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)}}{n}
-\mathbb E[H_{b,s}^{(1)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}]
\right).
\tag{M13}
\]
The transpose discrepancy has the corresponding contraction of two \(\delta^{(2)}\) fields. At a fixed mesh there are finitely many such terms. Their scalar errors tend to zero, and their vector norms stay bounded. The actual network quantities recomputed from the proxy arrays therefore differ from the oracle nodes by \(o_{\mathbb P}(1)\) in root mean square. Bounded readout and the operator bound justify each subsequent forward/backward comparison. In particular its recomputed vector field differs from its prescribed oracle grid velocity by \(o_{\mathbb P}(1)\).

For each fixed threshold \(R\), the oracle's empirical squared tails converge to their population counterparts. One may use continuous upper cutoffs, equal to one beyond \(R\) and zero below \(R-1\), to avoid discontinuities. The fixed finite Gaussian theorem applies to these quadratic-growth measurements. Consequently the root-mean-square tail of the recomputed proxy backward field has limit superior at most \(C e^{-cR^2}\), after modifying constants. This is proved for the reference oracle, not assumed for actual GD.

Linearly interpolate the proxy parameters. Their metric speed is bounded in probability, with limiting bound independent of the mesh, by (M4) and the node consistency just proved. Their between-grid vector-field defect is bounded by (M10), plus a fixed-mesh \(o_{\mathbb P}(1)\). Compare the actual GD interpolant to this proxy. Actual GD's derivative is its vector field at the preceding fine grid point; its state changes by \(O(\eta_n)\) inside a fine step. Apply (M9) with the proxy as reference. No tail bound for the actual GD is needed. Gronwall gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\sup_{t\le T}d_n(\theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),\theta_n^{\mathrm{proxy},\Delta}(t))
\le C e^{C(1+R)T}
\bigg(
&\frac{\|W_{n,0}^{(3)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
+(1+R)(\eta_n+\Delta)\\
&+e^{-cR^2}+o_{\mathbb P}(1)
\bigg).
\end{aligned}\tag{M14}
\]
The \(o_{\mathbb P}(1)\) here is at fixed \(R,\Delta\). Bounds on the actual readout supremum are obtained from its update, not from root-mean-square closeness.

The initialization discrepancy vanishes, and \(\eta_n\to0\). First take \(n\to\infty\) at fixed \(R,\Delta\); next take \(\Delta\to0\); finally take \(R\to\infty\). Together with (M11), the fixed-mesh oracle empirical convergence, and the triangle inequality for observables, this proves the claimed joint limit. Thus this local proof needs no width-dependent learning-rate restriction beyond \(\eta_n\to0\).

For the first kernel block, use (M9) once more to transfer the first-layer \(\delta^{(1)}\) fields in mean square; the other blocks follow directly from the bounded/Lipschitz maps. Products and contractions then converge by Cauchy–Schwarz. For hidden velocities,
\[
\dot z_a^{(2)}
=\dot W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)}
+W^{(2)}\bigl[\phi'(z_a^{(1)})\odot\dot z_a^{(1)}\bigr].
\]
The reference first-layer velocity is a finite linear combination of bounded multiples of the backward fields, hence has the same tail control. Applying the same comparison proves convergence of both hidden velocities in integrated mean square and hence of their integrated squared norms.

To obtain path-law convergence, first use the finite Gaussian theorem on any fixed finite time grid. Between two times separated by at most \(\varepsilon\), any absolutely continuous coordinate path satisfies
\[
\sup_{s,t\ \mathrm{in\ one\ grid\ cell}}|z(t)-z(s)|^2
\le\varepsilon\int_{\mathrm{cell}}|\dot z(u)|^2\,du.
\]
The proved mean-energy bounds therefore approximate empirical and population path laws, in squared uniform-path distance, by their piecewise linear grid reconstructions with error at most \(C\varepsilon\). Letting the grid become fine gives the joint two-input path-law assertion. The same finite-grid approximation and (M9) give uniform-in-time convergence of the stated fixed action probes.

## The limit is genuinely feature-learning for both label patterns

The preceding construction proves (M4). We now check its small-time behavior without assuming that its hidden features move.

Let
\[
Q_{ab}=\mathbb E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}].
\]
The first-layer root pair has full Gaussian support because \(|\rho|<1\). No nonzero linear combination of its two arctangents vanishes identically, so \(Q\) is positive definite. The initial second-layer pair \((Z_{0,1}^{(2)},Z_{0,2}^{(2)})\) is centered Gaussian with covariance \(Q\).

The following temporary names recur in the expansion:
\[
S=\sum_a y_aH_{0,a}^{(2)},\quad
U_a=S\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(2)}),\quad
P_a=(W_0^{(2)})^*U_a,\quad
B_a=\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})P_a,
\]
\[
V_{ab}=\mathbb E[U_aU_b],\qquad D_{ab}=\mathbb E[B_aB_b].
\tag{M15}
\]
The matrix \(V\) is positive definite. Indeed \(S=0\) only on the line \(Z_{0,2}^{(2)}=-y_1y_2Z_{0,1}^{(2)}\), a probability-zero event. If \(c_1U_1+c_2U_2=0\), full support and continuity force \(c_1\phi'(s)+c_2\phi'(t)=0\) for every \(s,t\), hence \(c_1=c_2=0\).

Conditioning on the two initial forward calls gives
\[
P_a=\sum_cH_{0,c}^{(1)}
\left[
Q^{-1}\mathbb E
\begin{pmatrix}
Z_{0,1}^{(2)}U_a\\ Z_{0,2}^{(2)}U_a
\end{pmatrix}
\right]_c+\Gamma_a,
\tag{M16}
\]
where \((\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)\sim N(0,V)\) is independent of the first-layer roots. This is also the first transpose instance of the appendix response rule, using Gaussian integration by parts. Since both \(\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})\) are strictly positive, the conditional covariance of \((B_1,B_2)\) is positive definite, so \(D\) is positive definite.

Define
\[
T_a=\sum_bG_{ab}y_bB_b,\qquad
R_a=\sum_b y_bQ_{ab}U_b+
W_0^{(2)}[\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a].
\tag{M17}
\]
Substitute \(r_a(0)=-y_a\), \(W^{(3)}(0)=0\) into the integral equations. Mean-square continuity and boundedness of \(\phi,\phi'\) give
\[
W^{(3)}(t)=2tS+o_{L^2}(t),\qquad
\delta_a^{(2)}(t)=2tU_a+o_{L^2}(t),
\]
\[
Z_a^{(1)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(1)}
=2t^2T_a+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]
\[
W^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}
=2t^2\sum_b y_bU_b\otimes H_{0,b}^{(1)}
+o_{\rm op}(t^2),
\]
\[
Z_a^{(2)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(2)}
=2t^2R_a+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\tag{M18}
\]
For example, dividing the readout integral by \(t\) gives \(2S\); dividing the backward field by \(t\) then gives \(2U_a\). The first-layer and matrix integrals therefore start at order \(t^2\). In the final line, the first term in \(R_a\) is the learned-matrix contribution and the second is the moving first-layer activation contribution. Differentiating the bounded smooth activation along these converging mean-square increments is justified by dominated convergence; no operator-norm differentiability of a nonlinear coordinate map on all of \(L^2\) is assumed.

Set
\[
A_1=\sum_{a,b}y_a y_bG_{ab}D_{ab}>0,\qquad
A_2=\sum_{a,b}y_a y_bQ_{ab}V_{ab}>0 .
\tag{M19}
\]
Positivity follows, for example, from
\(A_1=\mathbb E[(y_1B_1,y_2B_2)G(y_1B_1,y_2B_2)^\top]\)
and positive definiteness of \(G,D\). The analogous argument uses \(Q,V\).
Each \(T_a\) is nonzero in mean square, by the positive conditional covariance in (M16). Adjunction gives
\[
\sum_a y_a\mathbb E[U_aR_a]=A_1+A_2>0.
\tag{M20}
\]
In fact each \(R_a\) is nonzero. Swapping the two inputs preserves the initial action law, sends \(U_a\) to \(y_1y_2U_{3-a}\), and sends \(R_a\) to \(R_{3-a}\). Thus the two summands on the left of (M20) are equal, each \((A_1+A_2)/2>0\).

The limiting kernels are (M2) with normalized finite dot products replaced by same-layer expectations. Consequently
\[
K^{(1)}_{ab}(t)=4t^2G_{ab}D_{ab}+o(t^2),\qquad
K^{(2)}_{ab}(t)=4t^2Q_{ab}V_{ab}+o(t^2),
\]
\[
y^\top K(t)y
=y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y+8(A_1+A_2)t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{M21}
\]
For the last equality, expansion of \(H_a^{(2)}(t)\) in (M18) gives a readout-block increase \(4(A_1+A_2)t^2\); the other two blocks supply the same increase. Both matrices with entries \(G_{ab}D_{ab}\) and \(Q_{ab}V_{ab}\) are positive definite. For example, for nonzero \(c\),
\[
\sum_{a,b}c_ac_bG_{ab}D_{ab}
\ge\lambda_{\min}(G)\sum_a c_a^2D_{aa}>0.
\]
The initial readout block is positive definite by full support of the second-layer Gaussian pair. This proves positivity of all blocks at sufficiently small positive times and nonconstancy of the total kernel.

Finally,
\[
L(0)=2,\qquad \dot L(0)=-4y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y<0 .
\]
The expansions (M18), and their velocity versions obtained directly from (M4), give strictly positive squared displacements and integrated squared speeds for both inputs at both hidden layers. Initial preactivation variances are positive. The error of the best affine approximation to \(\arctan\) is initially positive on each nondegenerate Gaussian marginal. Its formula in terms of variances and covariance, and mean-square continuity, preserve positivity for small time.

This finishes the stated local-time theorem. The response bound in the appendix is deliberately local. It does not establish a bound on every finite horizon, so an arbitrary-angle all-compact-time theorem is not claimed.

- The TP III `ZHat`, `ZDot`, and symbolic-partial rules support (5)–(8). Freezing residuals, contractions, and response coefficients is correct.
- Same-step inputs cause no missing response: \(A_{ak,bk}=\mathbf1_{a=b}\mathbb E[W_k^{(3)}\phi''(Z_{a,k}^{(2)})]\), since the readout and memory involve earlier times.
- Singular covariance does not invalidate symbolic differentiation or the response representation. Pseudoinverse ambiguity lies in the Gram nullspace and annihilates the corresponding source combination. In particular, zero-variance \(\eta_{a,0}\) slots can have nonzero symbolic derivatives; retaining them is correct.
- (13)–(14) correctly control the first-layer derivative row sums. Jensen in (15) is valid without independence: average the \(2k\) terms and use \(\mathbb E e^{\lambda|N(0,\sigma^2)|}\le2e^{\lambda^2\sigma^2/2}\).

The continuum argument is mathematically viable. On a common realization with \(\|W_0^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le K\), the updates give \(\|W_k^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le K+CT^2\). Every remaining drift term is Lipschitz on the indicated bounded sets; the exceptional first-layer multiplication has exactly the modulus in (22). Its reciprocal is nonintegrable at zero, so one reference solution with Gaussian tails suffices for uniqueness.

1. State or cite a lemma coupling different meshes on the same layer probability spaces with the same bounded operator and adjoint. Separate Gaussian constructions for individual meshes do not themselves provide this.
2. When comparing interpolated Euler paths before obtaining a limit, apply (22) to their grid-point fields. Their grid-point state distance is at most the interpolated distance plus \(C(\Delta+\widetilde\Delta)\). This yields the stated vanishing defect and Osgood Cauchy argument without claiming exponential tails for interpolated fields.
3. Specify the initial-matrix normalization underlying (3), (5), and (6).

No algebraic repair to (13)–(21) is needed. Neither global continuation nor the finite-width transfer is proved here; the note correctly flags both limitations.

- The coefficient \(8(A_1+A_2)\) in (M21) is correct: the readout block contributes \(4(A_1+A_2)t^2\), and the other blocks together contribute the same amount. Positive definiteness holds for every fixed \(|\rho|<1\); no uniformity as \(|\rho|\uparrow1\) is required.
- The appendix bootstrap closes: (16) uses \(\mathcal A_{k-1}\), (18) uses only preceding second-layer derivatives, and (19) preserves \(V_k\le2\). This gives the claimed mesh-independent Gaussian tail bound.
- The reference-tail localization supports both population uniqueness and the comparison with actual GD. The proxy construction accounts for empirical versus population contractions before applying that comparison. The limit order in (M14) consequently permits arbitrary \(\eta_n\to0\).
- The bounded-operator construction, inheritance of the tail estimate, and action-law formulation support uniqueness and restartability on the constructed interval. The energy argument supports the asserted path-law and integrated-speed convergence.

The fixed-program Gaussian theorem and response formulas are supported by Theorem 2.10, Box 1, and Remarks 2.11–2.12 of [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685).

No required corrections. This verdict concerns the proved local theorem; it does not certify the contextual reference to an earlier global orthogonal-input argument.

## Ordinal 1531 agent_message /root/two_input_full_audit_a 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/two_input_full_audit_a
Payload:
PASS for the stated local-time theorem. I found no substantive gap in the normalization, common-space construction, mesh-uniform tail estimate, actual-GD transfer, or uniqueness argument.

The key checks are:

- **Normalization:** Differentiating the summed loss with layer multipliers \(n,1,n\) gives exactly (M1). The normalized rank-one action in (M4) matches the finite \(1/n\) factor. The three blocks in (M2) give \(\dot f=-2Kr\) and \(\dot L=-4r^\top Kr\).

- **Finite-program theorem:** The cited result permits matrix reuse and transpose calls without an additional rank-convergence assumption. Its symbolic derivative convention supports appendix (4)–(8), including singular Gaussian covariances. Fixed program length and polynomial growth of the recursions and their derivatives are sufficient here. [Tensor Programs III, Theorem 2.10, Box 1 and Remarks 2.11–2.12](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685)

- **Common bounded operator:** Finite unions give consistent joint coordinate laws. Applying the high-probability finite operator bound before passing to deterministic second-moment limits gives the required inequality on every generated vector. Rational linear combinations, completion, and the finite adjunction identity then produce a bounded linear operator and its adjoint. This does not require operator-norm convergence of the random finite matrices.

- **Tail appendix:** The bootstrap is causal. In particular, \(\mathcal C_k\) uses \(\mathcal A_{k-1}\); the forward-memory expression then bounds \(V_k\), which bounds \(\mathcal A_k\). Equation (18) avoids accumulation proportional to the number of steps. Jensen’s inequality in (15) needs no temporal independence. Consequently (21) supplies the uniform reference tails used later.

- **Actual-GD transfer:** Proxy first-layer coordinates and readout equal their oracle nodes. Thus recomputation first changes the forward matrix actions by contraction errors, then changes the adjoint fields through bounded operators; no unsupported multiplication of two merely \(L^2\)-close unbounded fields is needed. For (M14), one can directly compare the actual vector field at its preceding fine-grid point with the recomputed proxy vector field at its preceding coarse-grid point. Their state distance is bounded by the current distance plus \(C(\eta_n+\Delta)\). Applying (M9) with that proxy grid point as reference proves the stated estimate without actual-GD tail assumptions.

- **Arbitrary \(\eta_n\to0\):** The finite-program error is used only after fixing \(R,\Delta\). Sending \(n\to\infty\), then \(\Delta\to0\), then \(R\to\infty\) is valid. The exponential tail beats the Gronwall factor, so no hidden width-dependent step restriction enters.

- **Continuum and observables:** The metric completion, continuity of the bounded activation multiplier against a fixed \(L^2\) field, and inherited reference tails establish existence and uniqueness. The hidden-velocity transfer supplies the energy control needed for convergence in the uniform path norm with second moments.

I also checked (M16)–(M21): the Gaussian remainder covariance is \(V\), the input-swap argument works for both label patterns, and the coefficient \(8(A_1+A_2)\) is correct.

The revised sentence about the earlier orthogonal all-time argument is contextual; that earlier argument is not supplied here and was not independently certified. No correction is needed for the local theorem audited here.

## Ordinal 1558 message assistant final_answer
**Yes, on a fixed nonzero time interval, for every angle you specified and for either label pattern.** There is one important qualification: I have not extended the earlier *every finite time horizon* conclusion to nonorthogonal inputs. The result proved here is local in time.

The [complete proof](/tmp/TWO_INPUT_LOCAL_LIMIT_PROOF.md) passed three fresh, isolated adversarial reviews, following two independent checks of its main new lemma. The [review record](/tmp/TWO_INPUT_LOCAL_LIMIT_REVIEW.md) states their scope.

Here is the result and the mathematical argument that makes the extension work.

## The two-input system

Let the inputs be \(x_1,x_2\in\mathbb R^d\), with

\[
\frac{\|x_1\|_2^2}{d}
=
\frac{\|x_2\|_2^2}{d}
=1,
\qquad
\rho=\frac{x_1^\top x_2}{d}\in(-1,1).
\]

Their normalized dot products form the matrix

\[
G=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&\rho\\
\rho&1
\end{pmatrix}.
\]

Let \(y_1,y_2\in\{-1,1\}\). The two inputs use the **same weights**, but produce different preactivations:

\[
z_a^{(1)}=\frac{W^{(1)}x_a}{\sqrt d},
\qquad
h_a^{(1)}=\phi(z_a^{(1)}),
\qquad
z_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)},
\qquad
h_a^{(2)}=\phi(z_a^{(2)}),
\]

where \(a\in\{1,2\}\) and \(\phi=\arctan\). Here \(W^{(1)}\) is \(n\times d\), \(W^{(2)}\) is \(n\times n\), and \(W^{(3)}\) has \(n\) coordinates.

Use

\[
f_{n,a}
=
\frac1n(W^{(3)})^\top h_a^{(2)},
\qquad
r_{n,a}=f_{n,a}-y_a,
\qquad
L_n=r_{n,1}^2+r_{n,2}^2.
\]

I use the **sum** of the two squared errors to retain the previous factors of \(2\). Averaging the loss would simply halve the speed.

Initialize independently by

\[
W_{0,ij}^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),
\qquad
W_{0,ji}^{(2)}\sim N(0,1/n),
\qquad
W_{0,j}^{(3)}\sim N(0,1/n^2).
\]

Consequently, each first-layer neuron begins with a jointly Gaussian pair:

\[
\begin{pmatrix}
z_{1,0,i}^{(1)}\\
z_{2,0,i}^{(1)}
\end{pmatrix}
\sim N(0,G).
\]

These pairs are independent across neurons. Within one pair, the correlation is precisely the input correlation \(\rho\).

As before, define backpropagation without the residual:

\[
\delta_a^{(2)}
=
W^{(3)}\odot\phi'(z_a^{(2)}),
\]

\[
\delta_a^{(1)}
=
\phi'(z_a^{(1)})
\odot
(W^{(2)})^\top\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]

With the same layer scaling as the one-input system, exact GD becomes

\[
\begin{aligned}
z_{a,k+1}^{(1)}
&=
z_{a,k}^{(1)}
-
2\eta_n\sum_{b=1}^2
G_{ab}r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(1)},\\
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
&=
W_k^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\eta_n}{n}\sum_{b=1}^2
r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}
(h_{b,k}^{(1)})^\top,\\
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
&=
W_k^{(3)}
-
2\eta_n\sum_{b=1}^2
r_{n,b,k}h_{b,k}^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The first equation is the important change. For input 1, it reads

\[
z_{1,k+1}^{(1)}
=
z_{1,k}^{(1)}
-
2\eta_n
\left(
r_{n,1,k}\delta_{1,k}^{(1)}
+
\rho\,r_{n,2,k}\delta_{2,k}^{(1)}
\right).
\]

Training on input 2 moves input 1’s preactivation because their first-layer weights are shared. Their angle determines the amount of this coupling.

## The limiting result

There exists \(T_0>0\) such that, whenever

\[
\eta_n>0,
\qquad
\eta_n\longrightarrow0,
\]

the discrete networks, on the clock \(t=k\eta_n\), converge to a unique autonomous population evolution on \([0,T_0]\).

Thus the local result includes—and actually requires less than—the previous condition \(\eta_n\sqrt n\to0\).

The population state consists of

\[
Z_1^{(1)},\qquad Z_2^{(1)},\qquad W^{(2)},\qquad W^{(3)}.
\]

These are two first-layer fields, one shared matrix action, and one shared readout field. Everything else is computed from them:

\[
H_a^{(1)}=\phi(Z_a^{(1)}),
\qquad
Z_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}H_a^{(1)},
\qquad
H_a^{(2)}=\phi(Z_a^{(2)}),
\]

\[
f_a=\mathbb E[W^{(3)}H_a^{(2)}],
\qquad
r_a=f_a-y_a,
\]

\[
\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\phi'(Z_a^{(2)}),
\qquad
\delta_a^{(1)}
=
\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]

Their evolution is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot Z_a^{(1)}
&=-2\sum_{b=1}^2G_{ab}r_b\delta_b^{(1)},\\
\dot W^{(2)}
&=-2\sum_{b=1}^2r_b\,
\delta_b^{(2)}\otimes H_b^{(1)},\\
\dot W^{(3)}
&=-2\sum_{b=1}^2r_bH_b^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The rank-one notation means

\[
(\delta_b^{(2)}\otimes H_b^{(1)})V
=
\delta_b^{(2)}\,\mathbb E[H_b^{(1)}V].
\]

It is exactly the population counterpart of multiplying by
\(\delta_b^{(2)}(h_b^{(1)})^\top/n\).

The first-layer and second-layer populations remain separate. The operator \(W^{(2)}\) connects them; its adjoint connects them in reverse.

For example, for either hidden layer and every bounded Lipschitz measurement \(\psi\),

\[
\sup_{t\le T_0}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i
\psi\!\left(
z_{1,i}^{(\ell)}(t),
z_{2,i}^{(\ell)}(t)
\right)
-
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
Z_1^{(\ell)}(t),
Z_2^{(\ell)}(t)
\right)
\right|
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability. This retains the **joint behavior of both inputs at the same neuron**, not merely their separate distributions.

The proof also establishes convergence of the forward and adjoint measurements, predictions, losses, kernel blocks, hidden path laws, and integrated squared hidden speeds.

## What makes the proof work

The rank-one expansion and finite-mesh oracle construction survive the addition of a second input. The new difficulty is continuous-time stability.

For one input, setting \(X^{(1)}=F(Z^{(1)})\), where \(F(s)=s+s^3/3\), cancelled the activation derivative. With two inputs, the same calculation gives

\[
\dot X_a^{(1)}
=
-2\sum_bG_{ab}r_b
\frac{1+(Z_a^{(1)})^2}{1+(Z_b^{(1)})^2}
(W^{(2)})^*\delta_b^{(2)}.
\]

The diagonal ratios cancel. The cross-input ratios do not, and they are unbounded. So merely adding an input index to the old stability proof would leave a gap.

The new proof closes that gap by controlling the backward fields

\[
P_{a,k}:=(W_k^{(2)})^*\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}
\]

uniformly as the auxiliary Euler mesh becomes finer.

Here is the mechanism. At a fixed mesh, the initial-matrix transpose response has the form

\[
(W_0^{(2)})^*\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}
=
g_{a,k}^{(1)}
+
\sum_{b=1}^2\sum_{s\le k}
\mathbb E\!\left[
\frac{\partial\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}}
{\partial g_{b,s}^{(2)}}
\right]
H_{b,s}^{(1)}.
\]

The \(g^{(1)}\) and \(g^{(2)}\) variables denote the Gaussian parts of backward and forward matrix calls. The derivatives track the dependence of the current coordinate on earlier Gaussian inputs, holding the supplied population coefficients fixed. This is the fixed-program Gaussian response rule; it does not itself supply a continuous-time limit. [Tensor Programs III, Box 1 and Theorem 2.10](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685)

The new calculation bounds the **total absolute size of these response coefficients**, uniformly in the number of Euler steps.

There are two mutually supporting bounds:

- The second-layer coordinate derivatives are controlled because the activation and its derivatives are bounded, and \(W^{(3)}\) starts at zero.
- The first-layer derivatives involve Gaussian backward fields. Their accumulated growth is controlled using Gaussian exponential moments, without assuming independence across time.

A short-time induction closes both bounds. Including the learned rank-one corrections, it proves

\[
P_{a,k}
=
\text{centered Gaussian}
+
\text{bounded remainder},
\]

with Gaussian variance at most \(CT^2\) and remainder bounded by \(CT\), uniformly over meshes with \(k\Delta\le T\le T_0\). The remainder need not be independent of the Gaussian.

In particular,

\[
\sup_{\Delta}\max_{a,k:\,k\Delta\le T_0}
\mathbb E\exp(c|P_{a,k}|^2)
\le C.
\]

This is a **proved property of the population Euler construction**, not an extra initialization assumption.

Why is this enough? In comparing two first-layer updates, the troublesome difference is

\[
[\phi'(Z)-\phi'(\widetilde Z)]\,\widetilde P.
\]

Split coordinates according to whether \(|\widetilde P|\le R\). On that set, ordinary Lipschitz continuity gives a factor \(R\). Outside it, the Gaussian tail gives

\[
\sqrt{
\mathbb E\left|
[\phi'(Z)-\phi'(\widetilde Z)]\widetilde P
\right|^2
}
\le
CR\sqrt{\mathbb E|Z-\widetilde Z|^2}
+
Ce^{-cR^2}.
\]

Thus a small fraction of large backward coordinates cannot spoil the comparison.

After integrating the difference equations, the resulting error has the form

\[
\text{error}
\le
Ce^{C(1+R)T}
\left[
(1+R)(\eta_n+\Delta)
+
\text{oracle sampling error}
+
e^{-cR^2}
\right],
\]

apart from the vanishing initialization discrepancy.

Now take the limits in this order:

1. Fix \(R,\Delta\) and let \(n\to\infty\). The finite-mesh oracle sampling error vanishes, and \(\eta_n\to0\).
2. Let \(\Delta\to0\).
3. Let \(R\to\infty\).

The last step works because

\[
e^{C(1+R)T}e^{-cR^2}\longrightarrow0.
\]

The same estimate proves that population Euler paths approach a solution of the displayed differential equations, and that two solutions starting from the same state coincide. This establishes both sides of your original objective: **the finite system converges, and the limiting system genuinely determines its own evolution**.

Crucially, the comparison needs the tail bound only for the reference oracle. It does not assume that the actual finite-network coordinates remain iid or have independently established Gaussian tails.

Finally, this limit is genuinely feature-learning. Its kernel is now a \(2\times2\) matrix, with blocks

\[
K_{ab}^{(1)}
=
G_{ab}\mathbb E[\delta_a^{(1)}\delta_b^{(1)}],
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(2)}
=
\mathbb E[H_a^{(1)}H_b^{(1)}]\,
\mathbb E[\delta_a^{(2)}\delta_b^{(2)}],
\qquad
K_{ab}^{(3)}
=
\mathbb E[H_a^{(2)}H_b^{(2)}].
\]

Writing \(K=K^{(1)}+K^{(2)}+K^{(3)}\),

\[
\dot r=-2Kr,
\qquad
\dot L=-4r^\top Kr.
\]

The proof establishes, for each input and each hidden layer,

\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
\bigl(Z_a^{(\ell)}(t)-Z_a^{(\ell)}(0)\bigr)^2
\right]
=
c_{\ell,a}t^4+o(t^4),
\qquad c_{\ell,a}>0,
\]

and

\[
y^\top K(t)y
=
y^\top K(0)y+ct^2+o(t^2),
\qquad c>0.
\]

So both hidden layers move for both inputs, and the kernel changes. These strict inequalities hold for every \(|\rho|<1\), whether the labels agree or oppose.

The remaining unproved extension is **global time for nonorthogonal inputs**. The argument above proves the complete autonomous joint limit and nonlinear feature learning on a positive time interval; it does not silently promote that local theorem to every finite horizon.

This follows by adding and subtracting one factor at a time, using boundedness and Lipschitz continuity of \\\\(\\\\phi,\\\\phi\'\\\\), the readout supremum bound, and the operator bound.\\n\\nOnly the first-layer multiplication needs a separate estimate. Write\\n\\\\[\\nP_a=(W^{(2)})^*\\\\delta_a^{(2)},\\\\qquad\\n\\\\widetilde P_a=(\\\\widetilde W^{(2)})^*\\\\widetilde\\\\delta_a^{(2)}.\\n\\\\]\\nThen\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\phi\'(Z_a^{(1)})P_a-\\\\phi\'(\\\\widetilde Z_a^{(1)})\\\\widetilde P_a\\n=\\\\phi\'(Z_a^{(1)})(P_a-\\\\widetilde P_a)\\n+[\\\\phi\'(Z_a^{(1)})-\\\\phi\'(\\\\widetilde Z_a^{(1)})]\\\\widetilde P_a.\\n\\\\]\\nSplit the last term at \\\\(|\\\\widetilde P_a|=R\\\\). Since \\\\(\\\\phi\'\\\\) is bounded and Lipschitz,\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\|[\\\\phi\'(Z)-\\\\phi\'(\\\\widetilde Z)]\\\\widetilde P\\\\|_{L^2}\\n\\\\le C R\\\\|Z-\\\\widetilde Z\\\\|_{L^2}\\n+C\\\\|\\\\widetilde P\\\\,1_{\\\\{|\\\\widetilde P|>R\\\\}}\\\\|_{L^2}.\\n\\\\tag{M9}\\n\\\\]\\nHere \\\\(\\\\|V\\\\|_{L^2}=(\\\\mathbb E V^2)^{1/2}\\\\). Only the reference field needs a tail bound. For a reference Euler grid point, (M7) bounds the final term by \\\\(C e^{-cR^2}\\\\).\\n\\nAn Euler interpolant moves by at most \\\\(C\\\\Delta\\\\) in (M8) between grid points. Its backward field therefore differs from the grid field by at most \\\\(C\\\\Delta\\\\) in mean square, using the Lipschitz map above. Thus, at any intermediate time, the vector field differs from the interpolant\'s assigned grid velocity by at most\\n\\\\[\\nC(1+R)\\\\Delta+C e^{-cR^2}.\\n\\\\tag{M10}\\n\\\\]\\nThe same localization gives the comparison inequality for two meshes. Integration and the elementary Gronwall inequality yield, for \\\\(0<T\\\\le T_0\\\\),\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\sup_{t\\\\le T}d(\\\\theta^\\\\Delta(t),\\\\theta^{\\\\Delta\'}(t))\\n\\\\le\\nC e^{C(1+R)T}\\n\\\\left((1+R)(\\\\Delta+\\\\Delta\')+e^{-cR^2}\\\\right).\\n\\\\tag{M11}\\n\\\\]\\nFirst send both meshes to zero with \\\\(R\\\\) fixed. Then send \\\\(R\\\\) to infinity. The term \\\\(e^{C(1+R)T-cR^2}\\\\) vanishes, so the Euler paths are Cauchy uniformly in time in (M8).\\n\\nThe state spaces are complete, and the limit keeps the readout supremum and operator bounds. The coordinate product \\\\((Z,P)\\\\mapsto\\\\phi\'(Z)P\\\\) is continuous from \\\\(L^2\\\\times L^2\\\\) to \\\\(L^2\\\\): split off the change of \\\\(P\\\\), then use boundedness of \\\\(\\\\phi\'\\\\) and dominated convergence against the fixed limiting \\\\(P^2\\\\). This allows passage to the integral form of (M4). The limit is therefore a solution, not just a limit of observables. It inherits (M7) by convergence in probability and Fatou\'s inequality; at intermediate grid times use the \\\\(O(\\\\Delta)\\\\) backward-field difference.\\n\\nThe same estimate compares this solution with any other solution of (M4) in the bounded state class with the same initial state. Only the constructed reference solution requires (M7). Their error is bounded by \\\\(C e^{C(1+R)T-cR^2}\\\\) for every \\\\(R\\\\), hence is zero. This proves uniqueness.\\n\\nThe equations use only the current fields and the current action and adjoint. In particular, their future evolution does not require the stored Euler history. More formally, the closed spaces generated by current fields, bounded coordinate operations, and current forward/adjoint actions contain the future: clipped vector fields and their Euler approximations remain there, and (M9) removes the clipping. Equal current joint action laws identify these generated spaces by a mean-square isometry, which intertwines their actions. Uniqueness therefore identifies their future action laws. This proves autonomy and restartability along the constructed interval.\\n\\n## From the population flow to the actual fine-step GD\\n\\nFix a coarse mesh \\\\(\\\\Delta\\\\), independently of the actual step \\\\(\\\\eta_n\\\\). Build a finite oracle by expanding (M6), supplying it with the population residuals and every population contraction in these expansions. It starts from the same finite first-layer initialization and the same \\\\(W_0^{(2)}\\\\) as GD, but has \\\\(W_0^{(3)}=0\\\\). Every oracle instruction now has deterministic scalar coefficients. The fixed finite Gaussian theorem applies jointly to all its nodes and proves convergence of all the needed empirical contractions and second moments.\\n\\nThere is a useful distinction between oracle nodes and an actual finite network state. Construct proxy parameter arrays using its first-layer nodes and readout, and the matrix\\n\\\\[\\nW_{k,\\\\mathrm{proxy}}^{(2)}\\n=W_0^{(2)}-\\\\frac{2\\\\Delta}{n}\\n\\\\sum_{s<k,b}r_{b,s}\\\\delta_{b,s,\\\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(2)}\\n(h_{b,s,\\\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)})^\\\\top.\\n\\\\tag{M12}\\n\\\\]\\nApplying this proxy matrix to a current oracle activation differs from its prescribed oracle preactivation only by sums of empirical-minus-population contractions. For example each forward discrepancy has the form\\n\\\\[\\n-2\\\\Delta r_{b,s}\\\\delta_{b,s,\\\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(2)}\\n\\\\left(\\n\\\\frac{(h_{b,s,\\\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)})^\\\\top\\nh_{a,k,\\\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)}}{n}\\n-\\\\mathbb E[H_{b,s}^{(1)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}]\\n\\\\right).\\n\\\\tag{M13}\\n\\\\]\\nThe transpose discrepancy has the corresponding contraction of two \\\\(\\\\delta^{(2)}\\\\) fields. At a fixed mesh there are finitely many such terms. Their scalar errors tend to zero, and their vector norms stay bounded. The actual network quantities recomputed from the proxy arrays therefore differ from the oracle nodes by \\\\(o_{\\\\mathbb P}(1)\\\\) in root mean square. Bounded readout and the operator bound justify each subsequent forward/backward comparison. In particular its recomputed vector field differs from its prescribed oracle grid velocity by \\\\(o_{\\\\mathbb P}(1)\\\\).\\n\\nFor each fixed threshold \\\\(R\\\\), the oracle\'s empirical squared tails converge to their population counterparts. One may use continuous upper cutoffs, equal to one beyond \\\\(R\\\\) and zero below \\\\(R-1\\\\), to avoid discontinuities. The fixed finite Gaussian theorem applies to these quadratic-growth measurements. Consequently the root-mean-square tail of the recomputed proxy backward field has limit superior at most \\\\(C e^{-cR^2}\\\\), after modifying constants. This is proved for the reference oracle, not assumed for actual GD.\\n\\nLinearly interpolate the proxy parameters. Their metric speed is bounded in probability, with limiting bound independent of the mesh, by (M4) and the node consistency just proved. Their between-grid vector-field defect is bounded by (M10), plus a fixed-mesh \\\\(o_{\\\\mathbb P}(1)\\\\). Compare the actual GD interpolant to this proxy. Actual GD\'s derivative is its vector field at the preceding fine grid point; its state changes by \\\\(O(\\\\eta_n)\\\\) inside a fine step. Apply (M9) with the proxy as reference. No tail bound for the actual GD is needed. Gronwall gives\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\begin{aligned}\\n\\\\sup_{t\\\\le T}d_n(\\\\theta_n^{\\\\mathrm{GD}}(t),\\\\theta_n^{\\\\mathrm{proxy},\\\\Delta}(t))\\n\\\\le C e^{C(1+R)T}\\n\\\\bigg(\\n&\\\\frac{\\\\|W_{n,0}^{(3)}\\\\|_2}{\\\\sqrt n}\\n+(1+R)(\\\\eta_n+\\\\Delta)\\\\\\\\\\n&+e^{-cR^2}+o_{\\\\mathbb P}(1)\\n\\\\bigg).\\n\\\\end{aligned}\\\\tag{M14}\\n\\\\]\\nThe \\\\(o_{\\\\mathbb P}(1)\\\\) here is at fixed \\\\(R,\\\\Delta\\\\). Bounds on the actual readout supremum are obtained from its update, not from root-mean-square closeness.\\n\\nThe initialization discrepancy vanishes, and \\\\(\\\\eta_n\\\\to0\\\\). First take \\\\(n\\\\to\\\\infty\\\\) at fixed \\\\(R,\\\\Delta\\\\); next take \\\\(\\\\Delta\\\\to0\\\\); finally take \\\\(R\\\\to\\\\infty\\\\). Together with (M11), the fixed-mesh oracle empirical convergence, and the triangle inequality for observables, this proves the claimed joint limit. Thus this local proof needs no width-dependent learning-rate restriction beyond \\\\(\\\\eta_n\\\\to0\\\\).\\n\\nFor the first kernel block, use (M9) once more to transfer the first-layer \\\\(\\\\delta^{(1)}\\\\) fields in mean square; the other blocks follow directly from the bounded/Lipschitz maps. Products and contractions then converge by Cauchy–Schwarz. For hidden velocities,\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\dot z_a^{(2)}\\n=\\\\dot W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)}\\n+W^{(2)}\\\\bigl[\\\\phi\'(z_a^{(1)})\\\\odot\\\\dot z_a^{(1)}\\\\bigr].\\n\\\\]\\nThe reference first-layer velocity is a finite linear combination of bounded multiples of the backward fields, hence has the same tail control. Applying the same comparison proves convergence of both hidden velocities in integrated mean square and hence of their integrated squared norms.\\n\\nTo obtain path-law convergence, first use the finite Gaussian theorem on any fixed finite time grid. Between two times separated by at most \\\\(\\\\varepsilon\\\\), any absolutely continuous coordinate path satisfies\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\sup_{s,t\\\\ \\\\mathrm{in\\\\ one\\\\ grid\\\\ cell}}|z(t)-z(s)|^2\\n\\\\le\\\\varepsilon\\\\int_{\\\\mathrm{cell}}|\\\\dot z(u)|^2\\\\,du.\\n\\\\]\\nThe proved mean-energy bounds therefore approximate empirical and population path laws, in squared uniform-path distance, by their piecewise linear grid reconstructions with error at most \\\\(C\\\\varepsilon\\\\). Letting the grid become fine gives the joint two-input path-law assertion. The same finite-grid approximation and (M9) give uniform-in-time convergence of the stated fixed action probes.\\n\\n## The limit is genuinely feature-learning for both label patterns\\n\\nThe preceding construction proves (M4). We now check its small-time behavior without assuming that its hidden features move.\\n\\nLet\\n\\\\[\\nQ_{ab}=\\\\mathbb E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}].\\n\\\\]\\nThe first-layer root pair has full Gaussian support because \\\\(|\\\\rho|<1\\\\). No nonzero linear combination of its two arctangents vanishes identically, so \\\\(Q\\\\) is positive definite. The initial second-layer pair \\\\((Z_{0,1}^{(2)},Z_{0,2}^{(2)})\\\\) is centered Gaussian with covariance \\\\(Q\\\\).\\n\\nThe following temporary names recur in the expansion:\\n\\\\[\\nS=\\\\sum_a y_aH_{0,a}^{(2)},\\\\quad\\nU_a=S\\\\phi\'(Z_{0,a}^{(2)}),\\\\quad\\nP_a=(W_0^{(2)})^*U_a,\\\\quad\\nB_a=\\\\phi\'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})P_a,\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nV_{ab}=\\\\mathbb E[U_aU_b],\\\\qquad D_{ab}=\\\\mathbb E[B_aB_b].\\n\\\\tag{M15}\\n\\\\]\\nThe matrix \\\\(V\\\\) is positive definite. Indeed \\\\(S=0\\\\) only on the line \\\\(Z_{0,2}^{(2)}=-y_1y_2Z_{0,1}^{(2)}\\\\), a probability-zero event. If \\\\(c_1U_1+c_2U_2=0\\\\), full support and continuity force \\\\(c_1\\\\phi\'(s)+c_2\\\\phi\'(t)=0\\\\) for every \\\\(s,t\\\\), hence \\\\(c_1=c_2=0\\\\).\\n\\nConditioning on the two initial forward calls gives\\n\\\\[\\nP_a=\\\\sum_cH_{0,c}^{(1)}\\n\\\\left[\\nQ^{-1}\\\\mathbb E\\n\\\\begin{pmatrix}\\nZ_{0,1}^{(2)}U_a\\\\\\\\ Z_{0,2}^{(2)}U_a\\n\\\\end{pmatrix}\\n\\\\right]_c+\\\\Gamma_a,\\n\\\\tag{M16}\\n\\\\]\\nwhere \\\\((\\\\Gamma_1,\\\\Gamma_2)\\\\sim N(0,V)\\\\) is independent of the first-layer roots. This is also the first transpose instance of the appendix response rule, using Gaussian integration by parts. Since both \\\\(\\\\phi\'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})\\\\) are strictly positive, the conditional covariance of \\\\((B_1,B_2)\\\\) is positive definite, so \\\\(D\\\\) is positive definite.\\n\\nDefine\\n\\\\[\\nT_a=\\\\sum_bG_{ab}y_bB_b,\\\\qquad\\nR_a=\\\\sum_b y_bQ_{ab}U_b+\\nW_0^{(2)}[\\\\phi\'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a].\\n\\\\tag{M17}\\n\\\\]\\nSubstitute \\\\(r_a(0)=-y_a\\\\), \\\\(W^{(3)}(0)=0\\\\) into the integral equations. Mean-square continuity and boundedness of \\\\(\\\\phi,\\\\phi\'\\\\) give\\n\\\\[\\nW^{(3)}(t)=2tS+o_{L^2}(t),\\\\qquad\\n\\\\delta_a^{(2)}(t)=2tU_a+o_{L^2}(t),\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nZ_a^{(1)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(1)}\\n=2t^2T_a+o_{L^2}(t^2),\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nW^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}\\n=2t^2\\\\sum_b y_bU_b\\\\otimes H_{0,b}^{(1)}\\n+o_{\\\\rm op}(t^2),\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nZ_a^{(2)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(2)}\\n=2t^2R_a+o_{L^2}(t^2).\\n\\\\tag{M18}\\n\\\\]\\nFor example, dividing the readout integral by \\\\(t\\\\) gives \\\\(2S\\\\); dividing the backward field by \\\\(t\\\\) then gives \\\\(2U_a\\\\). The first-layer and matrix integrals therefore start at order \\\\(t^2\\\\). In the final line, the first term in \\\\(R_a\\\\) is the learned-matrix contribution and the second is the moving first-layer activation contribution. Differentiating the bounded smooth activation along these converging mean-square increments is justified by dominated convergence; no operator-norm differentiability of a nonlinear coordinate map on all of \\\\(L^2\\\\) is assumed.\\n\\nSet\\n\\\\[\\nA_1=\\\\sum_{a,b}y_a y_bG_{ab}D_{ab}>0,\\\\qquad\\nA_2=\\\\sum_{a,b}y_a y_bQ_{ab}V_{ab}>0 .\\n\\\\tag{M19}\\n\\\\]\\nPositivity follows, for example, from\\n\\\\(A_1=\\\\mathbb E[(y_1B_1,y_2B_2)G(y_1B_1,y_2B_2)^\\\\top]\\\\)\\nand positive definiteness of \\\\(G,D\\\\). The analogous argument uses \\\\(Q,V\\\\).\\nEach \\\\(T_a\\\\) is nonzero in mean square, by the positive conditional covariance in (M16). Adjunction gives\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\sum_a y_a\\\\mathbb E[U_aR_a]=A_1+A_2>0.\\n\\\\tag{M20}\\n\\\\]\\nIn fact each \\\\(R_a\\\\) is nonzero. Swapping the two inputs preserves the initial action law, sends \\\\(U_a\\\\) to \\\\(y_1y_2U_{3-a}\\\\), and sends \\\\(R_a\\\\) to \\\\(R_{3-a}\\\\). Thus the two summands on the left of (M20) are equal, each \\\\((A_1+A_2)/2>0\\\\).\\n\\nThe limiting kernels are (M2) with normalized finite dot products replaced by same-layer expectations. Consequently\\n\\\\[\\nK^{(1)}_{ab}(t)=4t^2G_{ab}D_{ab}+o(t^2),\\\\qquad\\nK^{(2)}_{ab}(t)=4t^2Q_{ab}V_{ab}+o(t^2),\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\ny^\\\\top K(t)y\\n=y^\\\\top K^{(3)}(0)y+8(A_1+A_2)t^2+o(t^2).\\n\\\\tag{M21}\\n\\\\]\\nFor the last equality, expansion of \\\\(H_a^{(2)}(t)\\\\) in (M18) gives a readout-block increase \\\\(4(A_1+A_2)t^2\\\\); the other two blocks supply the same increase. Both matrices with entries \\\\(G_{ab}D_{ab}\\\\) and \\\\(Q_{ab}V_{ab}\\\\) are positive definite. For example, for nonzero \\\\(c\\\\),\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\sum_{a,b}c_ac_bG_{ab}D_{ab}\\n\\\\ge\\\\lambda_{\\\\min}(G)\\\\sum_a c_a^2D_{aa}>0.\\n\\\\]\\nThe initial readout block is positive definite by full support of the second-layer Gaussian pair. This proves positivity of all blocks at sufficiently small positive times and nonconstancy of the total kernel.\\n\\nFinally,\\n\\\\[\\nL(0)=2,\\\\qquad \\\\dot L(0)=-4y^\\\\top K^{(3)}(0)y<0 .\\n\\\\]\\nThe expansions (M18), and their velocity versions obtained directly from (M4), give strictly positive squared displacements and integrated squared speeds for both inputs at both hidden layers. Initial preactivation variances are positive. The error of the best affine approximation to \\\\(\\\\arctan\\\\) is initially positive on each nondegenerate Gaussian marginal. Its formula in terms of variances and covariance, and mean-square continuity, preserve positivity for small time.\\n\\nThis finishes the stated local-time theorem. The response bound in the appendix is deliberately local. It does not establish a bound on every finite horizon, so an arbitrary-angle all-compact-time theorem is not claimed.\\n\\n## Appendix: the mesh-uniform response calculation\\n\\nThe following calculation supplies (M7). Its equation numbers are local to the appendix.\\n\\nLet the input Gram matrix be\\n\\\\[\\nG=\\\\begin{pmatrix}1&\\\\rho\\\\\\\\\\\\rho&1\\\\end{pmatrix},\\\\qquad |\\\\rho|<1,\\n\\\\]\\nand labels be \\\\(y_1,y_2\\\\in\\\\{-1,1\\\\}\\\\). Use the summed loss \\\\(\\\\sum_{a=1}^2r_a^2\\\\); averaging the loss only rescales time. The first-layer population root \\\\((Z_{1,0}^{(1)},Z_{2,0}^{(1)})\\\\) is centered Gaussian with covariance \\\\(G\\\\). The shared readout starts at \\\\(W_0^{(3)}=0\\\\).\\n\\nFor fixed mesh \\\\(\\\\Delta>0\\\\), population Euler obeys\\n\\\\[\\nH_{a,k}^{(1)}=\\\\phi(Z_{a,k}^{(1)}),\\\\quad\\nZ_{a,k}^{(2)}=W_k^{(2)}H_{a,k}^{(1)},\\\\quad\\nH_{a,k}^{(2)}=\\\\phi(Z_{a,k}^{(2)}),\\\\quad\\n\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}=W_k^{(3)}\\\\phi\'(Z_{a,k}^{(2)}),\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nr_{a,k}=\\\\mathbb E[W_k^{(3)}H_{a,k}^{(2)}]-y_a,\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nZ_{a,k+1}^{(1)}=Z_{a,k}^{(1)}-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{b=1}^2G_{ab}r_{b,k}\\\\phi\'(Z_{b,k}^{(1)})P_{b,k},\\n\\\\qquad P_{b,k}:=(W_k^{(2)})^*\\\\delta_{b,k}^{(2)},\\n\\\\tag{1}\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nW_{k+1}^{(2)}=W_k^{(2)}-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{b=1}^2r_{b,k}\\\\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}\\\\otimes H_{b,k}^{(1)},\\n\\\\qquad\\nW_{k+1}^{(3)}=W_k^{(3)}-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{b=1}^2r_{b,k}H_{b,k}^{(2)}.\\n\\\\tag{2}\\n\\\\]\\nHere \\\\(\\\\phi=\\\\arctan\\\\). All response derivatives below hold every deterministic coefficient in these finite recursions fixed, including residuals, contractions, and response coefficients. They do not differentiate through expectations or through the covariance law.\\n\\n## Exact finite-mesh Gaussian response representation\\n\\nIntroduce two centered jointly Gaussian collections \\\\(\\\\xi_{a,k}\\\\) and \\\\(\\\\eta_{a,k}\\\\), independent of one another. The first-layer root is independent of both. Their covariances are\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathbb E[\\\\xi_{a,k}\\\\xi_{b,s}]=\\\\mathbb E[H_{a,k}^{(1)}H_{b,s}^{(1)}],\\n\\\\qquad\\n\\\\mathbb E[\\\\eta_{a,k}\\\\eta_{b,s}]=\\\\mathbb E[\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\\\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}].\\n\\\\tag{3}\\n\\\\]\\nThese are separate-layer probability spaces; taking them independent on a product space is only a convenient representation, not an assertion of a natural pairing of neurons across layers.\\n\\nDefine the deterministic coefficients\\n\\\\[\\nA_{ak,bs}:=\\\\mathbb E\\\\left[\\\\frac{\\\\partial\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}}{\\\\partial\\\\xi_{b,s}}\\\\right],\\\\quad s\\\\le k,\\n\\\\qquad\\nC_{ak,bs}:=\\\\mathbb E\\\\left[\\\\frac{\\\\partial H_{a,k}^{(1)}}{\\\\partial\\\\eta_{b,s}}\\\\right],\\\\quad s<k.\\n\\\\tag{4}\\n\\\\]\\nThe derivatives are symbolic derivatives of the displayed causal recursions, treating Gaussian slots as separate real arguments even when their realized joint covariance is singular. Then the exact limiting initial-matrix actions are\\n\\\\[\\nW_0^{(2)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}=\\n\\\\xi_{a,k}+\\\\sum_{b=1}^2\\\\sum_{s<k}C_{ak,bs}\\\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)},\\n\\\\tag{5}\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\n(W_0^{(2)})^*\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}=\\n\\\\eta_{a,k}+\\\\sum_{b=1}^2\\\\sum_{s\\\\le k}A_{ak,bs}H_{b,s}^{(1)}.\\n\\\\tag{6}\\n\\\\]\\nExpanding the learned matrix gives the full equations\\n\\\\[\\nZ_{a,k}^{(2)}=\\n\\\\xi_{a,k}+\\\\sum_{b=1}^2\\\\sum_{s<k}\\n\\\\left(C_{ak,bs}-2\\\\Delta r_{b,s}\\\\mathbb E[H_{b,s}^{(1)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}]\\\\right)\\n\\\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)},\\n\\\\tag{7}\\n\\\\]\\n\\\\[\\nP_{a,k}=\\n\\\\eta_{a,k}+\\\\sum_{b=1}^2\\\\sum_{s\\\\le k}\\n\\\\left(A_{ak,bs}-\\\\mathbf1_{s<k}2\\\\Delta r_{b,s}\\\\mathbb E[\\\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}]\\\\right)\\nH_{b,s}^{(1)}.\\n\\\\tag{8}\\n\\\\]\\nTogether with (1), the readout update in (2), and \\\\(\\\\delta=W^{(3)}\\\\phi\'(Z^{(2)})\\\\), these are causal finite recursions. To construct step \\\\(k\\\\): first construct \\\\(H_{a,k}^{(1)}\\\\) and its derivatives using \\\\(\\\\eta\\\\)-slots through \\\\(k-1\\\\); next construct \\\\(Z_{a,k}^{(2)},\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\\\\) using \\\\(\\\\xi\\\\)-slots through \\\\(k\\\\); finally construct \\\\(P_{a,k}\\\\) and update first-layer coordinates and readout. The Gaussian covariance matrices extend consistently because they are Gram matrices of the constructed source variables.\\n\\nWhy the Gaussian response rule has this form: conditioning on previous forward and transpose products gives the Gaussian matrix regression formula with a projected fresh Gaussian matrix. In the limit, its Gaussian parts in each orientation have covariance equal to the Gram matrix of the sources. Its non-Gaussian part lies in the span of the previous sources in the opposite orientation. Gaussian integration by parts identifies the coefficients of this response as the expectations in (4). The elementary identity used is, for a centered Gaussian vector \\\\(g\\\\) with covariance \\\\(\\\\Sigma\\\\),\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathbb E[g_i f(g)]=\\\\sum_j\\\\Sigma_{ij}\\\\mathbb E[\\\\partial_j f(g)].\\n\\\\tag{9}\\n\\\\]\\nIt is valid even for singular \\\\(\\\\Sigma\\\\), by writing \\\\(g=B\\\\gamma\\\\) and integrating by parts in independent standard Gaussian coordinates \\\\(\\\\gamma\\\\). If the conditional regression uses a pseudoinverse, the symbolic derivative coefficients and the pseudoinverse coefficients can differ only by a vector in the covariance nullspace; the corresponding combination of source variables has zero squared norm, so the response itself is unchanged. This is the singular-covariance issue that must be retained in a complete proof.\\n\\nFor a rigorous external formulation of this finite-program identity, see [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685), Box 1, Remarks 2.11–2.12 and Theorem 2.10. The algebraic recursions here are smooth, polynomially bounded functions of finitely many Gaussian slots, and their symbolic derivatives are polynomially bounded for fixed program length. Thus all expectations in (4) exist. The finite-query theorem handles fixed program length; the estimates below are the additional ingredient needed uniformly as length increases. This note does not purport to reprove the entire master theorem in the preceding paragraph.\\n\\n## Uniform short-time response bounds\\n\\nFix \\\\(T\\\\le1\\\\) and \\\\(k\\\\Delta\\\\le T\\\\). Constants in this section depend only on bounds for \\\\(\\\\phi,\\\\phi\',\\\\phi\'\'\\\\), on \\\\(G\\\\), and on the number of inputs; they do not depend on \\\\(\\\\Delta\\\\), \\\\(k\\\\), or the Gaussian covariance ranks.\\n\\nPut \\\\(B_k=\\\\|W_k^{(3)}\\\\|_\\\\infty\\\\). Since \\\\(|r_{a,k}|\\\\le1+\\\\|\\\\phi\\\\|_\\\\infty B_k\\\\), (2) gives\\n\\\\[\\nB_{k+1}\\\\le B_k+4\\\\Delta\\\\|\\\\phi\\\\|_\\\\infty(1+\\\\|\\\\phi\\\\|_\\\\infty B_k).\\n\\\\]\\nThe resulting geometric sum gives, for a fixed \\\\(C_0\\\\),\\n\\\\[\\nB_k\\\\le C_0T,\\\\qquad |r_{a,k}|\\\\le C_0,\\\\qquad |\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}|\\\\le C_0T.\\n\\\\tag{10}\\n\\\\]\\nThe absolute row sums of the training-memory coefficients in (7) and (8) are therefore at most \\\\(C_0T\\\\) and \\\\(C_0T^3\\\\), respectively.\\n\\nDefine\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathcal A_k=\\\\max_{a,\\\\,r\\\\le k}\\\\sum_{b=1}^2\\\\sum_{s\\\\le r}|A_{ar,bs}|,\\n\\\\qquad\\n\\\\mathcal C_k=\\\\max_{a,\\\\,r\\\\le k}\\\\sum_{b=1}^2\\\\sum_{s<r}|C_{ar,bs}|.\\n\\\\tag{11}\\n\\\\]\\nEquation (8) already gives the pathwise bound\\n\\\\[\\n|P_{a,k}-\\\\eta_{a,k}|\\\\le C_0(\\\\mathcal A_k+T^3),\\n\\\\qquad\\n\\\\mathbb E[\\\\eta_{a,k}^2]\\\\le C_0^2T^2.\\n\\\\tag{12}\\n\\\\]\\n\\nFirst estimate \\\\(\\\\mathcal C_k\\\\) in terms of \\\\(\\\\mathcal A_{k-1}\\\\). Let \\\\(J_k\\\\) be the maximum, over the first-layer coordinates at times through \\\\(k\\\\), of the sum of absolute derivatives with respect to all backward Gaussian slots \\\\(\\\\eta_{b,s}\\\\). Initially \\\\(J_0=0\\\\). In (8), the derivative of \\\\(P_{a,k}\\\\) has absolute sum at most\\n\\\\[\\n1+(\\\\mathcal A_k+C_0T^3)J_k,\\n\\\\]\\nbecause \\\\(|\\\\phi\'|\\\\le1\\\\). Differentiating (1) and using bounded \\\\(G,r,\\\\phi\',\\\\phi\'\'\\\\) gives\\n\\\\[\\nJ_{k+1}\\\\le\\n\\\\left(1+C\\\\Delta\\\\left[1+\\\\mathcal A_k+|\\\\eta_{1,k}|+|\\\\eta_{2,k}|\\\\right]\\\\right)J_k+C\\\\Delta.\\n\\\\tag{13}\\n\\\\]\\nConsequently,\\n\\\\[\\nJ_k\\\\le CT\\\\exp\\\\left(CT(1+\\\\mathcal A_{k-1})+C\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{r<k}\\\\sum_{b=1}^2|\\\\eta_{b,r}|\\\\right).\\n\\\\tag{14}\\n\\\\]\\nNo independence over time is used. Jensen\'s inequality applied to the average of the \\\\(2k\\\\) terms, followed by the scalar Gaussian exponential bound, gives\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathbb E\\\\exp\\\\left(C\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{r<k}\\\\sum_{b=1}^2|\\\\eta_{b,r}|\\\\right)\\n\\\\le 2\\\\exp(2C^2T^2C_0^2T^2).\\n\\\\tag{15}\\n\\\\]\\nSince absolute expectation is bounded by expectation of absolute value, (4), (14), and (15) imply\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathcal C_k\\\\le CT\\\\exp\\\\left(CT(1+\\\\mathcal A_{k-1})+CT^4\\\\right),\\n\\\\tag{16}\\n\\\\]\\nafter increasing \\\\(C\\\\).\\n\\nNow estimate \\\\(\\\\mathcal A_k\\\\) through the second-layer recursion. Let \\\\(V_k\\\\) be the maximum, over \\\\(Z_{a,r}^{(2)}\\\\) for \\\\(r\\\\le k\\\\), of the sum of absolute derivatives with respect to all forward Gaussian slots \\\\(\\\\xi_{b,s}\\\\). It is a nonnegative random variable, but the bound below is deterministic.\\n\\nThe readout is\\n\\\\[\\nW_k^{(3)}=-2\\\\Delta\\\\sum_{r<k}\\\\sum_{a=1}^2r_{a,r}\\\\phi(Z_{a,r}^{(2)}).\\n\\\\]\\nTherefore its derivative absolute sum is at most \\\\(C_0T V_k\\\\). Differentiating \\\\(\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}=W_k^{(3)}\\\\phi\'(Z_{a,k}^{(2)})\\\\), using (10), yields\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\sum_{b,s}\\\\left|\\\\frac{\\\\partial\\\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}}{\\\\partial\\\\xi_{b,s}}\\\\right|\\\\le C_1T V_k.\\n\\\\tag{17}\\n\\\\]\\nEquation (7), whose memory uses only \\\\(s<k\\\\), yields\\n\\\\[\\nV_k\\\\le \\\\max\\\\{V_{k-1},\\\\,1+C_1T(\\\\mathcal C_k+C_0T)V_{k-1}\\\\}.\\n\\\\tag{18}\\n\\\\]\\nInitially \\\\(V_0=1\\\\) and \\\\(\\\\mathcal A_0=0\\\\).\\n\\nChoose fixed constants \\\\(M_A=2C_1\\\\) and \\\\(M_C\\\\) large enough that the right side of (16) is at most \\\\(M_CT\\\\) whenever \\\\(\\\\mathcal A_{k-1}\\\\le M_AT\\\\) and \\\\(T\\\\le T_0\\\\). Then choose \\\\(T_0>0\\\\) small enough that\\n\\\\[\\nC_1T_0^2(M_C+C_0)\\\\le\\\\tfrac12.\\n\\\\tag{19}\\n\\\\]\\nAn induction over \\\\(k\\\\) now proves\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\mathcal A_k\\\\le M_AT,\\\\qquad \\\\mathcal C_k\\\\le M_CT,\\\\qquad V_k\\\\le2,\\n\\\\qquad k\\\\Delta\\\\le T\\\\le T_0.\\n\\\\tag{20}\\n\\\\]\\nIndeed the bound for \\\\(\\\\mathcal A_{k-1}\\\\) supplies the bound for \\\\(\\\\mathcal C_k\\\\) in (16); (18)-(19) then give \\\\(V_k\\\\le2\\\\); (17) gives \\\\(\\\\mathcal A_k\\\\le2C_1T\\\\).\\n\\nCombining (12) and (20), every backward coordinate is a centered Gaussian of variance at most \\\\(C T^2\\\\), plus a bounded (possibly dependent) remainder of absolute value at most \\\\(CT\\\\). Thus there are fixed \\\\(c,C>0\\\\) such that\\n\\\\[\\n\\\\sup_{\\\\Delta>0}\\\\sup_{k\\\\Delta\\\\le T_0}\\\\max_a\\n\\\\mathbb E\\\\exp\\\\left(c|P_{a,k}|^2/T_0^2\\\\right)\\\\le C.\\n\\\\tag{21}\\n\\\\]\\nThis is the desired mesh-uniform Gaussian-tail bound. It was proved from bounded second-layer coordinate derivatives and a short-time response bootstrap. It was not assumed merely from fixed-program moment convergence.\\n"}'}]

## Ordinal 1631 agent_message /root/three_input_limit 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/three_input_limit
Payload:
Verified: the local existence and convergence proof extends to three inputs, including singular \(G\), with **one common positive \(T_0\)** valid for all normalized three-input geometries and all labels \(\pm1\). It is independent of width, learning rate, auxiliary mesh, dimension, and small Gram eigenvalues.

Full delta proof: [THREE_INPUT_LIMIT_EXTENSION_CHECK.md](/tmp/THREE_INPUT_LIMIT_EXTENSION_CHECK.md).

The reasons are specific:

- No part of the local convergence construction uses \(G^{-1}\) or \(\lambda_{\min}(G)>0\).
- Normalization gives \(|G_{ab}|\le1\) and \(\|G\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le3\).
- Three Gaussian roots with covariance \(G\) are permitted even when singular: represent them as \(G^{1/2}\gamma\).
- The response bootstrap merely changes sums over two inputs to sums over three. Its decisive Jensen estimate becomes
  \[
  \mathbb E e^{C\Delta\sum_{s<k,b\le3}|\eta_{b,s}|}
  \le2e^{(9/2)C^2C_0^2T^4}.
  \]
  Temporal independence is unnecessary.
- All ensuing localization, uniqueness, finite-GD comparison and mesh-removal estimates retain the same form. Any positive \(\eta_n\to0\) works.
- If \(Gc=0\), the first-layer relation \(\sum_ac_aZ_a^{(1)}=0\) is exact and preserved. It is not a stability obstruction.
- Hidden path laws, hidden velocities, kernels and integrated squared speeds converge as before. Physical first-weight energy is also expressed directly through \(G\), without inverses.

Important distinction: **the strict-feature-learning proof does need additional work**. Its former positive-definiteness-of-\(G\) and two-input exchange-symmetry arguments cannot simply be repeated for three inputs. The other assigned agent is handling that part.

## Ordinal 1658 agent_message /root/three_input_activity 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/three_input_activity
Payload:
All the short-time strict conclusions extend to three inputs, even when their \(3\times3\) input Gram matrix \(G\) has rank two. Full derivation: [three_input_activity_lemma.md](/tmp/three_input_activity_lemma.md).

Two essential fixes:

1. \(Q_{ab}=\mathbb E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}]\) is positive definite despite possible singularity of \(G\). A linear dependence among the three arctangent ridge functions, restricted to a suitably chosen line, gives a nonsingular Vandermonde system from the first three odd Taylor coefficients.

2. Each second-layer displacement coefficient \(R_a\) is nonzero without permutation symmetry. The new forward call \(W_0^{(2)}[\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a]\) has a fresh Gaussian component of strictly positive variance. Its input has positive conditional variance given the first-layer roots because it depends nontrivially on the preceding transpose-call Gaussian innovation. The learned-matrix term cannot cancel this independent new Gaussian component.

Consequently, for every input, both hidden layers move at order \(t^2\), squared displacement is strictly positive at order \(t^4\), and integrated squared velocity is strictly positive at order \(t^3\).

All three kernel blocks are positive definite for sufficiently small positive time. For the first block, singular \(G\) is harmless:

\[
G\circ D\ \succeq\ \lambda_{\min}(D)\,I
\]

because \(D\) is positive definite and \(G_{aa}=1\).

The total kernel obeys

\[
y^\top K(t)y
=
y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y
+
8(A_1+A_2)t^2+o(t^2),
\qquad A_1,A_2>0.
\]

Loss strictly decreases initially, and every hidden marginal retains positive variance and positive best-affine-fit error for \(\arctan\). No extra restrictions on the three label signs are needed.

If \(Gc=0\), then \(\sum_a c_a z_a^{(1)}=0\) initially and every update preserves this relation. No inverse of \(G\) is used.");
