const activity=String.raw`## Strict feature learning without assuming that G is invertible

We have proved existence, uniqueness, and convergence without needing \(G\) positive definite. For strict feature learning, two additional arguments replace the two-input proof's full-support and exchange-symmetry shortcuts.

Write
\[
Z_{0,a}^{(1)}=\frac{w^\top x_a}{\sqrt d},\qquad w\sim N(0,I_d),\qquad
Q_{ab}=\mathbb E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}].
\]
We first prove \(Q\) positive definite even if \(G\) has rank two. If \(c^\top Qc=0\), then
\[
\sum_{a=1}^3c_a\arctan(w^\top x_a/\sqrt d)=0
\]
almost surely. Continuity and the full support of \(w\) make this identity hold for every \(w\in\mathbb R^d\).

Choose \(v\in\mathbb R^d\) outside the finitely many hyperplanes
\[
v^\top x_a=0,\qquad v^\top(x_a-x_b)=0,\qquad
v^\top(x_a+x_b)=0.
\]
Each is a proper hyperplane because the inputs are nonzero and no two equal up to sign. A finite union of proper hyperplanes cannot cover \(\mathbb R^d\). Hence the numbers \(b_a=v^\top x_a/\sqrt d\) are nonzero and their squares are distinct. Restrict the identity to \(w=tv\). The coefficients of \(t,t^3,t^5\) in
\(\arctan(tb)=tb-(tb)^3/3+(tb)^5/5+O(t^7)\) give
\[
\sum_a c_a b_a(b_a^2)^k=0,\qquad k=0,1,2.
\]
The determinant of the matrix with columns \((1,b_a^2,b_a^4)^\top\) is
\(\prod_{a<b}(b_b^2-b_a^2)\ne0\). Thus \(c_a b_a=0\) for every \(a\), hence \(c=0\). This proves \(Q\succ0\).

The first forward calls now give a full-support Gaussian triple
\[
(Z_{0,1}^{(2)},Z_{0,2}^{(2)},Z_{0,3}^{(2)})\sim N(0,Q).
\]
Define the repeated coefficients
\[
S=\sum_a y_aH_{0,a}^{(2)},\quad
U_a=S\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(2)}),\quad
P_a=(W_0^{(2)})^*U_a,\quad
B_a=\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})P_a,
\]
\[
V_{ab}=\mathbb E[U_aU_b],\qquad D_{ab}=\mathbb E[B_aB_b].
\tag{M15}
\]
Conditional on the last two second-layer coordinates, \(S\) is a strictly monotone function of the first coordinate. That first coordinate has a continuous conditional Gaussian density, so \(\mathbb P(S=0)=0\). If \(\sum_ac_aU_a=0\), division by \(S\), full support, and continuity imply
\(\sum_ac_a\phi'(s_a)=0\) for every \(s\in\mathbb R^3\). Varying one coordinate while fixing the other two forces each \(c_a=0\). Therefore \(V\succ0\).

The simultaneous initial transpose responses satisfy
\[
P_a=\sum_{c=1}^3H_{0,c}^{(1)}
\left[
Q^{-1}
\begin{pmatrix}
\mathbb E[Z_{0,1}^{(2)}U_a]\\
\mathbb E[Z_{0,2}^{(2)}U_a]\\
\mathbb E[Z_{0,3}^{(2)}U_a]
\end{pmatrix}
\right]_c+\Gamma_a,
\tag{M16}
\]
where \(\Gamma\sim N(0,V)\) is independent of the first-layer roots. This follows by conditioning the initial Gaussian matrix on its three forward calls. Its fresh transpose part has source covariance \(V\); the finite-rank projection onto the initial forward directions disappears in normalized mean square. Equivalently, Gaussian integration by parts gives this instance of the appendix response rule.

Since every \(\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})>0\), the conditional covariance of \((B_1,B_2,B_3)\), given the first-layer roots, is positive definite. Hence \(D\succ0\).

Define the first- and second-layer displacement coefficients by
\[
T_a=\sum_bG_{ab}y_bB_b,\qquad
R_a=\sum_b y_bQ_{ab}U_b+
W_0^{(2)}[\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a].
\tag{M17}
\]
Each \(T_a\) has strictly positive conditional variance. In fact its coefficient of \(\Gamma_a\) in (M16) is \(G_{aa}y_a\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})\ne0\), and \(V\) is positive definite. Thus \(\mathbb E[T_a^2]>0\).

To prove every \(R_a\) nonzero we cannot use input-exchange symmetry: a general triple has none. Instead examine the next forward call. Denote its input, which recurs in this calculation, by
\[
J_a=\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a.
\]
Let \(\alpha_a\in\mathbb R^3\) solve
\[
\sum_c Q_{bc}\alpha_{a,c}=\mathbb E[H_{0,b}^{(1)}J_a],
\qquad b=1,2,3,
\]
and subtract the component explained by the previous forward inputs:
\[
J_a^\perp=J_a-\sum_c\alpha_{a,c}H_{0,c}^{(1)},\qquad
\sigma_a^2=\mathbb E[(J_a^\perp)^2].
\]
This is ordinary least-squares projection onto the three old \(H\) fields.

Given the first-layer roots, \(J_a\) is a linear function of the Gaussian vector \(\Gamma\), with coefficients
\[
c_b=\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})G_{ab}y_b\phi'(Z_{0,b}^{(1)}).
\]
In particular \(c_a=y_a\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})^2\ne0\). Subtracting a function of the roots cannot remove this conditional randomness, so
\[
\sigma_a^2
\ge\mathbb E[\operatorname{Var}(J_a\mid Z_{0,1}^{(1)},Z_{0,2}^{(1)},Z_{0,3}^{(1)})]
=\mathbb E[c^\top Vc]>0.
\tag{M18a}
\]

Condition on the already revealed forward calls
\(W_0^{(2)}H_{0,c}^{(1)}=Z_{0,c}^{(2)}\) and transpose calls
\((W_0^{(2)})^*U_b=P_b\). The new forward call has the population law
\[
W_0^{(2)}J_a
=\sum_c\alpha_{a,c}Z_{0,c}^{(2)}
+\sum_b\beta_{a,b}U_b+\sigma_a\gamma_a,
\qquad
\sum_c V_{bc}\beta_{a,c}=\mathbb E[P_bJ_a^\perp].
\tag{M18b}
\]
Here \(\gamma_a\sim N(0,1)\) is independent of the previous second-layer history. To see the variance, the remaining finite Gaussian matrix is projected orthogonally to the three observed input directions on each side. Applying it to the part of \(J_a\) perpendicular to the old \(H\) inputs gives variance \(\|J_a^\perp\|_2^2/n\). The output projection has fixed rank three, so its removal changes normalized mean square by \(O(1/n)\). Joint empirical convergence then gives (M18b). The response coefficients follow from the two recorded matrix identities. Both Gram inverses are legitimate because \(Q,V\succ0\). One such calculation is made for each \(a\); independence of the three \(\gamma_a\) from one another is neither claimed nor needed.

The learned-matrix contribution \(\sum_b y_bQ_{ab}U_b\) in \(R_a\) depends only on the previous second-layer coordinates. It cannot cancel the independent new Gaussian term. Thus
\[
\mathbb E[R_a^2]\ge\sigma_a^2>0,\qquad a=1,2,3.
\tag{M18c}
\]

Substitution in the integral equations, beginning with \(W^{(3)}(0)=0\) and \(r_a(0)=-y_a\), gives
\[
W^{(3)}(t)=2tS+o_{L^2}(t),\qquad
\delta_a^{(2)}(t)=2tU_a+o_{L^2}(t),
\]
\[
Z_a^{(1)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(1)}=2t^2T_a+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]
\[
W^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}
=2t^2\sum_b y_bU_b\otimes H_{0,b}^{(1)}+o_{\rm op}(t^2),
\]
\[
Z_a^{(2)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(2)}=2t^2R_a+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\tag{M19}
\]
For example the readout integral divided by \(t\) converges to \(2S\), then the second-layer backward field divided by \(t\) converges to \(2U_a\). Integrating the first-layer and matrix velocities therefore gives the factors \(2t^2\). Expanding \(W^{(2)}H_a^{(1)}\) gives the two terms in \(R_a\). Each passage uses bounded \(\phi,\phi'\), the bounded matrix action, and strong mean-square continuity. Differentiating the activation along these strongly convergent mean-square increments is justified by dominated convergence against their fixed limiting direction; no global differentiability claim for nonlinear maps on the whole mean-square space is used.

The corresponding hidden velocities are \(4tT_a+o_{L^2}(t)\) and \(4tR_a+o_{L^2}(t)\). Consequently each input's squared displacement has positive coefficient \(4\mathbb E[T_a^2]\) or \(4\mathbb E[R_a^2]\) times \(t^4\), and its integrated squared speed has positive coefficient \((16/3)\mathbb E[T_a^2]\) or \((16/3)\mathbb E[R_a^2]\) times \(t^3\).

It remains to check that singular \(G\) does not make a kernel block singular. Let \(\lambda>0\) be the smallest eigenvalue of \(D\). For every \(c\in\mathbb R^3\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{a,b}c_ac_bG_{ab}D_{ab}
&=\frac1d\sum_{j=1}^d
\mathbb E\left[\left(\sum_a c_a x_{a,j}B_a\right)^2\right]\\
&\ge\frac{\lambda}{d}\sum_{j=1}^d\sum_a c_a^2x_{a,j}^2
=\lambda\sum_a c_a^2 .
\end{aligned}
\tag{M20}
\]
This uses normalization, not invertibility of \(G\). Thus the matrix with entries \(G_{ab}D_{ab}\) is positive definite. Likewise the matrix with entries \(Q_{ab}V_{ab}\) is positive definite: if \(\lambda_V>0\) is the smallest eigenvalue of \(V\), its quadratic form is at least \(\lambda_V\sum_aQ_{aa}c_a^2>0\) for \(c\ne0\).

Put
\[
A_1=\sum_{a,b}y_ay_bG_{ab}D_{ab}>0,\qquad
A_2=\sum_{a,b}y_ay_bQ_{ab}V_{ab}>0.
\]
Adjunction and (M17) give
\[
\sum_a y_a\mathbb E[U_aR_a]=A_1+A_2.
\]
The kernel expansions are
\[
K^{(1)}_{ab}(t)=4t^2G_{ab}D_{ab}+o(t^2),\qquad
K^{(2)}_{ab}(t)=4t^2Q_{ab}V_{ab}+o(t^2).
\]
Expanding \(H_a^{(2)}(t)\) using (M19) shows that the readout block contributes \(4(A_1+A_2)t^2\) to its quadratic form in direction \(y\). The other two blocks contribute the same amount. Therefore
\[
y^\top K(t)y
=y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y+8(A_1+A_2)t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{M21}
\]
The total kernel is nonconstant. The first two blocks are positive definite at sufficiently small positive times by (M20). The initial readout block is positive definite because the second-layer Gaussian triple has full support, and the three coordinate arctangents have no linear relation. It remains positive definite by continuity.

Finally,
\[
L(0)=3,\qquad \dot L(0)=-4y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y<0.
\]
The loss strictly decreases on a sufficiently small interval. Every initial hidden marginal is a nondegenerate Gaussian, so it has positive variance and positive best-affine-fit error for \(\arctan\). The variance/covariance formula for that fit and mean-square continuity preserve positivity for small time.

All strict conclusions hold on some \((0,T_*]\) with \(0<T_*\le T_0\). These time constants do not depend on \(n\), \(\eta_n\), or \(\Delta\). The existence time \(T_0\) is uniform over normalized triples; the activity interval and positive activity coefficients may depend on the fixed triple and labels. No lower bound uniform near parallel configurations is claimed for those coefficients.

`;

For a fixed finite calculation from iid jointly Gaussian coordinate tuples, independent of a Gaussian matrix of entry variance \\\\(1/n\\\\), with smooth polynomially bounded coordinate functions and finitely many calls to that matrix and its transpose, every same-layer joint empirical average of a continuous polynomially bounded measurement has a deterministic limit. The limiting action is Gaussian randomness plus the responses forced by earlier opposite-direction calls. The precise response coefficients and their derivative convention are given and used in the appendix below. This is the smooth specialization of Theorem 2.10 and Box 1 of [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685). Its hypotheses hold here: after fixing population scalar coefficients, each instruction and each derivative needed below is a polynomially bounded function of finitely many Gaussian variables. PASS. No material gap found in the three-input local theorem, including rank-two \(G\), all eight label patterns, and the uniform existence-time claim.

The new points check out:

- The Vandermonde argument proves \(Q\succ0\). Pairwise nonparallelity makes every excluded hyperplane proper, permitting nonzero \(b_a\) with distinct squares.
- The conditional Gaussian covariance in (M16) is correctly \(V\). Thus \(D\succ0\) even when the first-layer root triple has singular covariance.
- In (M18a), subtracting the projection onto the old \(H\) fields cannot remove the conditional Gaussian component of \(J_a\). Its coefficient vector has \(c_a=y_a\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})^2\ne0\), giving \(\sigma_a^2>0\).
- Formula (M18b) has the correct response and fresh variance. Independently, writing \(P_b=m_b(H)+\Gamma_b\), orthogonality gives
  \[
  \mathbb E[P_bJ_a^\perp]
  =\mathbb E[\Gamma_bJ_a^\perp]
  =\sum_jV_{bj}\mathbb E[c_j].
  \]
  Hence its coefficients satisfy \(\beta_{a,j}=\mathbb E[c_j]\), exactly the transpose-response derivative rule. Regressing the new forward Gaussian part on the old forward Gaussian coordinates leaves variance \(\mathbb E[(J_a^\perp)^2]\). The resulting Gaussian is independent of the previous second-layer history, so the learned-matrix contribution cannot cancel it.
- Inequality (M20) correctly proves \(G\circ D\succeq\lambda_{\min}(D)I\), using \(G_{aa}=1\), without requiring \(G\) invertible.
- The factors in the expansions are correct: hidden increments \(2t^2T_a,2t^2R_a\); velocities \(4tT_a,4tR_a\); squared-displacement coefficients \(4\mathbb E[T_a^2]\), \(4\mathbb E[R_a^2]\); integrated-speed coefficients \(16/3\) times those second moments; total-kernel increase \(8(A_1+A_2)t^2\).

The existence proof uses only bounded activation derivatives, three inputs, \(|G_{ab}|\le1\), and the fixed initialization scales. Neither \(Q^{-1}\), \(V^{-1}\), nor a Gram eigenvalue lower bound enters its time bootstrap. Their later use for strict feature learning therefore does not compromise uniform \(T_0\).

The response representation agrees with Box 1 and Theorem 2.10 of [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685). The localization, oracle comparison, uniqueness, and restartability arguments support the remaining stated local conclusions.

No required fixes.

## Ordinal 1744 message assistant commentary
The singular case checks out: a linear relation among the inputs remains a linear relation among their first preactivations, but generally not among their arctangent activations. That lets the proof retain all three training directions. The existence-time bound also uses no minimum angle or Gram eigenvalue, so one common positive \(T_0\) can cover all normalized triples; the amount of feature movement need not have a uniform positive lower bound near parallel configurations.

- **Singular input geometry:** The first-layer dynamics preserve every relation \(c^\top Z^{(1)}=0\) with \(Gc=0\). Neither construction nor stability uses \(G^{-1}\). The Vandermonde argument proves \(Q\succ0\) under exactly the stated pairwise nonparallelity assumption, even when \(G\) has rank two.

- **Uniform \(T_0\):** The tail bootstrap uses only activation bounds, three inputs, \(|G_{ab}|\le1\), and bounded initialization scales. It contains no inverse covariance or minimum-eigenvalue estimate. Consequently \(T_0\) can be fixed independently of geometry, dimension, width, and learning rate. This establishes a common existence interval; it does not assert convergence rates uniform over changing geometries.

- **Response theorem and tails:** The cited finite-program theorem permits transpose reuse and singular Gaussian covariances. The appendix’s symbolic derivative convention is appropriate. Its bootstrap remains causal, and the factor \(9/2\) in the three-input Gaussian estimate (15) is correct. No temporal independence is assumed. [Tensor Programs III, Box 1, Theorem 2.10 and Remarks 2.11–2.12](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685)

- **Common spaces and continuum:** Consistent finite-program laws, the finite operator bound, and second-moment convergence yield bounded adjoint operators on the completed generated spaces. The one-sided reference-tail estimate then supplies Euler convergence and uniqueness. Singular \(G\) introduces no additional requirement here.

- **Oracle and arbitrary positive \(\eta_n\to0\):** Proxy recomputation errors are finite sums of empirical contraction errors at fixed coarse mesh. The first-layer proxy coordinates equal their oracle nodes, so this step does not assume continuity of an uncontrolled unbounded product. Comparing actual and proxy preceding-grid vector fields proves (M14) using only proxy tails. Its successive limits—width, coarse mesh, then cutoff—are valid without a width-dependent step restriction.

\[
\mathbb E[J_a^2]
-\mathbb E[J_aH]^\top Q^{-1}\mathbb E[HJ_a]
=\sigma_a^2.
\]
For an additional check on the response coefficient, write \(P=AH+\Gamma\) and \(J_a=c^\top\Gamma+\) a function of the first-layer roots. Orthogonality of \(J_a^\perp\) to \(H\) gives
\[
V^{-1}\mathbb E[PJ_a^\perp]
=V^{-1}\mathbb E[\Gamma J_a^\perp]
=\mathbb E[c],
\]
which is precisely the forward response coefficient. Thus the new independent Gaussian term in (M18b) is legitimate. Equation (M18a) makes its variance strictly positive for every input, since \(c_a\ne0\) and \(V\succ0\). The learned-matrix contribution cannot cancel it.

## Ordinal 1777 message assistant final_answer
Yes—exactly. **\(T_0\) is a fixed positive amount of physical time**, chosen before taking either limit. It is not a sequence of intervals shrinking with \(n\) or \(\eta_n\). Indeed, the number of GD updates covered by that interval grows:

\[
\left\lfloor\frac{T_0}{\eta_n}\right\rfloor\longrightarrow\infty.
\]

And **the same local result extends to three inputs under precisely your assumptions**, including configurations where the three inputs are linearly dependent.

The [complete three-input proof is here](/tmp/THREE_INPUT_LOCAL_LIMIT_PROOF.md). Below are the statement and the substantive changes.

## The three-input result

Fix \(x_1,x_2,x_3\in\mathbb R^d\), with

\[
\frac{\|x_a\|_2^2}{d}=1,
\qquad
x_a\ne \pm x_b\quad(a\ne b),
\qquad
y_a\in\{-1,1\}.
\]

Define their normalized dot-product matrix by

\[
G_{ab}=\frac{x_a^\top x_b}{d},
\qquad a,b\in\{1,2,3\}.
\]

Keep the same network and initialization:

\[
z_a^{(1)}=\frac{W^{(1)}x_a}{\sqrt d},
\qquad
h_a^{(1)}=\phi(z_a^{(1)}),
\qquad
z_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)},
\qquad
h_a^{(2)}=\phi(z_a^{(2)}),
\]

\[
\phi=\arctan,
\qquad
W_{0,ij}^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),
\qquad
W_{0,ji}^{(2)}\sim N(0,1/n),
\qquad
W_{0,j}^{(3)}\sim N(0,1/n^2),
\]

with independent initialization entries. The weights have dimensions \(n\times d\), \(n\times n\), and \(n\), respectively.

Use the summed loss

\[
f_{n,a}=\frac1n(W^{(3)})^\top h_a^{(2)},
\qquad
r_{n,a}=f_{n,a}-y_a,
\qquad
L_n=\sum_{a=1}^3r_{n,a}^2.
\]

Averaging these three losses instead would divide the speed by three.

With the same residual-free backpropagation quantities,

\[
\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\odot\phi'(z_a^{(2)}),
\qquad
\delta_a^{(1)}
=
\phi'(z_a^{(1)})\odot(W^{(2)})^\top\delta_a^{(2)},
\]

the exact GD updates are

\[
\begin{aligned}
z_{a,k+1}^{(1)}
&=
z_{a,k}^{(1)}
-
2\eta_n\sum_{b=1}^3
G_{ab}r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(1)},\\
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
&=
W_k^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\eta_n}{n}\sum_{b=1}^3
r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}
(h_{b,k}^{(1)})^\top,\\
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
&=
W_k^{(3)}
-
2\eta_n\sum_{b=1}^3
r_{n,b,k}h_{b,k}^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

There exists a fixed \(T_0>0\) such that, for every sequence of positive learning rates satisfying

\[
\eta_n\longrightarrow0,
\]

these networks converge on \([0,T_0]\) to a unique autonomous population gradient flow.

The convergence includes predictions, residuals, loss, all three kernel blocks, joint hidden trajectories, integrated squared hidden velocities, and the forward-and-adjoint measurements of \(W^{(2)}\).

For example, for either hidden layer and every bounded Lipschitz measurement \(\psi\),

\[
\sup_{t\le T_0}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i
\psi\!\left(
z_{1,i}^{(\ell)}(t),
z_{2,i}^{(\ell)}(t),
z_{3,i}^{(\ell)}(t)
\right)
-
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
Z_1^{(\ell)}(t),
Z_2^{(\ell)}(t),
Z_3^{(\ell)}(t)
\right)
\right|
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability. Thus the limit retains how **all three inputs behave jointly at a neuron**.

The population state now has five objects:

\[
Z_1^{(1)},\quad Z_2^{(1)},\quad Z_3^{(1)},
\quad W^{(2)},\quad W^{(3)}.
\]

Using the same forward and backward formulas with population coordinates, expectations, and \((W^{(2)})^*\), its equations are

\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot Z_a^{(1)}
&=-2\sum_{b=1}^3G_{ab}r_b\delta_b^{(1)},\\
\dot W^{(2)}
&=-2\sum_{b=1}^3r_b\,
\delta_b^{(2)}\otimes H_b^{(1)},\\
\dot W^{(3)}
&=-2\sum_{b=1}^3r_bH_b^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Here

\[
f_a=\mathbb E[W^{(3)}H_a^{(2)}],
\qquad r_a=f_a-y_a,
\]

and

\[
(\delta_b^{(2)}\otimes H_b^{(1)})V
=
\delta_b^{(2)}\,\mathbb E[H_b^{(1)}V].
\]

The current state determines every term in these equations; no stored training history is needed to continue the evolution.

## Why three inputs require an additional check

Pairwise nonparallel inputs need not be linearly independent. For example, if \(x_1,x_2\) are orthogonal and normalized as above, then

\[
x_3=\frac{x_1+x_2}{\sqrt2}
\]

is normalized and parallel to neither, but \(G\) is singular.

The existence proof accommodates this. Its estimates use

\[
|G_{ab}|\le1,
\]

not \(G^{-1}\) or a positive lower bound on its eigenvalues. The finite Gaussian construction also allows correlated, singular Gaussian root distributions. [Tensor Programs III, Setup 2.2 and Theorem 2.10](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685)

The backward-field estimate therefore extends with sums over three inputs:

\[
\sup_{\Delta}
\max_{a,k:\,k\Delta\le T_0}
\mathbb E\exp\!\left(
c\left|(W_k^{(2)})^*\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\right|^2
\right)
\le C.
\]

Only numerical constants change. The same cutoff comparison then proves existence, uniqueness, and convergence from actual GD.

In fact, **one common existence time \(T_0>0\) can be chosen for all normalized three-input configurations and all eight label patterns**. It does not deteriorate merely because \(G\) becomes singular. This is a common time interval, not a claim of convergence rates uniform over changing configurations.

The subtler question is whether feature learning remains strictly nonzero for every input.

First, a linear relation among the inputs does not force the same relation among their arctangent features. More precisely,

\[
Q_{ab}
=
\mathbb E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}]
\]

is positive definite even when \(G\) is singular.

Here is the core reason. Suppose

\[
\sum_{a=1}^3 c_a\arctan(w^\top x_a/\sqrt d)=0
\]

for Gaussian \(w\). Continuity makes this hold for every \(w\). Choose a direction \(v\) whose three projections \(b_a=v^\top x_a/\sqrt d\) are nonzero and have distinct absolute values; pairwise nonparallelity permits this choice. Setting \(w=tv\) and comparing the coefficients of \(t,t^3,t^5\) gives

\[
\sum_a c_ab_a=0,\qquad
\sum_a c_ab_a^3=0,\qquad
\sum_a c_ab_a^5=0.
\]

The coefficient matrix has nonzero determinant because the \(b_a^2\) are distinct. Therefore all \(c_a=0\).

So the three activation fields provide three genuinely distinct directions, despite any linear dependence among the inputs.

Second, the two-input proof’s exchange symmetry is unavailable for a general triple. To prove that **each** second-layer preactivation moves, the new proof examines its next reused forward call. That call contains a fresh Gaussian component of strictly positive variance, independent of the previously observed second-layer coordinates. The learned rank-one contributions depend on those previous coordinates and therefore cannot cancel this new component.

This establishes movement for every input, not merely movement after summing over inputs.

## What survives substantively

For each \(a\in\{1,2,3\}\) and each hidden layer,

\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
\bigl(Z_a^{(\ell)}(t)-Z_a^{(\ell)}(0)\bigr)^2
\right]
=
c_{\ell,a}t^4+o(t^4),
\qquad c_{\ell,a}>0.
\]

All three \(3\times3\) kernel blocks are positive definite for sufficiently small positive time, and

\[
y^\top K(t)y
=
y^\top K(0)y+ct^2+o(t^2),
\qquad c>0.
\]

Thus both hidden layers learn features for every input, and the kernel changes. Moreover,

\[
L(0)=3,
\qquad
\dot L(0)=-4y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y<0.
\]

These conclusions hold for arbitrary label signs.

The distinction to retain is: the **existence interval is a fixed, nonvanishing interval**, while the amount of feature movement—and the smaller interval on which all strict activity conclusions are guaranteed—may depend on the particular input geometry. Neither interval shrinks with width or learning rate. The arbitrary-geometry theorem remains local in time; global-time convergence is not claimed.

## Ordinal 1843 message assistant commentary
The nontriviality claims do hold, with one qualification: because the limiting readout starts at zero, hidden-feature velocity is zero at \(t=0\), then grows proportionally to \(t\). At each fixed sufficiently small \(t>0\), it has a positive, width-independent limit. The loss is already decreasing at \(t=0\). The proof also gives a positive error for the best affine fit to arctan on each hidden distribution, which rules out an asymptotically linear activation regime.

Hu\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue2028†Igor Babuschkin\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue2029†Szymon Sidor\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20210†Xiaodong Liu\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20211†David Farhi\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20212†Nick Ryder\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20213†Jakub Pachocki\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20214†Weizhu Chen\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20215†Jianfeng Gao\ue201 L12: \nL13: View a PDF of the paper titled Tensor Programs V: Tuning Large Neural Networks via Zero-Shot Hyperparameter Transfer, by Greg Yang and 9 other authors\nL14: \nL15: \ue200cite\ue20216†View PDF\ue201 \ue200cite\ue20217†HTML (experimental)\ue201 L16: > Abstract:Hyperparameter (HP) tuning in deep learning is an expensive process, prohibitively so for neural networks (NNs) with billions of parameters. We show that, in the recently discovered Maximal Update Parametrization (muP), many optimal HPs remain stable even as model size changes.\nL17: This leads to a new HP tuning paradigm we call muTransfer: parametrize the target model in muP, tune the HP indirectly on a smaller model, and zero-shot transfer them to the full-sized model, i.e., without directly tuning the latter at all. We verify muTransfer on Transformer and ResNet.\nL18: For example, 1) by transferring pretraining HPs from a model of 13M parameters, we outperform published numbers of BERT-large (350M parameters), with a total tuning cost equivalent to pretraining BERT-large once; 2) by transferring from 40M parameters, we outperform published numbers of the 6.7B GPT-3 model, with tuning cost only 7% of total pretraining cost. A Pytorch implementation of our technique can be found at \ue200cite\ue20218†this http URL†github.com\ue201 and installable via `pip install mup`.\nL19: Comments:  | NeurIPS 2021\nL20: Subjects:  | Repository files navigation\nL173: \nL174:   *   * \ue200cite\ue20288†README\ue201 L175:   * \ue200cite\ue20288†Code of conduct\ue201 L176:   * \ue200cite\ue20288†MIT license\ue201 L177:   * \ue200cite\ue20288†Security\ue201 L178: \nL179: More items\nL180: # Maximal Update Parametrization (μP) and Hyperparameter Transfer (μTransfer)\nL181: \nL182: \ue200cite\ue20289†Paper link†arxiv.org\ue201 | \ue200cite\ue20290†Blog link†www.microsoft.com\ue201 | \ue200cite\ue20291†YouTube link†www.youtube.com\ue201 L183: In \ue200cite\ue20289†Tensor Programs V: Tuning Large Neural Networks via Zero-Shot Hyperparameter Transfer†arxiv.org\ue201 , we show that optimal hyperparameters become stable across neural network sizes when we parametrize the model in \ue200cite\ue20292†maximal update parametrization (μP)†arxiv.org\ue201 . This can be used to tune extremely large neural networks such as large pretrained transformers, as we have done in our work.\nL184: More generally, μP reduces the fragility and uncertainty when transitioning from exploration to scaling up, which are not often talked about explicitly in the deep learning literature.\nL185: Figure above: Training loss against learning rate on Transformers of varying `d_model` trained with Adam.\nL186: μP turns out to be the unique "natural" parametrization that has this hyperparameter stability property across width, as empirically verified in the gif below on MLPs trained with SGD. Here, across time, we interpolate between PyTorch default and μP\'s learning rate and initialization scalings (right), and we scale up the width-256 model (log2(width)=8) to width 2^13 = 8192 using this interpolated scaling rule (left).\nL187: This repo contains the source code for the `mup` package, our tool that makes the implementation of μP in Pytorch models effortless and less error-prone.\nL188: ## Table of Contents\nL189: \nL190:   * \ue200cite\ue20293†Installation\ue201 L191:     * \ue200cite\ue20294†Install From Source\ue201 L192:   * \ue200cite\ue20295†Basic Usage\ue201 L193:   * \ue200cite\ue20296†How `mup` Works Under the Hood\ue201 L194:   * \ue200cite\ue20297†Current Limitations\ue201 L195:   * \ue200cite\ue20298†Checking Correctness of Parametrization\ue201 L196:     * \ue200cite\ue20299†Coord Check\ue201 L197:     * \ue200cite\ue202100†Making Your Own Coord Check Plots\ue201 L198:     * \ue200cite\ue202101†Wider is Always Better\ue201 L199:   * \ue200cite\ue202102†Examples\ue201 L200:   * \ue200cite\ue202103†Running Tests\ue201 L201:   * \ue200cite\ue202104†The Basic Math\ue201 L202:   * \ue200cite\ue202105†Contributing\ue201 L203:   * \ue200cite\ue202106†Trademarks\ue201 L204: \nL205: ## Installation\nL206: \nL207:     `pip install mup\nL208:     `\nL209: ### Install From Source\nL210: \nL211: Clone this repo, change to its directory, and do\nL212: \nL213:     `pip install -r requirements.txt\nL214:     pip install -e .\nL215:     `\nL216: ## Basic Usage\nL217: \nL218:     from mup import MuReadout, make_base_shapes, set_base_shapes, MuSGD, MuAdam\nL219: \nL220:     class MyModel(nn.Module):\nL221:         def __init__(self, width, ...):\nL222:             ...\nL223:             ### In model definition, replace output layer with MuReadout\nL224:             # readout = nn.Linear(width, d_out)\nL225:             readout = MuReadout(width, d_out)\nL226:             ### If tying weights with an input nn.Embedding layer, do\nL227:             # readout = MuSharedReadout(input_layer.weight)\nL228:             ...\nL229:         def forward(self, ...):\nL230:             ...\nL231:             ### If using a transformer, make sure to use\nL232:             ###   1/d instead of 1/sqrt(d) attention scaling\nL233:             # attention_scores = query @ key.T / d**0.5\nL234:             attention_scores = query @ key.T * 8 / d\nL235:             ### We use 8/d instead of 1/d here to be backward compatible\nL236:             ###   with 1/d**0.5 when d=64, a common head dimension.\nL237:             ...\nL238: \nL239:     ### Instantiate a base model\nL240:     base_model = MyModel(width=1)\nL241:     ### Optionally, use `torchdistx.deferred_init.deferred_init` to avoid instantiating the parameters\nL242:     ### Simply install `torchdistx` and use\nL243:     # base_model = torchdistx.deferred_init.deferred_init(MyModel, width=1)\nL244:     ### Instantiate a "delta" model that differs from the base model\nL245:     ###   in all dimensions ("widths") that one wishes to scale.\nL246:     ### Here it\'s simple, but e.g., in a Transformer, you may want to scale\nL247:     ###   both nhead and dhead, so the delta model should differ in both.\nL248:     delta_model = MyModel(width=2) # Optionally use `torchdistx` to avoid instantiating\nL249: \nL250:     ### Instantiate the target model (the model you actually want to train).\nL251:     ### This should be the same as the base model except\nL252:     ###   the widths could be potentially different.\nL253:     ### In particular, base_model and model should have the same depth.\nL254:     model = MyModel(width=100)\nL255: \nL256:     ### Set base shapes\nL257:     ### When `model` has same parameter shapes as `base_model`,\nL258:     ###   `model` behaves exactly the same as `base_model`\nL259:     ###   (which is in PyTorch\'s default parametrization).\nL260:     ###   This provides backward compatibility at this particular model size.\nL261:     ###   Otherwise, `model`\'s init and LR are scaled by μP.\nL262:     ### IMPORTANT: this should be called as soon as possible,\nL263:     ###   before re-initialization and optimizer definition.\nL264:     set_base_shapes(model, base_model, delta=delta_model)\nL265: \nL266:     ### Alternatively, one can save the base model shapes in a file\nL267:     # make_base_shapes(base_model, delta_model, filename)\nL268:     ### and later set base shapes directly from the filename\nL269:     # set_base_shapes(model, filename)\nL270:     ### This is useful when one cannot fit both\nL271:     ###   base_model and model in memory at the same time\nL272: \nL273:     ### Replace your custom init, if any\nL274:     for param in model.parameters():\nL275:         ### If initializing manually with fixed std or bounds,\nL276:         ### then replace with same function from mup.init\nL277:         # torch.nn.init.uniform_(param, -0.1, 0.1)\nL278:         mup.init.uniform_(param, -0.1, 0.1)\nL279:         ### Likewise, if using\nL280:         ###   `xavier_uniform_, xavier_normal_, kaiming_uniform_, kaiming_normal_`\nL281:         ### from `torch.nn.init`, replace with the same functions from `mup.init`\nL282: \nL283:     ### Use the optimizers from `mup.optim` instead of `torch.optim`\nL284:     # optimizer = torch.optim.SGD(model.parameters(), lr=0.1)\nL285:     optimizer = MuSGD(model.parameters(), lr=0.1)\nL286:     ### Then just train normally\nL287: Note the base and delta models do not need to be trained --- we are only extracting parameter shape information from them. Therefore, optionally, we can avoid instantiating these potentially large models by using the `deferred_init` function in `torchdistx`. After installing \ue200cite\ue202107†`torchdistx`\ue201 , use `torchdistx.deferred_init.deferred_init(MyModel, **args)` instead of `MyModel(**args)`. See \ue200cite\ue202108†this page†pytorch.org\ue201 for more detail.\nL288: In the MLP and Transformer examples (not `mutransformers`) we provided, you can activate this feature by passing `--deferred_init`.\nL289: ## How `mup` Works Under the Hood\nL290: By invoking `set_base_shapes(model, ...)`, each parameter tensor `p` of `model` gets a `p.infshape` attribute that stores, for each of its dimensions, the corresponding base dimension and whether that dimension should be considered `infinite` (i.e. will be scaled up/down, e.g., `d_model` of a Transformer) or `finite` (i.e. will be fixed, e.g., vocabulary size). This information is used in the initializers and optimizers to automatically scale the parameters or learning rates to be compliant with μP.\nL291: For example, the Adam learning rate of hidden weights `p` is calculated as `globalLR / p.infshape.width_mult()`, where `p.infshape.width_mult()` essentially calculates `fan_in / base_fan_in`.\nL292: ## Current Limitations\nL293:   * `set_base_shapes(model, ...)` assumes that `model` has just been randomly initialized in the standard way and rescales its parameters using the base shape information so the model is in μP.\nL294:   * If you want data parallelism, please use `torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel` instead of `torch.nn.DataParallel`. This is because the latter removes the attributes the `mup` package adds to each parameter tensor of the model. Also, for performance, `pytorch` \ue200cite\ue202109†recommends the former anyway†pytorch.org\ue201 .\nL295:   * We scale the learning rate according to μP explicitly by creating refined parameter groups from what is passed to the `mup` optimizer and by manipulating the `lr` attribute in those groups. This is compatible with PyTorch\'s learning rate schedulers. However, if you roll your own, make sure the scheduler sets the learning rate relative to what is currently in the refined parameter groups. The following is an example of what not to do and what is OK:\nL296:     optimizer = mup.MuAdam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)\nL297:     for pg in optimizer.param_groups:\nL298:       # what NOT to do: setting learning rate absolutely\nL299:       # pg[\'lr\'] = 1e-3 * 2\nL300:       # what is an OK alternative: setting it relatively\nL301:       pg[\'lr\'] *= 2\nL302:   * By default, any parameter matrix that has 2 "infinite" dimensions (i.e. dimensions that are different from base dimensions) are considered by `mup` to have shape (fan_out, fan_in), i.e., in the forward pass, this matrix multiplies its input on the right. This is the case with all `nn.Linear` weights from pytorch.\nL303: If you have a custom parameter, say `W`, that violates this convention, you can manually set `W.infshape.main_idx = 0; W.infshape.main = W.infshape[0]` to let `mup` know that its shape corresponds to (fan_in, fan_out). A similar discussion applies if you have a parameter tensor with many dimensions but exactly 2 "infinite" dimensions, for which the first is fan_in and the second is fan_out.\nL304:   * Currently, \ue200cite\ue202110†`torch.save` does not save the `infshape` objects attached to each parameter tensor\ue201 . Before this is fixed, you would have to set base shape manually after loading a model checkpoint like so:\nL305: \nL306:     model = torch.load(\'my/model/path.pt\')\nL307:     # Important: note the flag `rescale_params=False`!\nL308:     set_base_shapes(model, \'my/base/shape/path.bsh\', rescale_params=False)\nL309: (`set_base_shapes` by default rescales the parameters of `model`, assuming it\'s freshly initialized by PyTorch, to be consistent with μP. The `rescale_params=False` flag turns off this behavior.)\nL310: ## Checking Correctness of Parametrization\nL311: ### Coord Check\nL312: Just like gradient checking is a simple way of verifying the correctness of an autograd implementation, coordinate checking is a simple way to verify you have implemented μP correctly: calculate the average size (which we denote in the y-axis below by `l1`) of the coordinates of each activation vector in, and output of, the model, for a few steps of training and a few different widths.\nL313: If implemented correctly, then we shall see this `l1` stable over many widths; otherwise, the `l1` can blow up or shrink to 0 with width. (We are essentially checking desideratum 1 described below.) (The `l1` calculates `x.abs().mean()` for each activation vector `x` and is just one measure of the "average size" of `x`\'s entries; one can also use analogously defined `l2`, `l4`, etc, though they may exhibit greater fluctuation with random seeds.)\nL314: For example, in the following, we plot `width` vs `l1` for 2 steps of training, where t=1 means at initialization, before any gradient update. Each curve corresponds to an (pre-)activation vector of a layer or the output of the network. The first set of 3 plots shows an MLP in standard parametrization (SP), trained by adam. We see after 1 step of update, activation/output `l1` are exploding with width.\nL315: This means SP is "incorrect." We now do the same for an MLP in maximal update parametrization (μP) (including using `mup.optim.MuAdam` instead of `torch.optim.Adam`). In contrast to the above, all curves stay horizontal, indicating that μP is implemented correctly. We call this way of checking implementation correctness a coord check, short for "coordinate check."\nL316: ### Making Your Own Coord Check Plots\nL317: We provide an easy way to implement this check via functions in the `mup.coord_check` module. The workflow typically looks like the following.\nL318: \nL319:     from mup.coord_check import get_coord_data, plot_coord_data\nL320:     # construct a dictionary of lazy μP models with differing widths\nL321:     def lazy_model(width):\nL322:         # `set_base_shapes` returns the model\nL323:         return lambda: set_base_shapes(MyMuModel(width), \'my/base/shape/path.bsh\')\nL324:         # Note: any custom initialization with `mup.init` would need to\nL325:         # be done inside the lambda as well\nL326:     models = {64: lazy_model(64), ..., 1024: lazy_model(1024)}\nL327:     # make a dataloader with small batch size/seq len\nL328:     #   just for testing\nL329:     dataloader = ...\nL330:     # record data from the model activations over a few steps of training\nL331:     # this returns a pandas dataframe\nL332:     df = get_coord_data(models, dataloader)\nL333:     # This saves the coord check plots to filename.\nL334:     plot_coord_data(df, save_to=filename)\nL335:     # If you are in jupyter notebook, you can also do\nL336:     #   `plt.show()`\nL337:     # to show the plot\nL338: For example, the `mup.coord_check.example_plot_coord_check` function is implemented this way for toy MLP and CNN models.\nL339: If you see the curves blow up or shrink to 0 with width after a few steps of training, then there\'s a bug in your μP implementation (did you forget to vary some dimension, like `d_ffn`, in the delta model?). If instead you see the curves converge to the right, then most likely your implementation is correct. However, there are two typical exceptions to this; the following can shrink to 0 at initialization in μP (at a 1/sqrt(width) rate):\nL340: \nL341:   * the network output\nL342:   * the attention logits in a Transformer\nL343: These are transient, and after a few steps their curves should be roughly flat. Nevertheless, to remove the discrepancy at init, we recommend\nL344: \nL345:   * initializing the output layer (should be a `MuReadout` instance) weights to be 0 via the `readout_zero_init=True` option and\n'}]

An\nL1302@P21-22: 22Table 8: Alternative (Equivalent) µP Formulation for Easier Implementation. Same format as\nL1303@P22: in Table 3. This can be useful in some settings (e.g., Appendix D.2)\nL1347@P22: but detrimental in other settings (e.g., hidden weights).\nL1348@P22: What Are Considered Input Weights? Output Weights? Here, input weights very specifically\nL1349@P22: refer to weights that map from an infinite dimension to a finite dimension. As a counterexample, in\nL1350@P22: some architectures, the first layer can actually map from a finite dimension to another finite dimension,\nL1351@P22: e.g., a PCA layer. Then this is not an “input weight”; if the next layer maps into an infinite dimension,\nL1352@P22: then that’s the input weight. A similar, symmetric discussion applies to output weights.\nL1353@P22: What Counts As a “Model”? Does the MLP in a Transformer Count As a “Model”? For our\nL1354@P22: tables, a model is specifically a function that maps a finite dimension to another finite dimension,\nL1355@P22: consistent with the discussion above. For example, for an image model on CIFAR10, it maps from\nL1356@P22: 3×32×32 = 3072 dimensions to 10 dimensions, and these numbers are fixed regardless of the width\nL1357@P22: of the model. Likewise, for an autoregressive Transformer model, the input and output dimension are\nL1358@P22: both the vocab size, which is independent of the width. This\nL1465@P24: is compatible with Tables 3, 8 and 9.\nL1466@P24: Positional Bias Some Transformers use positional bias (of size contextsize×contextsize, which\nL1467@P24: are added to the attention logits). This\nL1469@P24: is compatible with Tables 3, 8 and 9.\nL1470@P24: Spatial MLPs Recent works [12, 24, 31, 48, 49] on MLP-only architectures in NLP and CV replace\nL1471@P24: the self-attention layer in Transformers with MLPs across tokens or spatial locations. In our language\nL1472@P24: here, such MLPs have finite input and output dimensions (the context size) and infinite hidden\nL1473@P24: dimensions, so their input, output, and hidden weights should be parametrized via the corresponding\nL1474@P24: columns in Tables 3, 8 and 9.\nL1475@P24: B.3 Optimizer Variants and Hyperparameters\nL1476@P24: AdamW Exactly the same as Adam in all of our tables, with the added benefit that weight decay is\nL1477@P24: automatically scaled correctly in AdamW (but is incompatible with µP Adam). For this reason, we\nL1478@P24: recommend using AdamW when weight decay is desired (which is consistent with current standard\nL1479@P24: practice).\nL1480@P24: Frobenius Normalization LARS [63], Adafactor [42], Lamb [64], Layca [8], Fromage [6], Nero\nL1481@P24: [28] all involve a normalization step in which the update g (which may be obtained from SGD,\nL1482@P24: Adam, or other optimzers) is normalized to have Frobenius norm equal to that of the parameter\nL1483@P24: w: g ← kwkF\nL1484@P24: kgkF\nL1485@P24: g. They can be made compatible with µP in Table 8 by scaling their learning rate\nL1486@P24: for hidden weights like 1/\nL1487@P24: √\nL1488@P24: fan_in (for Table 3, the output weight learning rate should be likewise\nL1489@P24: scaled). The intuitive reasoning (which can be formalized straightforwardly using Tensor Programs)\nL1490@P24: is as follows.\nL1491@P24: This normalization implicitly encodes a width scaling: If one initializes a weight matrix with variance\nL1492@P24: 1/fan_in, then an n×n matrix (e.g., a hidden weight matrix) has Frobenius norm √n at initialization.\nL1493@P24: Thus, in the first step and, by induction, in any step t, the normalized update to this n × n weight also\nL1494@P24-25: 25has Frobenius norm Θ(√\nL1495@P25: n) (for any fixed t, as n → ∞). Heuristically, this means each entry of g is\nL1496@P25: approximately of size Θ(1/\nL1497@P25: √\nL1498@P25: n). But, by the derivation of Appendix J, we want Θ(1/n) and this is\nL1499@P25: Θ(√n) too large! Thus, in wide enough networks, one should see a network blowup after one update,\nL1500@P25: like demonstrated in Fig. 5.\nL1501@P25: However, note that the Θ(1/\nL1502@P25: √\nL1503@P25: n) coordinate size induced by the normalization here is closer to the\nL1504@P25: right size Θ(1/n) than Adam, whose update have coordinate size Θ(1). This may partially explain\nL1505@P25: the apparent benefit of these optimizers. In particular, this may explain the observation that T5 [38],\nL1506@P25: using Adafactor, was able to train its entire range of models from 220 million to 11 billion parameters\nL1507@P25: with a fixed set of hyperparameters, while GPT-3 [7], using Adam, needed to decrease its learning\nL1508@P25: rate with model size.\nL1509@P25: RAdam RAdam [25] is a variant of Adam that uses SGD with momentum in an initial stage with\nL1510@P25: learning rate warmup, followed by a second stage of Adam with a particular setting of learning rate\nL1511@P25: with time. Thus, one can adapt RAdam to µP by individually scaling the learning rates of the initial\nL1512@P25: SGD stage and the final Adam stage according to Table 3, Table 8, or Table 9.\nL1513@P25: Adagrad and RMSProp Exactly the same as Adam in all of our tables.\nL1514@P25: \x0f in Adam and Its Variants All of our derivations here assume \x0f is negligible in Adam. If it is set\nL1515@P25: to a non-negligible number, then it needs to be scaled, for all parameters, like 1/fan_in2if it is added\nL1516@P25: before the square root, or like 1/fan_in if it is added after the square root.\nL1517@P25: Gradient Clipping Gradient (`2-norm-wise) clipping is compatible with Table 3 (as well as\nL1518@P25: Tables 8 and 9), for either SGD or Adam, if the clip value is held constant with respect to width.\nL1519@P25: Weight Decay Weight decay should be scaled independently of width in SGD and AdamW, for all\nL1520@P25: of our tables. However, note it’s not compatible with µP Adam.\nL1521@P25: Momentum Momentum should be scaled independently of width for all of our tables.\nL1522@P25: C Parametrization Matters: A Primer for Multiple Hyperparameters\nL1523@P25: Here we give more intuition why we need to reparametrize all hyperparameters. In practice, neural\nL1524@P25: networks have multitudes of hyperparameters all interacting together. In our example of Section 2,\nL1525@P25: hyperparameter optimization would be akin to minimizing the function19\nL1526@P25: Fn(c\nL1527@P25: 1\nL1528@P25: , . . . , ck)\nL1529@P25: def = E\nL1530@P25: x1,...,xn\nL1531@P25: f((c\nL1532@P25: 1 + · · · + ck\nL1533@P25: )(x1 + · · · + xn)).\nL1534@P25: where x1, . . . , xn are as in Eq. (1) and c\nL1535@P25: 1\nL1536@P25: , . . . , ckare analogous to k hyperparameters. For the same\nL1537@P25: reasoning in Section 2, the correct parametrization is in (α\nL1538@P25: 1\nL1539@P25: , . . . , αk) where α\nL1540@P25: i = ci√\nL1541@P25: n.\nL1542@P25: While this is straightforward, in practice, researchers often fix some hyperparameters (e.g., they tune\nL2726@P39: be scaled up and down as well according to Fig. 19 (though note that currently we require users to\nL2727@P39: recreate the base model shape at new depths, since the number of parameters now change with depth).\nL2728@P39: The mup Package We provide our tool as a Python package called mup designed to work with\nL2729@P39: PyTorch. The following example illustrates the usage of our package.\nL2730@P39-40: 40What Happens in the mup Package Under the hood, mup implements the µP formulation in\nL2731@P40: Table 8. By invoking set_base_shape(model, base_model), each parameter tensor p of model\nL2732@P40: gets a p.infshape attribute that stores, for each of its dimensions, the corresponding base dimension\nL2733@P40: and whether that dimension should be considered “infinite” (i.e. will be scaled up/down, e.g.,\nL2734@P40: dmodel of a Transformer) or “finite” (i.e. will be fixed, e.g., vocabulary size). This information\nL2735@P40: is used in the initializers and optimizers to automatically scale the parameters or learning rates\nL2736@P40: to be compliant with µP. For example, by Table 8, the Adam learning rate of hidden weights p\nL2737@P40: is calculated as η/p.infshape.width_mult(), where p.infshape.width_mult() essentially\nL2738@P40: calculates fan_in\nL2739@P40: base_fan_in .\nL2740@P40: I Reverse-µTransfer for Diagnosing Training Instability in Large Models\nL2741@P40: Large Transformers are famously fickle to train [25, 37]. We note that a possible source of this\nL2742@P40: instability for larger transformers is the failure of naive hyperparameter transfer via the standard\nL2743@P40: parametrization. This is certainly consistent with Fig. 1, which shows that the optimal learning\nL2744@P40: rate for small Transformers can lead to trivial performance in large Transformers. We support this\nL2745@P40: hypothesis further by reverse-µTransferring the instability-inducing HPs from a large Transformer to\nL2746@P40: a small one and replicating the training instability. This is shown in Fig. 21.\nL2747@P40: Practically, this reverse-µTransfer technique can be used to diagnose or debug training instability\nL2748@P40: problems of large models. We offer two case studies toward this claim.\nL2749@P40: 1) When training transformers of width 8192 on Wikitext-2, we found certain HP combinations\nL2750@P40: caused divergence in the middle of training. We reverse-µTransferred one such HP combination to\nL2751@P40: a model of width 256 and replicated this divergence. By analyzing this small model’s activations\nL2752@P40: right before this divergence, we found that the cause is due to attention logits blowing up. Note this\nL2753@P40-41: 41debugging session proceeded much more quickly than if we directly worked with the large model.\nL2754@P41: Later we confirmed this is indeed the same cause of the width-8192 model’s divergence.\nL2755@P41: 2) A 6B-parameter language model (in standard parametrization) in a separate project experienced\nL2756@P41: repeated blow-up in the middle of training. We reverse-µTransferred its hyperparameters to a smaller,\nL2757@P41: 100M-parameter model and replicated the training instability. This was solved by a retuning of the\nL3200@P45: For example, for output weights, Table 3 has A = 1, B = 1/fan_in, C = η/fan_in for SGD and\nL3201@P45: Adam. Then taking θ = 1/fan_in, we get the entries in Table 8, with A = 1/fan_in, B = 1,\nL3202@P45: C = η · fan_in for SGD and C = η for Adam. Taking θ = 1/\nL3203@P45: √\nL3204@P45: fan_in instead, we get the entries in\nL3205@P45: Table 9, with A = 1/\nL3206@P45: √\nL3207@P45: fan_in, B = 1/fan_in, C = η for SGD and η/√fan_in for Adam. Similar\nL3208@P45: calculations hold for the input weights scaling in those tables, after taking into consideration that\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn23view2\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: application/pdf; Number of pages: 48; Source: find({"ref_id":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466","pattern":"zero initialization"}); Total lines: 3311\nL1152@P18: 11 Conclusion 13\nL1153@P18: A Parametrization Terminologies 22\nL1154@P18: B Further Explanations of the µP Tables 22\nL1155@P18: B.1 Walkthrough of µP Implementation in a Transformer . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 24\nL1156@P18: B.2 Other Parameters . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 25\nL1157@P18: B.3 Optimizer Variants and Hyperparameters . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 25\nL1158@P18: C Parametrization Matters: A Primer for Multiple Hyperparameters 26\nL1159@P18: D Practical Considerations 26\nL1160@P18: D.1 Verifying µP Implementation via Coordinate Checking . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 27\nL1161@P18: D.2 Zero Initialization for Output Layers and Query Layers in Attention . . . . . . . . 27\nL1162@P18: D.3 Activation Functions . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 27\nL1163@P18-19: 19D.4 Enlarge dk . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 28\nL1164@P19: D.5 Non-Gaussian vs Gaussian Initialization . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 28\nL1165@P19: D.6 Using a Larger Sequence Length . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 28\nL1166@P19: D.7 Tuning Per-Layer Hyperparameters . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 28\nL1167@P19: E Which Hyperparameters Can Be Transferred? The scaling rule for their initialization, learning rate (for both SGD\nL1342@P22: and Adam), and multiplier is very simple: hold them constant with width.\nL1343@P22: Initialization Mean We did not specify the initialization mean in the tables, since most commonly\nL1344@P22: the mean is just set to 0, but it can be nonzero for vector-like parameters (e.g., layernorm weights)\nL1345@P22: and scalar-like parameters but must be 0 for matrix-like parameters.\nL1346@P22: Zero Initialization Variance The initialization scaling rules in our tables can all be trivially satis\x02fied if the initialization variance is set to 0. This can be useful in some settings (e.g., Appendix D.2)\nL1347@P22: but detrimental in other settings (e.g., hidden weights).\nL1348@P22: What Are Considered Input Weights? Output Weights? Here, input weights very specifically\nL1349@P22: refer to weights that map from an infinite dimension to a finite dimension. As a counterexample, in\nL1350@P22: some architectures, the first layer can actually map from a finite dimension to another finite dimension,\nL1351@P22: e.g., a PCA layer. Then this is not an “input weight”; if the next layer maps into an infinite dimension,\nL1352@P22: then that’s the input weight. A similar, symmetric discussion applies to output weights.\nL1353@P22: What Counts As a “Model”? Does the MLP in a Transformer Count As a “Model”? For our\nL1354@P22: tables, a model is specifically a function that maps a finite dimension to another finite dimension,\nL1355@P22: consistent with the discussion above. For example, for an image model on CIFAR10, it maps from\nL1356@P22: 3×32×32 = 3072 dimensions to 10 dimensions, and these numbers are fixed regardless of the width\nL1357@P22: of the model. Likewise, for an autoregressive Transformer model, the input and output dimension are\nL1358@P22: both the vocab size, which is independent of the width. In contrast, an MLP inside a Transformer is\nL1359@P22: not a “model” in this sense because its input and output dimension are both equal to the width of the\nL1360@P22: Transformer.\nL1577@P26: implementation correctness; similarly, we propose coordinate checking to verify the correctness\nL1578@P26: of µP implementation: Exemplified by Fig. 5, one calculates the average coordinate size of every\nL1579@P26: (pre)activation vector in the network over a few steps of training, as width is varied over a large\nL1580@P26: range. An incorrect implementation will see some activation vector blow up or shrink to zero with\nL1581@P26: width (like in the top row of Fig. 5). In the mup package we release with this paper, we include an\nL1582@P26: easy-to-use method for coordinate checking.\nL1583@P26: D.2 Zero Initialization for Output Layers and Query Layers in Attention\nL1584@P26: We find that the optimal hyperparameters of small and large width models match more closely when\nL1585@P26: we initialize output layers at 0 (i.e. with variance σ\nL1586@P26: 2/fan_in where σ = 0 instead of positive σ).\nL1587@P26: This is because the neural network in µP is approximately a Gaussian process (GP) at initialization\nL1588@P26: with variance on the order Θ(σ\nL1589@P26: 2/width) (contrast this with SP networks, which approximates a\nL1590@P26: GP with Θ(σ\nL1591@P26: 2\nL1592@P26: ) variance) [21, 29, 53, 57]. Of course, when width is large, this variance vanishes,\nL1593@P26: but this can be far from so in the small proxy model. This discrepancy in the initial GP can cause\nL1594@P26: the training trajectory of the proxy model to be very different from the trajectory of the large target\nL1595@P26: model, causing a mismatch in the optimal hyperparameters. By initializing the output layer at 0, we\nL1596@P26: remove this mismatch in the initial GP. Empirically we do not find this modification to be detrimental\nL1597@P26: to performance.\nL1598@P26: A similar consideration applies to the query layer in self-attention: At initialization, the attention logit\nL1599@P26: q\nL1600@P26: >k/dhead looks like a Gaussian with variance Θ(1/dhead) because q and k are almost independent\nL1601@P26: and zero-mean. In the limit dhead → ∞, the logit is exactly 0, which can be a large discrepancy\nL1602@P26: compared to when dhead is small in the small proxy model we want to tune. By initializing the\nL1603@P26: query projection matrix Wqto 0, q will also be 0, and hence the attention logit is always 0 at\nL1604@P26: initialization regardless of width (but will generally become nonzero after a gradient step), resolving\nL1605@P26: this discrepancy.\nL1606@P26: More generally, any layer or computation that goes from an “infinite” dimension (i.e. width) to a\n'}, {'type': Copyright 2022 Microsoft Corporation.\nL1: from torch.nn import Linear\nL2: \nL3: \nL4: class MuReadout(Linear):\nL5:     \'\'\'Drop-in replacement for all output linear layers.\nL6: \nL7:     An "output" linear layer is one that maps from a width dimension (e.g.,\nL8:     `d_model` in a Transformer) to a non-width dimension (e.g., vocab size).\nL9:     This layer implements the version of μP with a 1/width multiplier and a\nL10:     constant variance initialization for both weights and biases.\nL11:     \'\'\'\nL12:     def __init__(self, *args, readout_zero_init=False, output_mult=1.0, **kwargs):\nL13:         self.output_mult = output_mult\nL14:         self.readout_zero_init = readout_zero_init\nL15:         super().__init__(*args, **kwargs)\nL16: \nL17:     def reset_parameters(self) -> None:\nL18:         if self.readout_zero_init:\nL19:             self.weight.data[:] = 0\nL20:             if self.bias is not None:\nL21:                 self.bias.data[:] = 0\nL22:         else:\nL23:             super().reset_parameters()\nL24:     def width_mult(self):\nL25:         assert hasattr(self.weight, \'infshape\'), (\nL26:             \'Please call set_base_shapes(...). "\nL40:                 "Unless you know what you are doing, usually you should not be calling `_rescale_parameters` more than once.\\n"\nL41:                 "If you called `set_base_shapes` on a model loaded from a checkpoint, "\nL42:                 "or just want to re-set the base shapes of an existing model, "\nL43:                 "make sure to set the flag `rescale_params=False`.\\n"\nL44:                 "To bypass this error and *still rescale parameters*, set `self._has_rescaled_params=False` before this call.")\nL45:         if self.bias is not None:\nL46:             self.bias.data *= self.width_mult()**0.5\nL47:         self.weight.data *= self.width_mult()**0.5\nL48:         self._has_rescaled_params = True\nL49: \nL50:     def forward(self, x):\nL51:         return super().forward(\nL52:             self.output_mult * x / self.width_mult())\nL53: \nL54: class MuSharedReadout(MuReadout):\nL55:     \'\'\'`MuReadout` with weights shared with an `nn.Embedding` layer.\nL56: \nL57:     Inputs:\nL58:         weight: should be weight of an `nn.Embedding` layer\nL59:         other inputs are fed to `MuReadout`\nL60:     \'\'\'\nL61:     def __init__(self, weight, bias=True, **kwargs):\nL62:         super().__init__(*weight.shape, bias=bias, **kwargs)\nL63:         self.weight = weight\nL64: \nL65: def rescale_linear_bias(linear):\nL66:     \'\'\'Rescale bias in nn.Linear layers to convert SP initialization to μP initialization.\nL67:     Warning: This method is NOT idempotent and should be called only once\nL68:     unless you know what you are doing.\nL69:     \'\'\'\nL70:     if hasattr(linear, \'_has_rescaled_params\') and linear._has_rescaled_params:\nL71:         raise RuntimeError("`rescale_linear_bias` has been called once before already. ## Ordinal 1884 agent_message /root/three_input_activity 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/three_input_activity
Payload:
The positive-parameter extension is proved in [three_input_activity_lemma.md](/tmp/three_input_activity_lemma.md).

With layer multipliers \(\kappa_1,\kappa_2,\kappa_3>0\), define

\[
R_a^\kappa
=
\kappa_2\sum_b y_bQ_{ab}U_b
+
\kappa_1W_0^{(2)}
[\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a].
\]

Then

\[
Z_a^{(1)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(1)}
=
2\kappa_1\kappa_3t^2T_a+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
Z_a^{(2)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(2)}
=
2\kappa_3t^2R_a^\kappa+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

and both leading coefficients are nonzero for every input.

Thus each hidden RMS speed is bounded above and below by positive constants times \(t\), for sufficiently small \(t>0\). It survives the width/step limits at each fixed positive time, but is zero initially and is not uniformly bounded below on \((0,T_*]\).

The physical kernel is

\[
K=\kappa_1K^{(1)}+\kappa_2K^{(2)}+\kappa_3K^{(3)},
\]

and

\[
y^\top K(t)y
=
\kappa_3\mathbb E[S^2]
+
8\kappa_3^2(\kappa_1A_1+\kappa_2A_2)t^2
+
o(t^2).
\]

Each block is individually positive definite and nonconstant for sufficiently small positive time.

In contrast to the hidden speed, loss descent has a uniform positive slope already at initialization:

\[
-\dot L(t)\ge 2\kappa_3\mathbb E[S^2]>0
\]

on a short common interval.

All six hidden best-affine-fit errors for \(\arctan\), and all six hidden variances, have uniform positive lower bounds on that interval, including time zero.

Positive \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2\) preserve these conclusions. At zero, \(\sigma_1=0\), \(\sigma_2=0\), or \(\kappa_3=0\) freezes this zero-readout population initialization. Setting \(\kappa_1=0\) freezes the first hidden layer; setting \(\kappa_2=0\) freezes the middle matrix but need not freeze either hidden preactivation. If both are zero, readout-only learning has a constant kernel.

All constants may depend on the fixed geometry and hyperparameters; none depends on width or step size.

## Ordinal 1890 agent_message /root/two_input_full_audit_b 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/two_input_full_audit_b
Payload:
The precise description is **“μP SGD update scaling with vanishing-readout initialization.”** The learning-rate exponents match μP; the positive-variance textbook initialization does not.

In the existing stored-weight notation:

| Quantity | Current model | Positive-variance μP |
|---|---:|---:|
| Initialization SD of \(W^{(1)}\) | \(1\) | \(\Theta(1)\) |
| Initialization SD of \(W^{(2)}\) | \(n^{-1/2}\) | \(\Theta(n^{-1/2})\) |
| Initialization SD of stored \(W^{(3)}\), with explicit output factor \(1/n\) | \(n^{-1}\) | \(\Theta(1)\) |
| Initialization SD of actual final coefficient \(W^{(3)}/n\) | \(n^{-2}\) | \(\Theta(n^{-1})\) |
| SGD multipliers on stored weights | \((n,1,n)\) | \((n,1,n)\), up to fixed constants |

Microsoft’s [`MuReadout`](https://github.com/microsoft/mup/blob/main/mup/layer.py) explicitly implements constant-variance stored output weights accompanied by a \(1/\text{width}\) multiplier. [`MuSGD`](https://github.com/microsoft/mup/blob/main/mup/optim.py) multiplies the learning rate of parameters having one growing dimension by the width multiplier; square hidden matrices retain a constant multiplier.

The forward factors must also enter the SGD calculation. In particular,
\[
\Delta(W^{(3)}/n)
=-\frac{\eta_n}{n}\nabla_{W^{(3)}/n}L.
\]
For the three actual forward coefficients \(W^{(1)}/\sqrt d,\ W^{(2)},\ W^{(3)}/n\), the induced learning rates are
\[
\left(\frac{\eta_n n}{d},\ \eta_n,\ \frac{\eta_n}{n}\right).
\]
The fixed \(d\)-factor does not change width exponents.

[Tensor Programs V, Table 8 and Lemma J.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466) give these equivalent parameter conventions and the squared multiplier adjustment for SGD. Its Definition A.2 also distinguishes width-scaling rules from numerical hyperparameter choices.

Clock bookkeeping does not remove the initialization discrepancy. Algebraically,
\[
\eta_n(n,1,n)=(n\eta_n)(1,n^{-1},1).
\]
Thus the middle stored-weight learning rate is \(1/n\) times the input/readout rate. If the displayed base step becomes \(n\eta_n\), retain the original physical clock as \(t=k(n\eta_n)/n\).

The initialization distinction has dynamical content. Standard positive-variance μP also has predictions tending to zero initially, but its stored readout has a nonzero limiting coordinate distribution and generally nonzero initial hidden velocities. Here the limiting readout itself is zero, producing the proved \(t^2\) onset of hidden displacement. Exact zero-readout initialization is explicitly supported and recommended in the [official repository](https://github.com/microsoft/mup/blob/main/README.md#making-your-own-coord-check-plots).

A broader constant family can retain
\[
\operatorname{SD}(W^{(1)}_0)=\sigma_1,\quad
\operatorname{SD}(W^{(2)}_0)=\frac{\sigma_2}{\sqrt n},\quad
\operatorname{SD}(W^{(3)}_0)=\frac{\sigma_3}{n},
\]
and multipliers \((\lambda_1n,\lambda_2,\lambda_3n)\), with fixed positive \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\lambda_\ell\) and finite \(\sigma_3\ge0\). These preserve the displayed exponents; different constants generally change the limiting dynamics. Their inclusion in a convergence theorem should be proved explicitly.

[Tensor Programs IV, Definition 4.1](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c.pdf) supplies the original μP exponent convention. Its fixed-step theory and μTransfer results should not be presented as proving this model’s joint \(\eta_n\to0\), \(n\to\infty\) theorem.

Suggested wording: “The model uses μP’s SGD width scaling, with a smaller readout initialization that converges to zero. The local convergence theorem establishes the limit for this specified initialization and training clock.”

## Ordinal 1892 agent_message /root/three_input_limit 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/three_input_limit
Payload:
Verified sufficient family:

\[
W_{0,ij}^{(1)}\sim N(0,\sigma_1^2),\qquad
W_{0,ji}^{(2)}\sim N(0,\sigma_2^2/n),\qquad
W_{0,j}^{(3)}\sim N(0,\sigma_3^2n^{-2\beta}),
\]

with finite \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2>0\), \(\sigma_3\ge0\), and any \(\beta>0\), including exact zero readout. Layer learning multipliers may be

\[
n\kappa_{1,n},\qquad \kappa_{2,n},\qquad n\kappa_{3,n},
\qquad
\kappa_{\ell,n}\to\kappa_\ell\in(0,\infty).
\]

Every positive \(\eta_n\to0\) still works. Detailed verification: [THREE_INPUT_SCALED_LIMIT_EXTENSION_CHECK.md](/tmp/THREE_INPUT_SCALED_LIMIT_EXTENSION_CHECK.md).

Critical corrections:

- Population first-layer covariance is \(\sigma_1^2G\).
- Both initial-matrix Gaussian covariance formulas **and both derivative-response sums** acquire \(\sigma_2^2\).
- Learned rank-one terms acquire \(\kappa_2\), not \(\sigma_2^2\).
- The prediction kernel is \(\kappa_1K^{(1)}+\kappa_2K^{(2)}+\kappa_3K^{(3)}\).
- Converging \(\kappa_{\ell,n}\) add only a vanishing \(C\sum_\ell|\kappa_{\ell,n}-\kappa_\ell|\) comparison error.
- Every \(\beta>0\) gives both readout RMS convergence to zero and supremum convergence to zero. Thus the limiting initialization is unchanged; \(\beta,\sigma_3\) need not enter the population \(T_0\).

Separately, **bounded nonvanishing iid readout also admits local existence/convergence**, using the same sampled readout in the oracle. The bootstrap changes from \(\mathcal A=O(T)\) to \(\mathcal A=O(1+B_0)\), while \(\mathcal C=O(T)\); a sufficiently small positive \(T_0\) still closes it. Old feature-motion expansions must then be redone. Unbounded \(O(1)\) Gaussian readout is not established by that bounded-readout argument.

## Ordinal 1896 message assistant commentary
I’m using the teaching and rigorous-math skills to separate what the proof establishes from the comparison with μP. The scaling check gives a broader family: positive, width-independent initialization and learning-rate constants can vary, and the readout can shrink at any positive power of width. The zero-readout initialization remains an important part of the result.

The construction of the common bounded action and its adjoint is unchanged.\n\nHere is the exact correction to the response formulas. Keep \\(A,C\\) defined as expectations of derivatives with respect to the actual Gaussian slots. Their covariances now are\n\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[\\xi_{a,k}\\xi_{b,s}]\n=\\sigma_2^2\\mathbb E[H_{a,k}^{(1)}H_{b,s}^{(1)}],\n\\qquad\n\\mathbb E[\\eta_{a,k}\\eta_{b,s}]\n=\\sigma_2^2\\mathbb E[\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}].\n\\]\n\nBoth derivative-response sums acquire a factor \\(\\sigma_2^2\\):\n\n\\[\nW_0^{(2)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}\n=\\xi_{a,k}+\\sigma_2^2\\sum_{b,s<k}C_{ak,bs}\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)},\n\\]\n\n\\[\n(W_0^{(2)})^*\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\n=\\eta_{a,k}+\\sigma_2^2\\sum_{b,s\\le k}A_{ak,bs}H_{b,s}^{(1)}.\n\\]\n\nConsequently, the two complete recursions are\n\n\\[\nZ_{a,k}^{(2)}=\\xi_{a,k}+\n\\sum_{b,s<k}\n\\left(\\sigma_2^2C_{ak,bs}-2\\kappa_2\\Delta r_{b,s}\n\\mathbb E[H_{b,s}^{(1)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}]\\right)\n\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)},\n\\]\n\n\\[\nP_{a,k}=\\eta_{a,k}+\n\\sum_{b,s\\le k}\n\\left(\\sigma_2^2A_{ak,bs}\n-\\mathbf1_{s<k}2\\kappa_2\\Delta r_{b,s}\n\\mathbb E[\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}]\\right)\nH_{b,s}^{(1)}.\n\\]\n\nThe variance factor multiplies the initial-matrix responses, not the trained rank-one memory.\n\nThe readout estimate is now\n\n\\[\nB_{k+1}\\le B_k+6\\kappa_3\\Delta\\|\\phi\\|_\\infty\n(1+\\|\\phi\\|_\\infty B_k),\\qquad B_0=0.\n\\]\n\nThus \\(B_k\\le C_0T\\), \\(|r_{a,k}|\\le C_0\\), and \\(|\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}|\\le C_0T\\), with constants depending on \\(\\kappa_3\\). The forward and backward training-memory row sums remain \\(O(T)\\) and \\(O(T^3)\\). No lower eigenvalue of \\(G\\) enters.\n\nThe localization, Euler Cauchy, existence, uniqueness, autonomy, oracle and actual-GD comparison arguments are therefore unchanged. They use finite upper bounds for the readout supremum and matrix operator norm. For the actual initial readout, for every \\(\\varepsilon>0\\) and \\(\\sigma_3>0\\),\n\n\\[\n\\mathbb P(\\|W_{n,0}^{(3)}\\|_\\infty>\\varepsilon)\n\\le2n\\exp[-\\varepsilon^2n^{2\\beta}/(2\\sigma_3^2)]\\to0,\n\\]\n\nand\n\n\\[\n\\mathbb E\\left[\\|W_{n,0}^{(3)}\\|_2^2/n\\right]\n=\\sigma_3^2n^{-2\\beta}\\to0.\n\\]\n\nThe oracle can therefore still start from zero readout. Its initialization discrepancy vanishes, and the actual readout supremum is bounded with probability tending to one. These are precisely the two initialization obligations in the original bridge. The physical parameter-speed identities include the squared learning multipliers:\n\n\\[\n\\|\\dot W^{(1)}\\|_F^2/n=4\\kappa_1^2r^TK^{(1)}r,\n\\quad\n\\|\\dot W^{(2)}\\|_F^2=4\\kappa_2^2r^TK^{(2)}r,\n\\quad\n\\|\\dot W^{(3)}\\|_2^2/n=4\\kappa_3^2r^TK^{(3)}r.\n\\]\n\nAccordingly \\(-\\dot L\\) is the sum of these three quantities divided by their respective \\(\\kappa_\\ell\\).\n\n## Converging learning-rate constants\n\nThe same theorem allows \\(\\kappa_{\\ell,n}\\to\\kappa_\\ell\\in(0,\\infty)\\). The sequences are eventually uniformly bounded. In the oracle comparison, replacing the actual coefficients by their limits adds a vector-field forcing bounded by\n\n\\[\nC\\sum_{\\ell=1}^3|\\kappa_{\\ell,n}-\\kappa_\\ell|.\n\\]\n\nThe bound follows from the already proved uniform mean-square bounds on each component velocity; it requires no extra differentiability or finite-coordinate tail estimate. The comparison bracket therefore becomes\n\n\\[\n\\|W_{n,0}^{(3)}\\|_2/\\sqrt n\n+(1+R)(\\eta_n+\\Delta)\n+\\sum_\\ell|\\kappa_{\\ell,n}-\\kappa_\\ell|\n+e^{-cR^2}+o_{\\mathbb P}(1).\n\\]\n\nAll terms vanish in the existing order of limits. No convergence rate for \\(\\kappa_{\\ell,n}\\) is required. The same coupling argument would also permit converging positive \\(\\sigma_{1,n},\\sigma_{2,n}\\), with an additional vanishing initialization discrepancy, but fixed scales are already a sufficient family.\n\n## Separate bounded, nonvanishing readout extension\n\nSuppose instead the initial readout coordinates are iid copies of an independent random variable \\(W_0^{(3)}\\) with \\(|W_0^{(3)}|\\le B_0<\\infty\\). Keep that same initialization in the finite oracle and in the population state. The local existence/convergence argument still closes, but the limiting system and its small-time expansion are different.\n\nFor the finite-program step, a bounded scalar root can be represented as a bounded measurable function of an independent Gaussian root, using its quantile function. All response derivatives are taken with respect to forward/backward Gaussian slots, not that initial-root coordinate. Equivalently one can use the finite Gaussian conditioning theorem with an independent bounded root. Fixed-program moments and the needed response derivatives remain valid.\n\nThe changed estimates are\n\n\\[\n\\|W_k^{(3)}\\|_\\infty,\\ |\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}|\n\\le C(B_0+T),\\qquad\n\\mathbb E\\eta_{a,k}^2\\le C(B_0+T)^2.\n\\]\n\nThe backward learned-memory row sum is now \\(O(T)\\), not \\(O(T^3)\\). Differentiating the readout still contributes \\(CTV_k\\), since its independent initial root has zero derivative in every forward Gaussian slot. Differentiating the second-layer backpropagated field contributes at most \\(C(B_0+T)V_k\\). Thus\n\n\\[\n\\mathcal C_k\\le CT\n\\exp[CT(1+\\mathcal A_{k-1})+CT^2],\n\\]\n\n\\[\nV_k\\le\\max\\{V_{k-1},\n1+C(B_0+T)(\\mathcal C_k+CT)V_{k-1}\\},\n\\qquad\n\\mathcal A_k\\le C(B_0+T)V_k.\n\\]\n\nTake an \\(O(1+B_0)\\) cap for \\(\\mathcal A_k\\), an \\(O(T)\\) cap for \\(\\mathcal C_k\\), and choose \\(T_0>0\\) so the coefficient of \\(V_{k-1}\\) in the second alternative is at most \\(1/2\\). The same causal induction gives \\(V_k\\le2\\), bounded response row sums, and a mesh-uniform Gaussian tail for \\(P_{a,k}\\). All existence/convergence bridges follow.
