With a nonzero initial readout, hidden training usually begins at order \\(t\\). Those strict conclusions require a separate calculation. Also, unbounded \\(O(1)\\) Gaussian initial readout is not covered by this bounded-readout proof; its supremum and population essential supremum are unbounded, so additional localization is needed.\n\nThe safe primary claim is therefore: arbitrary positive finite first/second initialization scales, any polynomially vanishing Gaussian rescaled readout, and positive finite converging layer learning multipliers preserve the full local-limit proof; bounded nonvanishing iid readout preserves local existence/convergence by the explicitly modified bootstrap, but is a different initial-state theorem.\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': "# Three-input activity lemma, including rank-two input Gram matrices\n\nAssume the local autonomous population flow and its mean-square continuity have been established with the same equations as the two-input flow, with all input sums now over a=1,2,3. Let x_a/sqrt(d) have norm one, and assume x_a and x_b are never parallel or antiparallel. Labels y_a are arbitrary signs. The input Gram matrix G is positive semidefinite with diagonal one; it need not be invertible.\n\nWrite the first-layer Gaussian root as Z_{0,a}^{(1)}=w^T x_a/sqrt(d), w~N(0,I_d), and H_{0,a}^{(1)}=atan(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}). Define Q_ab=E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}].\n\n## Q is positive definite even when G is singular\n\nIf a linear combination of these arctangents vanishes almost surely, continuity and the full support of w make it vanish for every w. Choose v outside the finitely many proper hyperplanes defined by v^T x_a=0 and v^T(x_a±x_b)=0. This is possible by pairwise nonparallelity. The numbers b_a=v^T x_a/sqrt(d) are nonzero and have distinct squares. Restrict the identity to w=t v. The coefficients of t,t^3,t^5 in the arctangent Taylor series give\n\n    sum_a c_a b_a (b_a^2)^k = 0, k=0,1,2.\n\nThe Vandermonde determinant is nonzero, so c_a b_a=0 for every a, hence c=0. Thus Q is positive definite.\n\nConsequently Y_a:=Z_{0,a}^{(2)} are a full-support centered Gaussian triple with covariance Q. Define\n\n    S = sum_a y_a atan(Y_a),\n    U_a = S phi'(Y_a),\n    P_a = (W_0^{(2)})^* U_a,\n    B_a = phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}) P_a,\n    V_ab = E[U_a U_b],    D_ab = E[B_a B_b].\n\nThe event S=0 has probability zero: conditional on Y_2,Y_3, S is a strictly monotone function of Y_1, and Y_1 has a continuous conditional density. If a linear combination of U_a vanishes, division by S and full support imply sum_a c_a/(1+Y_a^2)=0 everywhere. Varying one coordinate at a time forces each c_a=0. Thus V is positive definite.\n\nThe first reused transpose call has the Gaussian conditioning representation\n\n    P_a = sum_c H_{0,c}^{(1)} [Q^{-1} E(Y U_a)]_c + Gamma_a,\n\nwhere Gamma~N(0,V) is independent of the first-layer Gaussian root. Therefore the conditional covariance of B given the first-layer root is\n\n    diag(phi'(Z_0^{(1)})) V diag(phi'(Z_0^{(1)})),\n\nwhich is positive definite everywhere. It follows that D is positive definite.\n\nDefine\n\n    T_a = sum_b G_ab y_b B_b,\n    A_a = phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}) T_a,\n    R_a = sum_b y_b Q_ab U_b + W_0^{(2)} A_a.\n\nThe first-layer t^2 coefficient T_a is nonzero in mean square for every a. Indeed its coefficient of Gamma_a conditional on the roots includes G_aa y_a phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}), which is nonzero; positive definiteness of V makes its conditional variance positive.\n\n## Each second-layer coefficient is nonzero; no permutation symmetry is needed\n\nLet alpha_a=Q^{-1}E[H_0^{(1)}A_a], and subtract the projection onto the three initial forward inputs:\n\n    A_a^perp = A_a - sum_c alpha_{a,c} H_{0,c}^{(1)},\n    sigma_a^2 = E[(A_a^perp)^2].\n\nConditional on the first-layer roots, A_a is a linear function of Gamma with coefficients\n\n    c_b = phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}) G_ab y_b phi'(Z_{0,b}^{(1)}).\n\nIn particular c_a=y_a phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})^2 is nonzero. Thus\n\n    sigma_a^2 >= E Var(A_a | first-layer roots) = E[c^T V c] > 0.\n\nCondition the initial matrix on all three already revealed forward calls W_0^{(2)}H_{0,c}^{(1)}=Y_c and all three transpose calls (W_0^{(2)})^*U_b=P_b. The next forward call has law\n\n    W_0^{(2)}A_a = sum_c alpha_{a,c}Y_c + sum_b beta_{a,b}U_b + sigma_a gamma_a,\n    beta_a = V^{-1} E[P A_a^perp],\n\nwhere gamma_a is standard Gaussian independent of the second-layer history Y (and therefore of U). This follows directly from the Gaussian matrix conditional mean and its independent doubly projected residual; the fixed-rank projection of a fresh Gaussian output disappears in normalized mean square. The Gram matrices Q,V are positive definite, so the displayed inverse formulas are legitimate. A separate such call suffices for each a; joint independence of different gamma_a is not claimed.\n\nThe other terms in R_a are functions of Y. Consequently\n\n    E[R_a^2] >= sigma_a^2 > 0.\n\nThis establishes each input's second-hidden-layer activity without the two-input proof's swap argument.\n\n## Small-time expansion and strict conclusions\n\nSubstitution in the integral equations, using bounded activation derivatives and the bounded initial action, gives\n\n    W^{(3)}(t) = 2t S + o_L2(t),\n    delta_a^{(2)}(t) = 2t U_a + o_L2(t),\n    Z_a^{(1)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(1)} = 2t^2 T_a + o_L2(t^2),\n    W^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)} = 2t^2 sum_b y_b U_b tensor H_{0,b}^{(1)} + o_op(t^2),\n    Z_a^{(2)}(t)-Y_a = 2t^2 R_a + o_L2(t^2).\n\nThe corresponding velocities are 4t T_a+o_L2(t) and 4t R_a+o_L2(t). Hence each hidden squared displacement has positive leading coefficient 4E[T_a^2] or 4E[R_a^2] times t^4, and each integrated squared hidden speed has positive leading coefficient (16/3)E[T_a^2] or (16/3)E[R_a^2] times t^3.\n\nSet A_1=y^T(G entrywise-times D)y and A_2=y^T(Q entrywise-times V)y. Both are strictly positive. In particular the first conclusion does not need G positive definite: if D>=lambda I, diagonalizing G as sum_j v_j v_j^T gives\n\n    G entrywise-times D = sum_j diag(v_j)D diag(v_j) >= lambda diag(G) = lambda I.\n\nLikewise Q entrywise-times V is positive definite. Adjunction gives\n\n    sum_a y_a E[U_a R_a] = A_1+A_2 > 0.\n\nThus the first and second kernel blocks are\n\n    K^{(1)}(t)=4t^2(G entrywise-times D)+o(t^2),\n    K^{(2)}(t)=4t^2(Q entrywise-times V)+o(t^2),\n\nand both are positive definite for sufficiently small positive t. The initial readout block E[atan(Y)atan(Y)^T] is positive definite by the full support of Y, so it remains positive definite for small t. The total kernel satisfies\n\n    y^T K(t)y = y^T K^{(3)}(0)y + 8(A_1+A_2)t^2 + o(t^2),\n\nso it is nonconstant. The loss starts at three and has derivative -4y^T K^{(3)}(0)y<0, hence strictly decreases on a sufficiently short interval.\n\nEvery initial hidden marginal is a nondegenerate Gaussian. Its variance is positive, and atan cannot agree with an affine function on its full support. Thus its best-affine-fit error is positive. Mean-square continuity, and the variance/covariance formula for this fit, preserve these facts on a sufficiently small common interval.\n\nThe same proof works for any fixed finite number of pairwise nonparallel normalized inputs; for m inputs the Vandermonde argument uses the first m odd Taylor coefficients.\n\n## Positive initialization and learning-rate constants\n\nLet the first-weight entries initially have variance sigma_1^2>0 and the second-weight entries variance sigma_2^2/n, with sigma_2>0. Let the three fixed positive layer multipliers be kappa_1,kappa_2,kappa_3, so the population equations are\n\n    dot Z_a^{(1)} = -2 kappa_1 sum_b G_ab r_b delta_b^{(1)},\n    dot W^{(2)} = -2 kappa_2 sum_b r_b delta_b^{(2)} tensor H_b^{(1)},\n    dot W^{(3)} = -2 kappa_3 sum_b r_b H_b^{(2)}.\n\nThe readout initialization tends to zero. All constants are fixed independently of width and step size.\n\nThe first roots now have covariance sigma_1^2 G. Their activation Gram matrix Q remains positive definite by the same Vandermonde argument. The second Gaussian root has covariance sigma_2^2 Q and still has full support. Use the preceding definitions S,U,P,B,T,V,D and A_1,A_2 under this changed initial law. The physical kernel is the weighted sum\n\n    K = kappa_1 K^{(1)} + kappa_2 K^{(2)} + kappa_3 K^{(3)},\n\nnot the unweighted sum. Its first two blocks have expansions\n\n    kappa_1 K^{(1)}(t) = 4 kappa_1 kappa_3^2 t^2 (G entrywise-times D) + o(t^2),\n    kappa_2 K^{(2)}(t) = 4 kappa_2 kappa_3^2 t^2 (Q entrywise-times V) + o(t^2).\n\nThe readout block has label-direction expansion\n\n    y^T K^{(3)}(t)y = E[S^2] + 4 kappa_3 t^2 (kappa_1 A_1+kappa_2 A_2) + o(t^2).\n\nConsequently\n\n    y^T K(t)y = kappa_3 E[S^2]\n                 + 8 kappa_3^2 (kappa_1 A_1+kappa_2 A_2)t^2 + o(t^2).\n\nAll three weighted blocks are positive definite for sufficiently small positive time, and the total kernel is nonconstant.\n\n### Exact size and meaning of nontrivial hidden motion\n\nThe mean-square velocity limits are\n\n    dot Z_a^{(1)}(t)/t -> 4 kappa_1 kappa_3 T_a,\n    dot Z_a^{(2)}(t)/t -> 4 kappa_3 R_a^kappa.\n\nHence for some positive constants c_{ell,a},C_{ell,a} and common positive T_*,\n\n    c_{ell,a} t <= sqrt(E|dot Z_a^{(ell)}(t)|^2) <= C_{ell,a} t,\n    0<t<=T_*.\n\nIn particular the RMS hidden speed is positive for each fixed t>0 in the interval and survives the width/step limits. It is not a time-independent positive speed: it equals zero initially and vanishes linearly as t tends to zero. A positive uniform speed lower bound holds on [tau,T_*] for every fixed tau>0, not on (0,T_*].\n\nThe squared first-layer displacement and integrated squared speed are respectively\n\n    4 kappa_1^2 kappa_3^2 E[T_a^2] t^4 + o(t^4),\n    (16/3) kappa_1^2 kappa_3^2 E[T_a^2] t^3 + o(t^3).\n\nFor the second layer replace kappa_1^2 E[T_a^2] by E[(R_a^kappa)^2].\n\nBy contrast, the loss slope is already nonzero at initialization:\n\n    -dot L(0) = 4 kappa_3 E[S^2] > 0.\n\nContinuity allows the common interval to be reduced so that\n\n    -dot L(t) >= 2 kappa_3 E[S^2] > 0, 0<=t<=T_*.\n\nOnly the summed loss is asserted to decrease; the individual residual magnitudes need not all decrease.\n\nFor the nonlinear claim define the best affine-fit error\n\n    E_aff(Z) = inf_{a,b} E[(phi(Z)-a Z-b)^2]\n             = Var(phi(Z)) - Cov(Z,phi(Z))^2/Var(Z)\n\nwhenever Var(Z)>0. Every initial hidden marginal is a nondegenerate Gaussian, and atan is not affine on its full support, so the six initial values are positive. Mean-square continuity gives a common short interval with\n\n    E_aff(Z_a^{(ell)}(t)) >= (1/2) min_{b,j} E_aff(Z_{0,b}^{(j)}) > 0,\n\nand with each hidden variance at least half the minimum initial hidden variance. These are genuine bounds uniform over time including zero, but they depend on the fixed geometry and positive hyperparameters.\n\n### What fails at zero parameters\n\nIf kappa_3=0, the zero limiting readout stays zero, all backpropagated fields remain zero, and the entire system is frozen. If sigma_1=0 or sigma_2=0, the second-layer activation is initially zero; with zero limiting readout there is no seed for its update and the entire population system is again frozen.\n\nIf kappa_1=0, the first hidden layer is fixed and its weighted kernel block is zero. If kappa_2=0, the second weight matrix is fixed and its weighted kernel block is zero, although the second hidden preactivation can still move through the moving first-layer activation. With kappa_1=0 but kappa_2,kappa_3>0, each second hidden preactivation still moves: its coefficient is kappa_2 S sum_b y_b Q_ab phi'(Y_b), which cannot vanish almost surely by full support and the linear independence of the coordinate derivative functions. If kappa_1=kappa_2=0, only readout training remains and the total kernel is constant.\n\nStrict positivity for every fixed positive choice does not imply a lower bound uniform as a hyperparameter approaches zero or as the allowed input configurations approach a degenerate boundary. The existence time and all quantitative nontriviality constants may depend on those fixed choices, but not on width or step size.\n"}]

For input \\(a=1,2,3\\), use\n\\[\nz_a^{(1)}=\\frac{W^{(1)}x_a}{\\sqrt d},\\quad\nh_a^{(1)}=\\phi(z_a^{(1)}),\\quad\nz_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)},\\quad\nh_a^{(2)}=\\phi(z_a^{(2)}),\\qquad \\phi=\\arctan,\n\\]\n\\[\nf_{n,a}=\\frac{(W^{(3)})^\\top h_a^{(2)}}n,\\qquad\nr_{n,a}=f_{n,a}-y_a,\\qquad L_n=\\sum_{a=1}^3r_{n,a}^2.\n\\]\nThe summed loss retains the earlier factors of two; using the average of the three losses divides the speed by three.\n\nAll initialization entries are independent:\n\\[\nW_{0,ij}^{(1)}\\sim N(0,1),\\quad\nW_{0,ji}^{(2)}\\sim N(0,1/n),\\quad\nW_{0,j}^{(3)}\\sim N(0,1/n^2).\n\\]\nConsequently each first-layer root triple is \\(N(0,G)\\), independently over neuron index \\(i\\). The three preactivations for one neuron may be correlated and may satisfy an exact linear relation. This is permitted.\n\nSet\n\\[\n\\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\\odot\\phi'(z_a^{(2)}),\\qquad\n\\delta_a^{(1)}=\\phi'(z_a^{(1)})\\odot(W^{(2)})^\\top\\delta_a^{(2)}.\n\\]\nThus \\(\\delta_a^{(\\ell)}=n\\,\\partial f_{n,a}/\\partial z_a^{(\\ell)}\\).\nIn the displayed rescaled parameterization the layer learning-rate multipliers are \\(n,1,n\\). The exact first-weight update is\n\\[\nW_{k+1}^{(1)}\n=W_k^{(1)}-\\frac{2\\eta_n}{\\sqrt d}\n\\sum_{b=1}^3r_{n,b,k}\\delta_{b,k}^{(1)}x_b^\\top.\n\\]\nMultiplication by \\(x_a/\\sqrt d\\) gives\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\nz_{a,k+1}^{(1)}\n&=z_{a,k}^{(1)}\n-2\\eta_n\\sum_bG_{ab}r_{n,b,k}\\delta_{b,k}^{(1)},\\\\\nW_{k+1}^{(2)}\n&=W_k^{(2)}-\\frac{2\\eta_n}{n}\n\\sum_b r_{n,b,k}\\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}(h_{b,k}^{(1)})^\\top,\\\\\nW_{k+1}^{(3)}\n&=W_k^{(3)}-2\\eta_n\\sum_b r_{n,b,k}h_{b,k}^{(2)} .\n\\end{aligned}\\tag{M1}\n\\]\nUse physical time \\(t=k\\eta_n\\), linearly interpolate these parameters, and recompute the forward pass at intermediate times.\n\nThe whole effect of input geometry on first-layer training is \\(G\\): training on input \\(b\\) moves input \\(a\\)'s preactivation with coefficient \\(G_{ab}\\). If \\(Gc=0\\), then \\(\\sum_a c_a z_a^{(1)}=0\\) initially and every update preserves this relation. No inverse of \\(G\\) is used.\n\nThe finite continuous flow has the kernel blocks\n\\[\nK^{(1)}_{n,ab}\n=G_{ab}\\frac{(\\delta_a^{(1)})^\\top\\delta_b^{(1)}}n,\\quad\nK^{(2)}_{n,ab}\n=\\frac{(h_a^{(1)})^\\top h_b^{(1)}}n\n \\frac{(\\delta_a^{(2)})^\\top\\delta_b^{(2)}}n,\\quad\nK^{(3)}_{n,ab}\n=\\frac{(h_a^{(2)})^\\top h_b^{(2)}}n .\n\\tag{M2}\n\\]\nDifferentiation gives \\(\\dot f_n=-2K_n r_n\\) and \\(\\dot L_n=-4r_n^\\top K_n r_n\\), where \\(K_n\\) is the sum of the blocks. Each block is positive semidefinite: it is the Gram matrix of the corresponding scaled parameter gradients. Individual residual magnitudes need not decrease; their squared sum does.\n\n## Why a new stability estimate is needed\n\nWith \\(F(s)=s+s^3/3\\), the componentwise transformation \\(X_a^{(1)}=F(Z_a^{(1)})\\) gives\n\\[\n\\dot X_a^{(1)}\n=-2\\sum_bG_{ab}r_b\n\\frac{1+(Z_a^{(1)})^2}{1+(Z_b^{(1)})^2}\n(W^{(2)})^*\\delta_b^{(2)}.\n\\tag{M3}\n\\]\nIf the inputs are mutually orthogonal, only diagonal terms occur and the ratios cancel. Otherwise nonzero cross-input terms remain and their ratios are unbounded. We therefore work with the original preactivations and prove a uniform tail estimate for the backward fields.\n\nThe exact limiting equations will be\n\\[\nH_a^{(1)}=\\phi(Z_a^{(1)}),\\quad\nZ_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}H_a^{(1)},\\quad\nH_a^{(2)}=\\phi(Z_a^{(2)}),\\quad\nf_a=\\mathbb E[W^{(3)}H_a^{(2)}],\\quad r_a=f_a-y_a,\n\\]\n\\[\n\\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\\phi'(Z_a^{(2)}),\\qquad\n\\delta_a^{(1)}=\\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\\delta_a^{(2)},\n\\]\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\n\\dot Z_a^{(1)}&=-2\\sum_bG_{ab}r_b\\delta_b^{(1)},\\\\\n\\dot W^{(2)}&=-2\\sum_b r_b\\delta_b^{(2)}\\otimes H_b^{(1)},\\\\\n\\dot W^{(3)}&=-2\\sum_b r_bH_b^{(2)}.\n\\end{aligned}\\tag{M4}\n\\]\nHere \\((u\\otimes v)V=u\\,\\mathbb E[vV]\\). This definition displays the normalization that replaces the finite factor \\(1/n\\). First-layer and second-layer coordinates belong to separate populations. \\(W^{(2)}\\) acts from the former to the latter, and its adjoint acts in reverse. Every expectation pairs coordinates of the same population.\n\nThe initial root triple is \\(N(0,G)\\), the population readout is zero, and the initial action \\(W_0^{(2)}\\) is the joint forward-and-transpose limit of the initial Gaussian matrix, constructed next. The state consists of three first-layer fields and the shared matrix action and readout field. It has finitely many kinds of state objects, not finitely many scalar degrees of freedom.\n\n## The fixed-mesh construction and the new tail lemma\n\nWe use the following finite Gaussian fact. For a fixed finite calculation from iid jointly Gaussian coordinate tuples, independent of a Gaussian matrix of entry variance \\(1/n\\), with polynomially bounded coordinate functions and finitely many calls to that matrix and its transpose, every same-layer joint empirical average of a continuous polynomially bounded measurement has a deterministic limit. The limiting action is Gaussian randomness plus the responses forced by earlier opposite-direction calls. The precise response coefficients and their derivative convention are given and used in the appendix below. This is the specialization of Theorem 2.10 and Box 1 of [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685). Its hypotheses hold here: after fixing population scalar coefficients, each instruction is polynomially bounded, and the training instructions whose derivatives are used below are smooth with polynomially bounded derivatives, as functions of finitely many Gaussian variables. The initial tuple covariance may be singular; represent it as a fixed linear image of independent Gaussian roots. No claim of iid finite coordinates after matrix reuse is used.\n\nFor completeness, the underlying conditioning mechanism is as follows. If previous calls have revealed \\(W_0^{(2)}V=Y\\) and \\((W_0^{(2)})^\\top U=R\\), then the remaining Gaussian matrix is an independent Gaussian projected onto the directions orthogonal to the columns of \\(U\\) and \\(V\\). Its conditional mean obeys both recorded equations. A new product is the response fixed by these equations plus that projected Gaussian applied to the unexplored part of the new input. Gaussian integration by parts identifies the response coefficients. The finite Gaussian theorem makes this induction valid also with dependent query directions. We explicitly rely on this stated finite-program result, not on a claim that the infinite-time or growing-program-length theorem already follows from it.\n\nThese finite limits can be realized on two common population coordinate spaces. Here are the details relevant to the subsequent continuous-time argument. Take all the programs needed for rational meshes, their finite unions, and a countable closure under rational linear combinations, the coordinate operations in the proof, and bounded Lipschitz approximations. Their finite joint laws are consistent, so they can be realized jointly. Take the mean-square closure of their linear spans on each layer. Exact finite linear identities pass to these laws.\n\nA fixed sufficiently large \\(M\\) satisfies\n\\[\n\\mathbb P(\\|W_0^{(2)}\\|_{\\rm op}>M)\n\\le2\\,9^{2n}e^{-nM^2/8}\\longrightarrow0.\n\\tag{M5}\n\\]\nIndeed two \\(1/4\\)-nets of the unit sphere each have at most \\(9^n\\) points, and every fixed bilinear form of the matrix is \\(N(0,1/n)\\). This finite operator inequality and joint second-moment convergence imply\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[(W_0^{(2)}V)^2]\\le M^2\\mathbb E[V^2]\n\\]\nfor every generated \\(V\\). Thus the action is well-defined on equal-in-mean-square variables and extends to the closure. The same reasoning applies to the transpose. The exact finite identity\n\\[\n\\frac{u^\\top W_0^{(2)}v}{n}\n=\\frac{((W_0^{(2)})^\\top u)^\\top v}{n}\n\\]\npasses to the limit and identifies the two actions as adjoints. Bounded coordinate multipliers and their required products belong to these spaces by clipping and mean-square approximation. This constructs the common bounded-operator realization used below; it is not an additional assumption.\n\nDefine population Euler for (M4) in this common realization, starting from the stated initialization. Its rank expansion is\n\\[\nW_k^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}\n-2\\Delta\\sum_{s<k}\\sum_b\nr_{b,s}\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}\\otimes H_{b,s}^{(1)}.\n\\tag{M6}\n\\]\nThus each fixed mesh is a finite Gaussian calculation with deterministic population residuals and contractions. Iterative construction is causal: the current fields determine their expectations and then the next fields. Its realization agrees with the finite Gaussian law, because applying the same rank expansion reduces each action to an initial-matrix call and those deterministic contractions.\n\nThe appendix proves the essential new estimate:\n\\[\n\\sup_{\\Delta:\\,\\Delta\\le T_0}\\ \n\\max_{a,k:\\,k\\Delta\\le T_0}\n\\mathbb E\\exp\\!\\left(\nc\\left|(W_k^{(2)})^*\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\\right|^2\n\\right)\\le C .\n\\tag{M7}\n\\]\nHere \\(T_0,c,C>0\\) do not depend on the mesh. In particular the squared tail beyond \\(R\\) is at most \\(C e^{-c'R^2}\\), after reducing \\(c'\\).\n\nThe appendix is a derivation, not an assumed light-tail condition. It controls the accumulated deterministic response coefficients on both layers. The second-layer derivatives stay bounded; the first-layer derivatives have a Gaussian exponential bound. A short-time induction closes these bounds uniformly in the number of Euler steps.\n\n## Population Euler converges to a unique flow\n\nFor comparing two population states use\n\\[\nd=\\sum_{a=1}^3\n\\bigl(\\mathbb E|Z_a^{(1)}-\\widetilde Z_a^{(1)}|^2\\bigr)^{1/2}\n+\\|W^{(2)}-\\widetilde W^{(2)}\\|_{\\rm op}\n+\\bigl(\\mathbb E|W^{(3)}-\\widetilde W^{(3)}|^2\\bigr)^{1/2}.\n\\tag{M8}\n\\]\nAt finite width replace each mean-square norm by the Euclidean norm divided by \\(\\sqrt n\\).\n\nAll Euler trajectories, and all the actual discrete trajectories with \\(\\eta_n\\le1\\), have bounds independent of width on a fixed bounded time interval, whenever the initial readout supremum and matrix operator norm are bounded. To see this, boundedness of \\(\\phi\\) gives\n\\[\n|r_a|\\le 1+\\frac{\\pi}{2}\\|W^{(3)}\\|_\\infty,\\qquad\n\\|W^{(3)}_{k+1}\\|_\\infty\n\\le\\|W^{(3)}_k\\|_\\infty+\nC\\eta_n(1+\\|W^{(3)}_k\\|_\\infty).\n\\]\nThe geometric sum bounds the readout supremum. The rank-one update then bounds the matrix operator norm, and the first-layer update bounds the root-mean-square velocity and displacement. The same calculation applies to population Euler with \\(\\Delta\\). All initial bounds hold with probability tending to one for the finite initialization.\n\nOn these bounded sets, the forward quantities, residuals, second-layer backward fields, and the maps\n\\[\n(Z_1^{(1)},Z_2^{(1)},Z_3^{(1)},W^{(2)},W^{(3)})\n\\longmapsto (W^{(2)})^*\\delta_a^{(2)}\n\\]\nare Lipschitz in (M8). This follows by adding and subtracting one factor at a time, using boundedness and Lipschitz continuity of \\(\\phi,\\phi'\\), the readout supremum bound, and the operator bound.\n\nOnly the first-layer multiplication needs a separate estimate. Write\n\\[\nP_a=(W^{(2)})^*\\delta_a^{(2)},\\qquad\n"}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': "\\sigma_a^2=\\mathbb E[(J_a^\\perp)^2].\n\\]\nThis is ordinary least-squares projection onto the three old \\(H\\) fields.\n\nGiven the first-layer roots, \\(J_a\\) is a linear function of the Gaussian vector \\(\\Gamma\\), with coefficients\n\\[\nc_b=\\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})G_{ab}y_b\\phi'(Z_{0,b}^{(1)}).\n\\]\nIn particular \\(c_a=y_a\\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})^2\\ne0\\). Subtracting a function of the roots cannot remove this conditional randomness, so\n\\[\n\\sigma_a^2\n\\ge\\mathbb E[\\operatorname{Var}(J_a\\mid Z_{0,1}^{(1)},Z_{0,2}^{(1)},Z_{0,3}^{(1)})]\n=\\mathbb E[c^\\top Vc]>0.\n\\tag{M18a}\n\\]\n\nCondition on the already revealed forward calls\n\\(W_0^{(2)}H_{0,c}^{(1)}=Z_{0,c}^{(2)}\\) and transpose calls\n\\((W_0^{(2)})^*U_b=P_b\\). The new forward call has the population law\n\\[\nW_0^{(2)}J_a\n=\\sum_c\\alpha_{a,c}Z_{0,c}^{(2)}\n+\\sum_b\\beta_{a,b}U_b+\\sigma_a\\gamma_a,\n\\qquad\n\\sum_c V_{bc}\\beta_{a,c}=\\mathbb E[P_bJ_a^\\perp].\n\\tag{M18b}\n\\]\nHere \\(\\gamma_a\\sim N(0,1)\\) is independent of the previous second-layer history. To see the variance, the remaining finite Gaussian matrix is projected orthogonally to the three observed input directions on each side. Applying it to the part of \\(J_a\\) perpendicular to the old \\(H\\) inputs gives variance \\(\\|J_a^\\perp\\|_2^2/n\\). The output projection has fixed rank three, so its removal changes normalized mean square by \\(O(1/n)\\). Joint empirical convergence then gives (M18b). The response coefficients follow from the two recorded matrix identities. Both Gram inverses are legitimate because \\(Q,V\\succ0\\). One such calculation is made for each \\(a\\); independence of the three \\(\\gamma_a\\) from one another is neither claimed nor needed.\n\nThe learned-matrix contribution \\(\\sum_b y_bQ_{ab}U_b\\) in \\(R_a\\) depends only on the previous second-layer coordinates. It cannot cancel the independent new Gaussian term. Thus\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[R_a^2]\\ge\\sigma_a^2>0,\\qquad a=1,2,3.\n\\tag{M18c}\n\\]\n\nSubstitution in the integral equations, beginning with \\(W^{(3)}(0)=0\\) and \\(r_a(0)=-y_a\\), gives\n\\[\nW^{(3)}(t)=2tS+o_{L^2}(t),\\qquad\n\\delta_a^{(2)}(t)=2tU_a+o_{L^2}(t),\n\\]\n\\[\nZ_a^{(1)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(1)}=2t^2T_a+o_{L^2}(t^2),\n\\]\n\\[\nW^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}\n=2t^2\\sum_b y_bU_b\\otimes H_{0,b}^{(1)}+o_{\\rm op}(t^2),\n\\]\n\\[\nZ_a^{(2)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(2)}=2t^2R_a+o_{L^2}(t^2).\n\\tag{M19}\n\\]\nFor example the readout integral divided by \\(t\\) converges to \\(2S\\), then the second-layer backward field divided by \\(t\\) converges to \\(2U_a\\). Integrating the first-layer and matrix velocities therefore gives the factors \\(2t^2\\). Expanding \\(W^{(2)}H_a^{(1)}\\) gives the two terms in \\(R_a\\). Each passage uses bounded \\(\\phi,\\phi'\\), the bounded matrix action, and strong mean-square continuity. Differentiating the activation along these strongly convergent mean-square increments is justified by dominated convergence against their fixed limiting direction; no global differentiability claim for nonlinear maps on the whole mean-square space is used.\n\nThe corresponding hidden velocities are \\(4tT_a+o_{L^2}(t)\\) and \\(4tR_a+o_{L^2}(t)\\). Consequently each input's squared displacement has positive coefficient \\(4\\mathbb E[T_a^2]\\) or \\(4\\mathbb E[R_a^2]\\) times \\(t^4\\), and its integrated squared speed has positive coefficient \\((16/3)\\mathbb E[T_a^2]\\) or \\((16/3)\\mathbb E[R_a^2]\\) times \\(t^3\\).\n\nIt remains to check that singular \\(G\\) does not make a kernel block singular. Let \\(\\lambda>0\\) be the smallest eigenvalue of \\(D\\). For every \\(c\\in\\mathbb R^3\\),\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\n\\sum_{a,b}c_ac_bG_{ab}D_{ab}\n&=\\frac1d\\sum_{j=1}^d\n\\mathbb E\\left[\\left(\\sum_a c_a x_{a,j}B_a\\right)^2\\right]\\\\\n&\\ge\\frac{\\lambda}{d}\\sum_{j=1}^d\\sum_a c_a^2x_{a,j}^2\n=\\lambda\\sum_a c_a^2 .\n\\end{aligned}\n\\tag{M20}\n\\]\nThis uses normalization, not invertibility of \\(G\\). Thus the matrix with entries \\(G_{ab}D_{ab}\\) is positive definite. Likewise the matrix with entries \\(Q_{ab}V_{ab}\\) is positive definite: if \\(\\lambda_V>0\\) is the smallest eigenvalue of \\(V\\), its quadratic form is at least \\(\\lambda_V\\sum_aQ_{aa}c_a^2>0\\) for \\(c\\ne0\\).\n\nPut\n\\[\nA_1=\\sum_{a,b}y_ay_bG_{ab}D_{ab}>0,\\qquad\nA_2=\\sum_{a,b}y_ay_bQ_{ab}V_{ab}>0.\n\\]\nAdjunction and (M17) give\n\\[\n\\sum_a y_a\\mathbb E[U_aR_a]=A_1+A_2.\n\\]\nThe kernel expansions are\n\\[\nK^{(1)}_{ab}(t)=4t^2G_{ab}D_{ab}+o(t^2),\\qquad\nK^{(2)}_{ab}(t)=4t^2Q_{ab}V_{ab}+o(t^2).\n\\]\nExpanding \\(H_a^{(2)}(t)\\) using (M19) shows that the readout block contributes \\(4(A_1+A_2)t^2\\) to its quadratic form in direction \\(y\\). The other two blocks contribute the same amount. Therefore\n\\[\ny^\\top K(t)y\n=y^\\top K^{(3)}(0)y+8(A_1+A_2)t^2+o(t^2).\n\\tag{M21}\n\\]\nThe total kernel is nonconstant. The first two blocks are positive definite at sufficiently small positive times by (M20). The initial readout block is positive definite because the second-layer Gaussian triple has full support, and the three coordinate arctangents have no linear relation. It remains positive definite by continuity.\n\nFinally,\n\\[\nL(0)=3,\\qquad \\dot L(0)=-4y^\\top K^{(3)}(0)y<0.\n\\]\nThe loss strictly decreases on a sufficiently small interval. Every initial hidden marginal is a nondegenerate Gaussian, so it has positive variance and positive best-affine-fit error for \\(\\arctan\\). The variance/covariance formula for that fit and mean-square continuity preserve positivity for small time.\n\nAll strict conclusions hold on some \\((0,T_*]\\) with \\(0<T_*\\le T_0\\). These time constants do not depend on \\(n\\), \\(\\eta_n\\), or \\(\\Delta\\). The existence time \\(T_0\\) is uniform over normalized triples; the activity interval and positive activity coefficients may depend on the fixed triple and labels. No lower bound uniform near parallel configurations is claimed for those coefficients.\n\n## Appendix: the mesh-uniform response calculation\n\nThe following calculation supplies (M7). Its equation numbers are local to the appendix.\n\nLet the input Gram matrix be\n\\[\nG_{ab}=x_a^\\top x_b/d,\\qquad G\\succeq0,\\quad G_{aa}=1,\\quad |G_{ab}|\\le1,\n\\]\nand labels be \\(y_1,y_2,y_3\\in\\{-1,1\\}\\). Use the summed loss \\(\\sum_{a=1}^3r_a^2\\); averaging the loss only rescales time. The first-layer population root \\((Z_{1,0}^{(1)},Z_{2,0}^{(1)},Z_{3,0}^{(1)})\\) is centered Gaussian with covariance \\(G\\). The shared readout starts at \\(W_0^{(3)}=0\\).\n\nFor fixed mesh \\(\\Delta>0\\), population Euler obeys\n\\[\nH_{a,k}^{(1)}=\\phi(Z_{a,k}^{(1)}),\\quad\nZ_{a,k}^{(2)}=W_k^{(2)}H_{a,k}^{(1)},\\quad\nH_{a,k}^{(2)}=\\phi(Z_{a,k}^{(2)}),\\quad\n\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}=W_k^{(3)}\\phi'(Z_{a,k}^{(2)}),\n\\]\n\\[\nr_{a,k}=\\mathbb E[W_k^{(3)}H_{a,k}^{(2)}]-y_a,\n\\]\n\\[\nZ_{a,k+1}^{(1)}=Z_{a,k}^{(1)}-2\\Delta\\sum_{b=1}^3G_{ab}r_{b,k}\\phi'(Z_{b,k}^{(1)})P_{b,k},\n\\qquad P_{b,k}:=(W_k^{(2)})^*\\delta_{b,k}^{(2)},\n\\tag{1}\n\\]\n\\[\nW_{k+1}^{(2)}=W_k^{(2)}-2\\Delta\\sum_{b=1}^3r_{b,k}\\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}\\otimes H_{b,k}^{(1)},\n\\qquad\nW_{k+1}^{(3)}=W_k^{(3)}-2\\Delta\\sum_{b=1}^3r_{b,k}H_{b,k}^{(2)}.\n\\tag{2}\n\\]\nHere \\(\\phi=\\arctan\\). All response derivatives below hold every deterministic coefficient in these finite recursions fixed, including residuals, contractions, and response coefficients. They do not differentiate through expectations or through the covariance law.\n\n## Exact finite-mesh Gaussian response representation\n\nIntroduce two centered jointly Gaussian collections \\(\\xi_{a,k}\\) and \\(\\eta_{a,k}\\), independent of one another. The first-layer root is independent of both. Their covariances are\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[\\xi_{a,k}\\xi_{b,s}]=\\mathbb E[H_{a,k}^{(1)}H_{b,s}^{(1)}],\n\\qquad\n\\mathbb E[\\eta_{a,k}\\eta_{b,s}]=\\mathbb E[\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}].\n\\tag{3}\n\\]\nThese are separate-layer probability spaces; taking them independent on a product space is only a convenient representation, not an assertion of a natural pairing of neurons across layers.\n\nDefine the deterministic coefficients\n\\[\nA_{ak,bs}:=\\mathbb E\\left[\\frac{\\partial\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}}{\\partial\\xi_{b,s}}\\right],\\quad s\\le k,\n\\qquad\nC_{ak,bs}:=\\mathbb E\\left[\\frac{\\partial H_{a,k}^{(1)}}{\\partial\\eta_{b,s}}\\right],\\quad s<k.\n\\tag{4}\n\\]\nThe derivatives are symbolic derivatives of the displayed causal recursions, treating Gaussian slots as separate real arguments even when their realized joint covariance is singular. Then the exact limiting initial-matrix actions are\n\\[\nW_0^{(2)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}=\n\\xi_{a,k}+\\sum_{b=1}^3\\sum_{s<k}C_{ak,bs}\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)},\n\\tag{5}\n\\]\n\\[\n(W_0^{(2)})^*\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}=\n\\eta_{a,k}+\\sum_{b=1}^3\\sum_{s\\le k}A_{ak,bs}H_{b,s}^{(1)}.\n\\tag{6}\n\\]\nExpanding the learned matrix gives the full equations\n\\[\nZ_{a,k}^{(2)}=\n\\xi_{a,k}+\\sum_{b=1}^3\\sum_{s<k}\n\\left(C_{ak,bs}-2\\Delta r_{b,s}\\mathbb E[H_{b,s}^{(1)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}]\\right)\n\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)},\n\\tag{7}\n\\]\n\\[\nP_{a,k}=\n\\eta_{a,k}+\\sum_{b=1}^3\\sum_{s\\le k}\n\\left(A_{ak,bs}-\\mathbf1_{s<k}2\\Delta r_{b,s}\\mathbb E[\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}]\\right)\nH_{b,s}^{(1)}.\n\\tag{8}\n\\]\nTogether with (1), the readout update in (2), and \\(\\delta=W^{(3)}\\phi'(Z^{(2)})\\), these are causal finite recursions. To construct step \\(k\\): first construct \\(H_{a,k}^{(1)}\\) and its derivatives using \\(\\eta\\)-slots through \\(k-1\\); next construct \\(Z_{a,k}^{(2)},\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\\) using \\(\\xi\\)-slots through \\(k\\); finally construct \\(P_{a,k}\\) and update first-layer coordinates and readout. The Gaussian covariance matrices extend consistently because they are Gram matrices of the constructed source variables.\n\nWhy the Gaussian response rule has this form: conditioning on previous forward and transpose products gives the Gaussian matrix regression formula with a projected fresh Gaussian matrix. In the limit, its Gaussian parts in each orientation have covariance equal to the Gram matrix of the sources. Its non-Gaussian part lies in the span of the previous sources in the opposite orientation. Gaussian integration by parts identifies the coefficients of this response as the expectations in (4). The elementary identity used is, for a centered Gaussian vector \\(g\\) with covariance \\(\\Sigma\\),\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[g_i f(g)]=\\sum_j\\Sigma_{ij}\\mathbb E[\\partial_j f(g)].\n\\tag{9}\n\\]\nIt is valid even for singular \\(\\Sigma\\), by writing \\(g=B\\gamma\\) and integrating by parts in independent standard Gaussian coordinates \\(\\gamma\\). If the conditional regression uses a pseudoinverse, the symbolic derivative coefficients and the pseudoinverse coefficients can differ only by a vector in the covariance nullspace; the corresponding combination of source variables has zero squared norm, so the response itself is unchanged. This is the singular-covariance issue that must be retained in a complete proof.\n\nFor a rigorous external formulation of this finite-program identity, see [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685), Box 1, Remarks 2.11–2.12 and Theorem 2.10. The algebraic recursions here are smooth, polynomially bounded functions of finitely many Gaussian slots, and their symbolic derivatives are polynomially bounded for fixed program length. Thus all expectations in (4) exist. The finite-query theorem handles fixed program length; the estimates below are the additional ingredient needed uniformly as length increases. This note does not purport to reprove the entire master theorem in the preceding paragraph.\n\n"}]

Payload:
PASS for A–C, with the usual flow regularity interpretation.

A. Put \(q_a=(y_bQ_{ba})_{b=1}^3\). Applying the operator expansion to \(H^{(1)}_{0,a}\) gives
\[
[W^{(2)}(t)-W^{(2)}_0]H^{(1)}_{0,a}
=2\kappa_2\kappa_3t^2\sum_b y_bQ_{ba}U_b+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\]
Thus
\[
c_a=2\kappa_2\kappa_3\sqrt{q_a^\top Vq_a}>0,
\]
because \(V\succ0\) and \(q_{a,a}=y_aQ_{aa}\ne0\).

B. Write \(F=\lim_{t\downarrow0}\dot Z(t)/t\ne0\) in \(L^2\). Then
\[
\phi'(Z(t))\frac{\dot Z(t)}t\longrightarrow\phi'(Z_0)F
\quad\text{in }L^2.
\]
Split off \(\dot Z(t)/t-F\); the remaining term converges using boundedness of \(\phi'\), convergence in probability of \(Z(t)\), and integrability of \(F^2\). Since \(\phi'(Z_0)>0\) almost surely,
\(c'=\|\phi'(Z_0)F\|_2>0\).
The chain rule holds for the \(C^1(L^2)\) flow; with only absolute continuity, derivative statements are initially almost everywhere in time.

C. When \(\operatorname{Var}(Z)>0\), the error equals
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\phi(Z))
-\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(Z,\phi(Z))^2}{\operatorname{Var}(Z)}.
\]
All terms are continuous under the stated mean-square convergence. Initial Gaussian full support makes the error strictly positive because arctangent is not affine. Taking minima across six marginals gives positive lower bounds on one sufficiently short interval. These bounds need not be uniform over varying initialization constants.

The μP citation also passes: immediately below Tables 8–9, Appendix B’s “Zero Initialization Variance” paragraph explicitly permits zero variance for all its table scaling rules; Appendix D.2 specifically discusses zero output initialization. Cite [Tensor Programs V, PDF pages 23 and 27](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466). This supports the initialization convention, not a joint width–gradient-flow convergence theorem.

## Ordinal 1938 message assistant commentary
The μP comparison is precise: with your explicit \(1/n\) output factor, the SGD learning-rate multipliers \((n,1,n)\) match μP’s width scaling. μP also explicitly permits zero readout initialization. One distinction matters: a nonzero random readout can give initial predictions tending to zero without the readout itself tending to zero—and that changes when hidden-layer motion begins.

## Ordinal 1952 message assistant final_answer
Yes—in the sense that the motion survives the width and learning-rate limits. For every fixed allowed three-input configuration, there is a fixed \(T_*>0\) on which both hidden layers learn, the middle matrix changes its action, the loss decreases, and neither activation becomes effectively linear.

There is one important qualification: **the hidden velocities are not constant in time.** They start at zero because the limiting readout starts at zero, and initially grow proportionally to \(t\). At each fixed sufficiently small \(t>0\), their size is strictly positive and independent of width and learning rate.

Let me make both the scaling and these conclusions precise.

## The scaling family and its physical clock

Keep three fixed inputs \(x_a\in\mathbb R^d\), with
\[
\frac{\|x_a\|_2^2}{d}=1,\qquad
G_{ab}=\frac{x_a^\top x_b}{d},
\]
no two parallel or antiparallel, and labels \(y_a\in\{-1,1\}\).

The stored parameters have dimensions
\[
W^{(1)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times d},\qquad
W^{(2)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times n},\qquad
W^{(3)}\in\mathbb R^n.
\]

Their normalization is part of the model:
\[
z_{a,k}^{(1)}=\frac{W_k^{(1)}x_a}{\sqrt d},
\qquad
z_{a,k}^{(2)}=W_k^{(2)}h_{a,k}^{(1)},
\qquad
h_{a,k}^{(\ell)}=\arctan\!\left(z_{a,k}^{(\ell)}\right),
\]
\[
f_{n,a,k}=\frac{(W_k^{(3)})^\top h_{a,k}^{(2)}}n,
\qquad
r_{n,a,k}=f_{n,a,k}-y_a,
\qquad
L_{n,k}=\sum_{a=1}^3r_{n,a,k}^2.
\]

Here is a sufficient family extending the specific configuration we proved. Initialize all entries independently as centered Gaussians:

| Stored parameter | Initialization variance per entry | Learning rate multiplying its loss gradient |
|---|---:|---:|
| \(W^{(1)}\) | \(\sigma_1^2\) | \(\eta_n n\kappa_1\) |
| \(W^{(2)}\) | \(\sigma_2^2/n\) | \(\eta_n\kappa_2\) |
| \(W^{(3)}\) | \(\sigma_3^2n^{-2\beta}\) | \(\eta_n n\kappa_3\) |

The assumptions are
\[
0<\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\kappa_1,\kappa_2,\kappa_3<\infty,
\qquad
0\le \sigma_3<\infty,
\qquad
\beta>0,
\]
with these constants fixed as width grows. Exact zero readout initialization is also allowed.

The earlier configuration was the special choice with all these scale constants equal to \(1\), \(\beta=1\), and \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\).

Why these learning-rate powers? Recall the residual-free backpropagation fields:
\[
\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}
=W_k^{(3)}\odot\phi'(z_{a,k}^{(2)}),
\qquad
\delta_{a,k}^{(1)}
=\phi'(z_{a,k}^{(1)})
\odot(W_k^{(2)})^\top\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}.
\]
They equal \(n\,\partial f_{n,a,k}/\partial z_{a,k}^{(\ell)}\).

Applying the learning rates in the table gives the exact updates
\[
\begin{aligned}
z_{a,k+1}^{(1)}
&=z_{a,k}^{(1)}
-2\eta_n\kappa_1
\sum_bG_{ab}r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(1)},\\
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
&=W_k^{(2)}
-\frac{2\eta_n\kappa_2}{n}
\sum_b r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}
(h_{b,k}^{(1)})^\top,\\
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
&=W_k^{(3)}
-2\eta_n\kappa_3
\sum_b r_{n,b,k}h_{b,k}^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The explicit \(n\)'s in the input and readout learning rates cancel the \(1/n\)'s in their gradients. The resulting field updates have the common step size \(\eta_n\).

For the middle matrix, its \(1/n\) update factor also does **not** make its action vanish. Holding its current input fixed,
\[
\begin{aligned}
&(W_{k+1}^{(2)}-W_k^{(2)})h_{a,k}^{(1)}
\\
&\qquad=
-2\eta_n\kappa_2
\sum_b r_{n,b,k}\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}
\frac{(h_{b,k}^{(1)})^\top h_{a,k}^{(1)}}n.
\end{aligned}
\]
The last factor is an empirical average with a finite, generally nonzero limit. Thus entrywise-small matrix updates produce width-independent changes in the matrix’s action.

The physical clock is
\[
t=k\eta_n.
\]
For the **local three-input theorem**, the joint limit requires
\[
n\longrightarrow\infty,
\qquad
\eta_n\longrightarrow0.
\]
No additional relation between them is needed in that local argument. For example, \(\eta_n=n^{-p}\) works for every fixed \(p>0\).

In particular, the displayed input-layer learning rate \(n\eta_n\) need not itself tend to zero: its gradient contains the compensating \(1/n\). What matters for the continuous-time approximation is the actual field increment.

The earlier condition \(\eta_n\sqrt n\to0\) remains sufficient, but the newer local multi-input argument does not need it. This is not a claim that we have strengthened the earlier theorem on every finite time horizon.

Within this family, the local convergence proof still closes. The positive scale constants modify its bounds but introduce no growing powers of width. Also,
\[
\frac{\|W_0^{(3)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}\longrightarrow0,
\qquad
\max_j|W_{0,j}^{(3)}|\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability}
\]
for every \(\beta>0\). Consequently, all these readout initializations give the same population initialization \(W_0^{(3)}=0\).

## What is nontrivial in the resulting limit?

Write \(Z_a^{(\ell)}(t)\) for a population preactivation and
\[
H_a^{(\ell)}(t)=\phi(Z_a^{(\ell)}(t)).
\]
Expectations below average over neurons in the corresponding population.

The short-time expansion gives positive constants \(c,C,T_*\) such that
\[
ct
\le
\sqrt{\mathbb E\left[
\left|\frac{dZ_a^{(\ell)}(t)}{dt}\right|^2
\right]}
\le Ct,
\qquad
0<t\le T_*,
\]
for every input \(a\) and both hidden layers \(\ell=1,2\).

This quantity is the RMS velocity across neurons. The same type of bounds holds for the activation velocity \(dH_a^{(\ell)}/dt\), since
\[
\frac{dH_a^{(\ell)}}{dt}
=
\phi'(Z_a^{(\ell)})\frac{dZ_a^{(\ell)}}{dt},
\qquad
\phi'(z)=\frac1{1+z^2}>0.
\]

So the precise picture is:

- At \(t=0\), hidden velocity is zero.
- At each fixed small \(t>0\), hidden velocity is positive and does not disappear with width.
- On any fixed interval \([\tau,T_*]\), with \(\tau>0\), it is bounded below by a positive constant.
- The initial hidden displacement is proportional to \(t^2\), not to a vanishing power of width.

The matrix itself is genuinely learning—not merely receiving a moving input. Holding the **initial** first-layer activation fixed, the expansion gives
\[
\sqrt{\mathbb E\left[
\left|
[W^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}]H_{0,a}^{(1)}
\right|^2
\right]}
=
c_a t^2+o(t^2),
\qquad c_a>0.
\]
Thus its action changes even in this fixed-input test. The prediction kernel also changes; the limit is neither a frozen-feature model nor a constant-kernel model.

The delay in hidden motion has a simple cause. Initially,
\[
W_0^{(3)}=0,
\qquad
\delta_a^{(2)}(0)=\delta_a^{(1)}(0)=0.
\]
But the readout immediately starts moving:
\[
\dot W^{(3)}(0)
=
2\kappa_3\sum_a y_aH_{0,a}^{(2)}.
\]
It becomes proportional to \(t\), so the backpropagated fields become proportional to \(t\), producing hidden displacement proportional to \(t^2\).

The loss does not have this initial delay. Since the population predictions start at zero,
\[
L(0)=3,
\]
and
\[
-\dot L(0)
=
4\kappa_3\,
\mathbb E\left[
\left(\sum_a y_aH_{0,a}^{(2)}\right)^2
\right]
>0.
\]
Strict positivity uses the initial-feature independence argument proved for nonparallel inputs: the displayed label-weighted combination cannot be the zero random variable. By continuity, after possibly reducing \(T_*\),
\[
-\dot L(t)\ge c_L>0,
\qquad 0\le t\le T_*.
\]
This concerns the summed loss; individual examples’ squared residuals need not all decrease.

Finally, neither hidden activation is being linearized by shrinking preactivations. Already at initialization, with \(g\sim N(0,1)\),
\[
\operatorname{Var}(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})=\sigma_1^2>0,
\]
\[
\operatorname{Var}(Z_{0,a}^{(2)})
=
\sigma_2^2\,
\mathbb E[\arctan(\sigma_1g)^2]
>0.
\]
Both quantities are independent of width.

There is a stronger statement than nonvanishing variance. For both layers and every input, on a fixed short interval,
\[
\inf_{c_0,c_1\in\mathbb R}
\mathbb E\left[
\left(
\arctan(Z_a^{(\ell)}(t))
-c_0-c_1Z_a^{(\ell)}(t)
\right)^2
\right]
\ge c_{\mathrm{nonlinear}}>0.
\]
Even the **best affine approximation** retains positive mean-squared error.

At initialization, this is positive because each hidden marginal is a nondegenerate Gaussian and arctan is not affine. Mean-square continuity preserves positivity for short time. Thus the claim rules out the activation becoming asymptotically linear under the actual neuron distribution, not merely at a selected preactivation value.

All these positive constants are independent of width and learning rate. They may depend on the fixed input geometry and hyperparameters. In particular, choosing \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2\) extremely small can make the nonlinear effect numerically small; we are not claiming uniform lower bounds as these constants approach zero.

## How close is this to μP?

Quite close: **the SGD width scaling is μP’s scaling**, in the convention where the readout has an explicit \(1/n\) forward multiplier.

With fixed input dimension, μP uses width-independent input initialization, middle-weight variance proportional to \(1/n\), and the stored-parameter SGD learning-rate powers
\[
(n,\;1,\;n).
\]
These match the table above. Different fixed input-normalization conventions only change constants, not width powers. [Tensor Programs V, Table 8](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466#page=23).

The initialization distinction matters. Positive-variance μP commonly gives the stored \(W_0^{(3)}\) order-one coordinates, while our family makes those coordinates vanish. But zero initialization is explicitly permitted by μP’s scaling rules, and zero readout initialization is discussed separately in the paper. [Tensor Programs V, Appendix B and Appendix D.2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466#page=27).

So an accurate description is:

> μP SGD scaling with a vanishing-readout initialization, together with a proved local joint width–gradient-flow limit.

A standard nonvanishing random readout may also produce initial predictions tending to zero, but its population readout field is not zero. It is therefore a different initial state, generally with different initial hidden velocities. The zero-readout proof cannot simply be declared a proof for that entire family.

Our affirmative result is consequently **a class of configurations, not one isolated numerical choice**. The positive constants \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\kappa_1,\kappa_2,\kappa_3\) can vary. Their values affect the population dynamics; multiplying all three \(\kappa_\ell\) by the same constant merely rescales time. The readout seed parameters \(\sigma_3,\beta>0\) disappear from the limiting initialization.

This is a sufficient class, not a classification of every possible width scaling. And the existence and nontriviality guarantees here remain local in time.

The detailed checks are in the [scaling extension](/tmp/THREE_INPUT_SCALED_LIMIT_EXTENSION_CHECK.md) and [nontriviality calculation](/tmp/three_input_activity_lemma.md).

Deriving Feature Learning Infinite-Width Limit: Intuition and ExamplesWe propose the Tensor Programs technique for deriving the infinite-width limit of any abc-parametrization. ... We consider SGD with a singleton minibatch {(ξ_t, y_t)} at time t = 0, 1, 2, . . ., where ξ_t is the network input and y_t is the label.\n\nBy calculating r for the standard parametrization (SP) (c.f. Table 1), we easily see that it cannot admit feature learning in the sense here without becoming unstable. Intuitively, this is because of an imbalance of gradient and parameter sizes in SP: The last layer weight matrix is too large at init and gets too much gradient, so that the learning rate needs to be small (O(1/n)) in order for SGD not to blow up — but then the features of the network don’t change enough. We can fix this by dividing the logits by √n and using Θ(1) learning rate, so that f(ξ) = n^−1/2 w_{L+1}^t x_L^t(ξ) (where w_{L+1} has 𝒩(0, 1/n) initialization, as usual). This suffices to enable feature learning.\n\nAnother minor problem of SP is that the input layer gets too little gradient compared to other layers. We can fix this by multiplying the first layer preactivation by √n and use fan-out initialization for w^1, i.e. h^1(ξ) = √n w^1 ξ where w^1 has initialization 𝒩(0, 1/n).\n\nWe can summarize these modifications as follows:\n\nDefinition 4.1. The Maximal Update Parametrization (abbreviated MUP, or μP), in the context of an L-hidden-layer MLP (Eq. (1)), is given by\n\nc = 0,\u2003b_l = 1/2 ∀l,\u2003a_l = \n-1/2\u2003l = 1  \n0\u20032 ≤ l ≤ L  \n1/2\u2003l = L + 1.\n\nReaders who find the abc values too abstract are recommended to see Appendices D and E for pedagogical examples working out why SP cannot do feature learning and how μP fixes this problem.\n\nHow is μP Maximal? Recall Δ•_t means •_t − •_0 for any object •. Simple arithmetic shows the NN function changes like Δf_t(ξ) = ΔW_{L+1}^t x_L^t(ξ) + W_0^{L+1} Δx_t^L(ξ) over the course of SGD. μP turns out to be the unique stable abc-parametrization where both contributions to Δf_t(ξ) are Θ(1) and where every layer learns features (in the sense that ΔW_t^l x_t^{l−1} has Θ(1) coordinates for every l ∈ [L + 1]). See Appendix E.3 for a formalization of this statement.\n\nMFP is a Special Case of μP  Different abc values can actually correspond to equivalent parametrizations, intuitively because adjusting a_l, b_l values effectively gives W^l its own learning rate, so that the global learning rate c is algebraically redundant; this is made rigorous in Appendix C.1. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nGitHub - microsoft/mup: maximal update parametrization (µP) · GitHub (https://github.com/microsoft/mup)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn29search1\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 4.5 years ago; Crawled: 3 months ago; For example, the Adam learning rate of hidden weights `p` is calculated as `globalLR / p.infshape.width_mult()`, where `p.infshape.width_mult()` essentially calculates `fan_in / base_fan_in`. ...   * By default, any parameter matrix that has 2 "infinite" dimensions (i.e. dimensions that are different from base dimensions) are considered by `mup` to have shape (fan_out, fan_in), i.e., in the forward pass, this matrix multiplies its input on the right. ... Just like gradient checking is a simple way of verifying the correctness of an autograd implementation, coordinate checking is a simple way to verify you have implemented μP correctly: calculate the average size (which we denote in the y-axis below by `l1`) of the coordinates of each activation vector in, and output of, the model, for a few steps of training and a few different widths. ... (The `l1` calculates `x.abs().mean()` for each activation vector `x` and is just one measure of the "average size" of `x`\'s entries; one can also use analogously defined `l2`, `l4`, etc, though they may exhibit greater fluctuation with random seeds.) ... We now do the same for an MLP in maximal update parametrization (μP) (including using `mup.optim.MuAdam` instead of `torch.optim.Adam`).\n## How `mup` Works Under the Hood\n\nBy invoking `set_base_shapes(model, ...)`, each parameter tensor `p` of `model` gets a `p.infshape` attribute that stores, for each of its dimensions, the corresponding base dimension and whether that dimension should be considered `infinite` (i.e. will be scaled up/down, e.g., `d_model` of a Transformer) or `finite` (i.e. will be fixed, e.g., vocabulary size). This information is used in the initializers and optimizers to automatically scale the parameters or learning rates to be compliant with μP. For example, the Adam learning rate of hidden weights `p` is calculated as `globalLR / p.infshape.width_mult()`, where `p.infshape.width_mult()` essentially calculates `fan_in / base_fan_in`.\n\n## Current Limitations\n\n  * `set_base_shapes(model, ...)` assumes that `model` has just been randomly initialized in the standard way and rescales its parameters using the base shape information so the model is in μP.\n  * If you want data parallelism, please use `torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel` instead of `torch.nn.DataParallel`. This is because the latter removes the attributes the `mup` package adds to each parameter tensor of the model. Also, for performance, `pytorch` recommends the former anyway.\n  * We scale the learning rate according to μP explicitly by creating refined parameter groups from what is passed to the `mup` optimizer and by manipulating the `lr` attribute in those groups. This is compatible with PyTorch\'s learning rate schedulers. However, if you roll your own, make sure the scheduler sets the learning rate relative to what is currently in the refined parameter groups. The following is an example of what not to do and what is OK:\n    \n    optimizer = mup.MuAdam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)\n    for pg in optimizer.param_groups:\n      # what NOT to do: setting learning rate absolutely\n      # pg[\'lr\'] = 1e-3 * 2\n      # what is an OK alternative: setting it relatively\n      pg[\'lr\'] *= 2\n\n  * By default, any parameter matrix that has 2 "infinite" dimensions (i.e. dimensions that are different from base dimensions) are considered by `mup` to have shape (fan_out, fan_in), i.e., in the forward pass, this matrix multiplies its input on the right. If instead you see the curves converge to the right, then most likely your implementation is correct. However, there are two typical exceptions to this; the following can shrink to 0 at initialization in μP (at a 1/sqrt(width) rate):\n\n  * the network output\n  * the attention logits in a Transformer\n\nThese are transient, and after a few steps their curves should be roughly flat. Nevertheless, to remove the discrepancy at init, we recommend\n\n  * initializing the output layer (should be a `MuReadout` instance) weights to be 0 via the `readout_zero_init=True` option and\n  * initializing the query matrix in a Transformer to 0 (this has to be done manually). If symmetry-breaking is desired in the attention logits at init, initialize the (relative) position biases with nonzero variance.\n\n#### Tips for Coord Check\n\n  * Use a large learning rate (larger than you\'d use for actual training). This would emphasize any potential exploding coordinates issue, which could be hidden by the initialization if the learning rate is too small.\n  * If you reuse a module multiple times in the forward pass, then `mup.get_coord_data` will only record the statistics from the last usage. In this case, for testing purposes, one can wrap different usages with `nn.Identity` modules of different names to distinguish them.\n\n### Wider is Always Better\n\nAnother sign that μP has not been implemented correctly is if going wider does worse (on training loss) after some width, at some point during training. The figure above illustrates this in a collection of training curves: (left) the correct implementation should always see performance improve with width, at any point in training; (middle) if you used standard parametrization (SP), sometimes you may see performance improve with width up to some point and then suddenly it becomes worse with wider models; (right) or you may immediately see worsening performance even for narrow models.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nFeature Learning in Infinite-Width Neural Networks (https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.14522)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn29academia13\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 5.8 years ago; We find that they outperform both NTK baselines and finite-width networks, with the latter approaching the infinite-width feature learning performance as width increases. ... We focus on the extensive-width regime, where the teacher and student network widths scale proportionally with the input dimension, and the sample size grows quadratically. This scaling aims to describe overparameterized neural networks in which feature learning still plays a central role. Our theory is based on the dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT) and is applicable to a wide range of models encompassing generalized linear models and two-layer neural networks.\nTitle: High-Dimensional Limit of Stochastic Gradient Flow via Dynamical Mean-Field Theory\nAuthors: Sota Nishiyama, Masaaki Imaizumi\nDate: Fri Feb  6 02:37:10 2026\n\nModern machine learning models are typically trained via multi-pass stochastic gradient descent (SGD) with small batch sizes, and understanding their dynamics in high dimensions is of great interest. However, an analytical framework for describing the high-dimensional asymptotic behavior of multi-pass SGD with small batch sizes for nonlinear models is currently missing. In this study, we address this gap by analyzing the high-dimensional dynamics of a stochastic differential equation called a \\emph{stochastic gradient flow} (SGF), which approximates multi-pass SGD in this regime. In the limit where the number of data samples $n$ and the dimension $d$ grow proportionally, we derive a closed system of low-dimensional and continuous-time equations and prove that it characterizes the asymptotic distribution of the SGF parameters. Our theory is based on the dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT) and is applicable to a wide range of models encompassing generalized linear models and two-layer neural networks. We further show that the resulting DMFT equations recover several existing high-dimensional descriptions of SGD dynamics as special cases, thereby providing a unifying perspective on prior frameworks such as online SGD and high-dimensional linear regression. Our proof builds on the existing DMFT technique for gradient flow and extends it to handle the stochasticity in SGF using tools from stochastic calculus.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n[2602.06320] High-Dimensional Limit of Stochastic Gradient Flow via Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2602.06320)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn32search1\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 7 months ago; Crawled: 2 days ago; View a PDF of the paper titled High-Dimensional Limit of Stochastic Gradient Flow via Dynamical Mean-Field Theory, by Sota Nishiyama and Masaaki Imaizumi ... We are interested in going beyond the infinite-width limit to study more realistic finite-width networks.\nG_{\\mu\\nu}^\\ell(t,s) \\equiv \\frac{1}{N} g_\\mu^\\ell(t) \\cdot g_\\nu^\\ell(s),\n\\tag{2}\n\\]\n\nwhere \\(g_\\mu^\\ell(t) = \\gamma N \\frac{\\partial f_\\mu(t)}{\\partial h_\\mu^\\ell}\\) are the back-propagated gradient signals. ## Ordinal 1991 message assistant commentary
I’m using the teaching skill here to make the logical distinction explicit. So far, the answer is no: our result does not identify a scaling different from μP. The possible addition is a convergence theorem under that scaling—not the discovery that infinite-width networks can learn features, which μP already established.

\nL130: Share on\nL131: ### Abstract\nL132: This paper studies the infinite-width limit of deep linear neural networks (NNs) initialized with random parameters. We obtain that, when the number of parameters diverges, the training dynamics converge (in a precise sense) to the dynamics obtained from a gradient descent on an infinitely wide deterministic linear NN.\nL133: Moreover, even if the weights remain random, we get their precise law along the training dynamics, and prove a quantitative convergence result of the linear predictor in terms of the number of parameters. We finally study the continuous-time limit obtained for infinitely wide linear NNs and show that the linear predictors of the NN converge at an exponential rate to the minimal $$\\ell _2$$-norm minimizer of the\xa0risk.\nL134: ##\nL135: \nL136: REFERENCES\nL137: \nL138:   * 1 D. Stat. Mech. (2023) 114009\nL25@P1: PAPER: ML 2023\nL26@P1: Self-consistent dynamical field theory\nL27@P1: of kernel evolution in wide neural\nL28@P1: networks∗\nL29@P1: Blake Bordelon and Cengiz Pehlevan∗∗\nL30@P1: John Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences, Center for Brain\nL31@P1: Science, Kempner Institute for the Study of Natural & Artificial Intelligence,\nL32@P1: Harvard University, Cambridge, MA 02138, United States of America\nL33@P1: E-mail: cpehlevan@g.harvard.edu\nL34@P1: Received 15 June 2023\nL35@P1: Accepted for publication 13 September 2023\nL36@P1: Published 15 November 2023\nL37@P1: Online at stacks.iop.org/JSTAT/2023/114009\nL38@P1: https://doi.org/10.1088/1742-5468/ad01b0\nL39@P1: Abstract. We analyze feature learning in infinite-width neural networks\nL40@P1: trained with gradient flow through a self-consistent dynamical field theory. We\nL41@P1: construct a collection of deterministic dynamical order parameters which are\nL42@P1: inner-product kernels for hidden unit activations and gradients in each layer at\nL43@P1: pairs of time points, providing a reduced description of network activity through\nL44@P1: training. These kernel order parameters collectively define the hidden layer activ\x02ation distribution, the evolution of the neural tangent kernel (NTK), and con\x02sequently, output predictions. We show that the field theory derivation recov\x02ers the recursive stochastic process of infinite-width feature learning networks\nL45@P1: obtained by Yang and Hu with tensor programs. For deep linear networks, these\nL46@P1: kernels satisfy a set of algebraic matrix equations. For nonlinear networks, we\nL47@P1: provide an alternating sampling procedure to self-consistently solve for the ker\x02nel order parameters. We provide comparisons of the self-consistent solution to\nL48@P1: various approximation schemes including the static NTK approximation, gradi\x02ent independence assumption, and leading order perturbation theory, showing\nL49@P1: that each of these approximations can break down in regimes where general\nL50@P1: self-consistent solutions still provide an accurate description. Lastly, we provide\nL51@P1: ∗This article is an updated version of: Bordelon B and Pehlevan C 2022 Self-consistent dynamical field theory of\nL52@P1: kernel evolution in wide neural networks Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems vol 35, ed S Koyejo, S\nL53@P1: Mohamed, A Agarwal, D Belgrave, K Cho and A Oh (Curran Associates, Inc.) pp 32240–56.\nL54@P1: ∗∗Author to whom any correspondence should be addressed.\nL55@P1-2: © 2023 IOP Publishing Ltd and SISSA Medialab srl 1742-5468/23/114009+70$33.00Self-consistent dynamical field theory of kernel evolution in wide neural networks\nL56@P2: J. Stat. Mech. (2023) 114009\nL57@P2: experiments in more realistic settings which demonstrate that the loss and kernel\nL58@P2: dynamics of convolutional neural networks at fixed feature learning strength are\nL59@P2: preserved across different widths on a image classification task.\nL60@P2: Keywords: deep learning, machine learning, learning theory\nL61@P2: Contents\nL62@P2: 1. A nontrivial stable abc-parametrization ad\x02mits feature learning iff it evolves the feature kernel iff\nL311@P2: r = 0.\nL312@P2: Kernel Regime While feature learning here is defined by\nL313@P2: looking at the embedding of an input ξ, we can also look\nL314@P2: at the dynamics of the function represented by the neural\nL315@P2: network.\nL316@P2: Definition 3.6 (c.f. Definition N.11). We say an abc\x027\nL317@P2: For the sake of streamlining the main text presentation, we\nL318@P2: defined feature learning and feature kernel evolution slightly dif\x02ferently than in Definition N.9, but ultimately they are equivalent\nL319@P2-3: as a result of our theorems.Feature Learning in Infinite-Width Neural Networks\nL320@P3: Kernel\nL321@P3: Regime\nL322@P3: Neural\nL323@P3: Tangent\nL324@P3: Standard\nL325@P3: 𝐿𝑅 = Θ(1/𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑡ℎ)\nL326@P3: Maximal\nL327@P3: Update\nL328@P3: (ours) Unstable\nL329@P3: or\nL330@P3: Trivial\nL331@P3: Space of abc-Parametrizations\nL332@P3: Mean Field\nL333@P3: when depth=1\nL334@P3: Standard\nL335@P3: 𝐿𝑅 = Θ(1)\nL336@P3: Figure 2. A Caricature of abc-Parametrizations. The nontriv\x02ial stable parametrizations form a high dimensional polyhedron.\nL337@P3: Those on a part of its boundary admit feature learning, while all\nL338@P3: others are in kernel regime. µP is a vertex in the former, while NTP,\nL339@P3: latter. See Fig. 7 for a more geometrically accurate depiction.\nL340@P3: parametrization is in kernel regime if there exists a positive\nL341@P3: semidefinite kernel K such that, for any training routine,\nL342@P3: time t ≥ 0, and input ξ, in the n → ∞ limit,\nL343@P3: ft+1(ξ) = ft(ξ)−ηK(ξ, ξt)L\nL344@P3: 0\nL345@P3: (ft(ξt), yt), ∀t ≥ 0. (5)\nL346@P3: In other words, SGD reduces to kernel gradient descent in\nL347@P3: the large n limit.\nL348@P3: Theorem 3.7. A nontrivial stable abc-parametrization is in\nL349@P3: kernel regime iff r > 0.\nL350@P3: NTP is a typical example of this, where r = 1/2 and K is\nL351@P3: given by the NTK.\nL352@P3: Dynamical Dichotomy Since a stable abc\x02parametrization has either r = 0 or r > 0 by Eq. (3):\nL353@P3: Corollary 3.8. A nontrivial stable abc-parametrization ei\x02ther admits feature learning or is in kernel regime, but not\nL354@P3: both.\nL355@P3: We note that we are under Assumption I.1. For example, if\nL356@P3: φ is linear, then this dichotomy doesn’t hold, as a 1-hidden\x02layer linear network where only the first layer is trained\nL357@P3: would both admit feature learning and is in kernel regime.\nL358@P3: An interesting consequence of Dynamical Dichotomy is\nL359@P3: Corollary 3.9. Any nontrivial stable feature learning abc\x02parametrization must have limn→∞ f0(ξ) = 0 for all ξ,\nL360@P3: where the limit is almost sure.\nL361@P3: Theorems 3.5 and 3.7 and Corollary 3.9 are consequences\nL362@P3: of the more general classification theorem Theorem N.12,\nL363@P3: which in addition shows: 1) feature learning in layer l would\nL364@P3: imply the same for layers l, . . . , L; 2) in any feature learning\nL365@P3: parametrization, ft in the large n limit becomes determinis\x02tic, and thus is incompatible with any Bayesian perspective\nL366@P3: (in contrast to the NNGP limit).\nL367@P3: Dynamical Dichotomy in the shallow perceptron case is\nL368@P3: illustrated by the NTK and MF limits, as presented in\nL369@P3: Appendix B, which shows the NTK limit exemplifies\nL370@P3: Theorem 3.7 while the MF limit exemplifies Theorem 3.5.\nL371@P3: We present a simplified picture of abc-parametrizations in\nL372@P3: Fig. 2, but see Fig. 7 for a more geometrically accurate\nL373@P3: depiction.\nL374@P3: Remark 3.10 (Function Space Picture). A kernel regime\nL375@P3: limit resides solely in the function space picture, i.e. the evo\x02lution of f at any time being solely determined by the func\x02tion values {lim ft(ζ)}ζ themselves (as opposed to the in\x02ternal activations of f as well) along with η, L, and (ξt, yt).\nL376@P3: Intuitively, this cannot be true for the feature learning limit,\nL377@P3: and therefore, at least informally, Dynamical Dichotomy is\nL378@P3: also a dichotomy over the sufficiency of the function space\nL379@P3: picture for determining the training evolution: We can con\x02struct two settings where {lim ft(ζ)}ζ , η, L, and (ξt, yt)\nL380@P3: are the same but ft+1 are different. 1) The first setting is at\nL381@P3: t = 0, where lim ft(ζ) = 0 for all input ζ by Corollary 3.9.\nL382@P3: Here a typical SGD will change f. 2) In the second setting,\nL383@P3: suppose φ is relu. Design a sequence of inputs such that\nL384@P3: training the MLP on them with very large learning rate will\nL385@P3: make all relu neurons saturated in the 0 region. Then f is\nL386@P3: everywhere 0, and an SGD step will not change that.\nL387@P3: Remark 3.11 (Not All Dynamics are Infinite-Width Limits).\nL388@P3: Accordingly, a nonlinear function space dynamics cannot\nL389@P3: be a valid infinite-width limit of some abc-parametrization.\nL390@P3: By nonlinear, we mean ft+1(ξ) − ft(ξ) is nonlinear in\nL391@P3: L\nL392@P3: 0\nL393@P3: (ft(ξt), yt). For example, any natural higher-order gener\x02alization of Eq. For exam\x02ple, consider linear transfer learning, the popular style of\nL398@P3: transfer learning where one discards the pretrained linear\nL399@P3: classifier layer and train a new one on top of the features,\nL400@P3: which are fixed. Indeed, this is a linear problem and thus\nL401@P3: only depends on the kernel of the features. If this kernel\nL402@P3: is the same as the kernel at initialization, as is the case for\nL403@P3: 8\nL404@P3: It may seem that Neural Tangent Hierarchy (Huang & Yau,\nL405@P3: 2019), which allow some kind of higher order dynamics in the\nL406@P3: function space, violates our observation. But their infinite-width\nL407@P3: limit is identical to NTK in the constant time t = O(1) regime,\nL408@P3: which is what Remark 3.11 (and this paper) concerns. Moreover,\nL409@P3: here we are talking about functional dynamics that doesn’t depend\nL410@P3: on n (because we are already at the n → ∞ limit) whereas their\nL411@P3-4: functional dynamics does.Feature Learning in Infinite-Width Neural Networks\nL412@P4: kernel regime limits (by Corollary 3.8 and Theorem 3.5),\nL413@P4: then the pretraining phase has had no effect on the outcome\nL414@P4: of this “transfer” learning.\nL415@P4: In fact, a more sophisticated reasoning shows pretraining\nL416@P4: in the NTK limit is no better than random initialization\nL417@P4: for transfer learning even if finetuning is performed to the\nL418@P4: whole network, not just the classifier layer. In a gist, such an infinite-width\nL759@P6: µP linear network with input dimension d and output dimen\x02sion do is equivalent to a width-(d+do) linear network with\nL760@P6: the same input/output dimensions but a “diagonal”, instead\nL761@P6: of random, initialization. Our Word2Vec and MAML exper\x02iments will crucially rely on this simplifying observation.\nL762@P6: We remark that, in contrast to our approach, such an obser\x02vation would be obscured by the PDE perspective of prior\nL763@P6: works (Chizat & Bach, 2018; Mei et al., 2018; Rotskoff &\nL764@P6: Vanden-Eijnden, 2018; Sirignano & Spiliopoulos, 2018).\nL765@P6: 5.2. Deep MLP: A Summary\nL766@P6: For more than 1 hidden layer, the mathematics of the µP\nL767@P6: limit becomes significantly more complicated. For the lack\nL768@P6: of space and because our main experiments only use the\nL769@P6: 1-hidden-layer limit, we will just summarize the key points\nL770@P6: here for the deep case, but see Appendix F for pedagogical\nL771@P6: examples that are worked out completely.\nL772@P6: Compared to the shallow case, a deep MLP involves n × n\nL773@P6: random Gaussian matrices corresponding to the initializa\x02tion of middle layer weights. Such a matrix W has a unique\nL774@P6: set of behaviors not seen in the weights of the shallow MLP,\nL775@P6: where only one dimension tends to infinity. 1) (Gaussian\nL776@P6: behavior) If x is roughly independent from W, then W x\nL777@P6: will be a Gaussian vector with roughly iid coordinates. This\nL778@P6: intuition should be familiar to readers having seen NTK or\nL779@P6: NNGP calculations. 2) (Correlation with W>) In a typical\nL780@P6: NTK calculation, W> can be safely assumed to be inde\x02pendent from W (i.e. Gradient Independence Assumption\nL781@P6: (Yang, 2020a)). This turns out to be wrong if we unroll\nL782@P6: SGD for more than 1 step, so one must take care to keep\nL783@P6: track of the contributions from W and W> separately so as\nL784@P6: to calculate their interactions that arise over the course of\nL785@P6: SGD.\nL786@P6: The Tensor Programs framework neatly and rigorously pack\x02ages such complex calculations into a mechanical algorithm\nL787@P6: generalizing the calculus of Z random variables in Sec\x02tion 5.1; see Appendix F. This framework can calculate the\nL788@P6: limit of any abc-parametrization; see Appendix N.\nL789@P6: 6. Experiments\nL790@P6: We present our main experiments (Omniglot and Word2Vec)\nL791@P6: in the main text, while we also empirically verified the\nL792@P6: Figure 3. Our Omniglot Results\nL793@P6: validity of our infinite-width theory in various toy settings\nL794@P6: in Appendix J.1.\nL795@P6: 6.1. Few-Shot Learning on Omniglot via First Order\nL796@P6: MAML\nL797@P6: In few-shot learning, the model is given only a small number\nL798@P6: of labeled examples before asking to make predictions on\nL799@P6: unseen data. Therefore, this tests whether a model contains a\nL800@P6: good prior that can adapt quickly to the small amount of data\nL801@P6: at hand. We compare finite- and infinite-width models on\nL802@P6: Omniglot (Lake et al., 2015), a standard few-shot learning\nL803@P6: benchmark, via First Order MAML (Finn et al., 2017), a\nL804@P6: standard few-shot learning algorithm using metalearning.\nL805@P6: We will be concerned with the 1-shot, 5-way classification\nL806@P6: task (i.e. there are 5 labels, and the training set consists of\nL807@P6: 1 example for each label). The result is compatible with μP. It adds a local joint-limit argument for this model; it establishes neither a new necessary scaling nor a contradiction of μP’s uniqueness theorem.

The precise references and distinctions are:

- **Fixed steps:** TPIV §2 uses learning rate \(\eta n^{-c}\) with width-independent \(\eta\), and §3 explicitly describes a discrete-time limit. [TPIV main paper](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c.pdf).
- **Program length:** Supplement Theorem G.4 fixes a Tensor Program. §N.5 applies it to training-indexed observables. Definition N.4 permits bounds depending on training time and the training routine. These statements do not provide uniform estimates for an index growing with width. [TPIV supplement, Theorem G.4 and §§N.1, N.5](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf).
- **Exact uniqueness:** Definition E.3 defines maximal readout initialization through an order-one contribution of the initial readout to feature changes. Proposition E.4 characterizes it; Theorem E.5 requires every layer to be maximally updated **and** the readout maximally initialized. [TPIV supplement §E.3](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf#page=7).
- **Weaker requirements:** Theorem 3.3 characterizes nontriviality; Theorems 3.5 and 3.7 distinguish \(r=0\) feature learning from \(r>0\) kernel behavior. Corollary 3.8 states the dichotomy. Nontriviality or feature learning alone does not uniquely select μP; the paper depicts a larger feature-learning part of the admissible parameter space. [TPIV §3](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c.pdf#page=3).
- **Activation hypotheses:** Assumption I.1 restricts the classification and §E.3 results to tanh or sufficiently sharp smooth ReLU. Assumption N.21, used for limit construction, is weaker and covers arctangent. [TPIV supplement §§I.1, N.3](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf).
- **Small readouts were anticipated:** §N.3 explicitly discusses smaller and zero initial readouts and the resulting first-step delay. TPV Appendix B expressly permits zero initialization in its scaling tables, and Appendix D.2 recommends zero output initialization. [TPIV supplement §N.3](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf#page=27), [TPV Appendices B and D.2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466).

For our model, the initial-readout contribution
\[
\frac{(W^{(3)}_0)^\top[h^{(2)}(t)-h^{(2)}(0)]}{n}
\]
vanishes in the limit. Hence the original maximal-initialization hypothesis is absent. Modern usage allowing zero-readout μP is consistent with this; it does not make the original uniqueness theorem’s hypotheses identical.

The substantive extra obligation is that
\[
k_n(t)\approx t/\eta_n\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Convergence for every fixed integer \(k\) does not imply convergence along these growing indices. The mesh-uniform tail estimate and stability comparison address that issue, yielding the stated uniform-in-time local flow and observable convergence. Conversely, at fixed \(k\), \(\eta_n\to0\) means elapsed physical time tends to zero; vanishing movement there does not indicate an NTK regime at positive physical time.

The three-input theorem also proves specific quantitative activity and nondegeneracy statements for its fixed loss, activation, initialization and geometry. Those are stronger assertions about this particular model than merely admitting feature learning for some training routine. They are not the same universal hypotheses used in an abc-parametrization classification.

Safe conclusion: “We rigorously realize a local gradient-flow population limit within μP’s update-scaling framework, including a joint width/step-size limit. We have not proved that these requirements force a different scaling, or established novelty relative to the entire continuous-time feature-learning literature.”
