Payload:
Confirmed. The comparison avoids overclaims with these qualifications:

- Say “μP-compatible learning-rate width powers **in our rescaled parameterization**.” The powers alone do not specify our special initialization, especially the zero limiting readout.
- For \(\eta_n=n^{-p}\), every fixed \(p>0\) is covered. The resulting rates \((n^{1-p},n^{-p},n^{1-p})\) preserve the relative layer scaling \(n:1:n\); they refine the common physical clock. Individual effective learning multipliers need not vanish.
- \(p=0\) is not covered by the gradient-flow limit. More generally, the theorem permits any \(\eta_n\to0\), not only powers.
- Use \(k/n\) explicitly as a **schematic counterexample** to exchanging pointwise-in-\(k\) convergence with a growing-\(k\) limit—not as our actual error estimate.
- Our added result is the local autonomous continuous-time limit, with uniqueness, joint GD convergence and verified strict activity under the specified assumptions. It is neither a necessary/unique scaling classification nor a claim to originate feature learning.

The fixed-three-input, arctan, vanishing-readout, positive finite layer-multiplier and local-time restrictions should remain visible.

## Ordinal 2045 message assistant final_answer
**No—not in the sense of discovering a different scaling forced by the joint GF–width limit.** The result uses μP-compatible scaling and proves a continuous-time convergence statement within that regime. Those are different contributions, and my previous answer should not be read as claiming a new scaling law.

μP already established that infinite-width networks can have moving hidden features and non-kernel training dynamics. It was introduced precisely to capture feature learning outside the frozen-NTK regime—not as an approximation that assumes NTK-style frozen features. [Tensor Programs IV](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c.pdf).

To see why taking the GF limit does not change our scaling, retain your parameter convention
\[
z^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h^{(1)},
\qquad
f_n=\frac{(W^{(3)})^\top h^{(2)}}n.
\]
Our layerwise learning rates are
\[
\eta_n\bigl(n\kappa_1,\ \kappa_2,\ n\kappa_3\bigr).
\]
The width factors \((n,1,n)\) match μP’s SGD scaling in this convention. [Tensor Programs V, Table 8](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466#page=23).

Choosing, for example, \(\eta_n=n^{-p}\) makes the displayed rates
\[
\bigl(\kappa_1n^{1-p},\ \kappa_2n^{-p},\ \kappa_3n^{1-p}\bigr).
\]
These look different, but every layer has acquired the **same additional factor** \(n^{-p}\). Meanwhile, we measure time by
\[
t=k\eta_n.
\]
We are taking smaller steps and proportionally more of them per unit of physical time. We have not changed the relative layer scaling.

The substantive additional question is whether this increasingly fine discretization remains valid as width also grows.

The original tensor-program limit controls training after any fixed finite number of steps. Its bounds can depend on that number of steps and the learning rate. That does not by itself control
\[
k\approx \frac{T}{\eta_n}\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
[Tensor Programs IV, §2 and supplement §§G, N](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf).

Here is the logical gap in elementary form: an error of \(k/n\) vanishes for every fixed \(k\), but equals \(1\) at \(k=n\). This is only an illustration—not our actual error bound. It shows why fixed-step convergence cannot simply be substituted into a growing-step argument.

Our additional estimates address that gap. For the restricted model we studied, they establish a unique autonomous population flow and, for example,
\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T_0}
\left|
L_{n,\lfloor t/\eta_n\rfloor}-L(t)
\right|
\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability},
\]
with corresponding hidden-field and matrix-action convergence. The interval \(T_0>0\) does not shrink with width or step size. That is additional theorem content beyond merely identifying the fixed-step limiting recursion. It is not a different feature-learning regime.

There is also a distinction in “the same axioms.” Our proof establishes:

> This specified scaling and initialization are sufficient for the desired local limit.

It does **not** establish:

> Every scaling with the desired properties must have these exponents.

Moreover, the original μP uniqueness theorem imposes an additional maximal-readout-initialization condition: the initial readout must itself detect order-one feature changes. Our zero limiting readout does not satisfy that condition. Smaller and zero readouts are explicitly discussed in the tensor-program work, so our choice is neither a contradiction nor a newly discovered exception. [Tensor Programs IV, supplement §E.3 and §N.3](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf).

In particular, **we have not shown that taking the GF limit requires the readout initialization to vanish.** That is a sufficient initialization for our argument, not a necessity theorem.

The specific conclusions about motion in both hidden layers for every allowed three-input configuration and label pattern also add model-specific information. But “additional information relative to a general μP statement” is not automatically “new to the research literature.” Continuous-time infinite-width feature-learning descriptions already exist, including the work of [Bordelon and Pehlevan](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10541946). Establishing novelty of our exact convergence and well-posedness theorem would require comparing its precise assumptions and conclusions with that broader literature.

So the defensible interpretation is: **a local joint GF–width convergence result within a μP-compatible regime—not evidence that the joint limit forces a scaling beyond μP.**
